Nathan McKnight: a photographer, artist, and linguophile/xenophile/technophile from a large-ish city, somewhere, somewhen. That's all he really wants to say at the moment... about himself, anyway.

TITLE: Kashmir Sky (unfinished short)

(c) 2003 by Nathan McKnight

Kashmir Sky
Intro by ChatGPT

Against the turbulent skies of Kashmir, one young man finds himself pulled between duty, family, and a dream just out of reach. Through masterful detail and introspection, McKnight’s unfinished piece captures the spirit of those who strive for something beyond the everyday.

Note... Undecided on character name

The tickle of wind made Teerth(Yogin?) aware, for the first time in his life, of his nose hairs. Strapped to his belt, the radio spurted staticky voices. He had noticed his father’s nose hairs, stiff as a brush, during his Mekhal eight years ago. Jets quaked past, so fast he couldn’t even tell whether they sported the Pak green and white, or the Indian green, white, and saffron. The Mekhal was supposed to be a washing away of boyhood; instead it was a washing away of family. Teerth pushed his feet down...left...right...left...right...and with each pedal of the rust red gear, hairy ropes lurched...forward...backward...forward...backward...pulling the bicycle’s makeshift palm wings ...up...down...up...down...through the blazing air of the Kashmir sky. 


Kashmir Sky, Part 1. First Flight/ Memory Palace

“We will be forced to take the nuclear option,” said the defense minister.




“It’s just a shitstorm of bigotry,” said Yogin.
“There’s more to it than that.”
	Father in an oversized brown business suit and Yogin in an oversized hoodie and grossly oversized, patched corduroys sat in the airport amongst a patchwork of Indians and Americans. Sikhs in turbans, Muslims in robes and burkhas, Hindus with several different bindhis showing their particular affiliation, all crowded together waiting on flight 1137 to the subcontinent. 
	Yogin had been in the States for ten years—more than half his life. In this crowd, he felt conspicuous for not being conspicuous. He sunk down into his seat, head almost as low as his knees, and he pulled down his cap, sinking his eyes into the Gameboy’s glowing screen.
 
Bathed in watery green light.

“Good, then you’ll be out of here in no time. We’re just going to hold you for questioning for a little while and if everything’s alright, then you’ll be free to go. Nine times out of ten that’s the way these things go down, so there’s probably nothing to worry about.”

“How long?”

“The questioning will only take a few minutes.”

“But we’re running a little late already and our tickets are nonrefundable.”

“That don’t matter. We’ve got a backlog, so you’ll probably spend an hour or two in the comfort center. Hopefully we’ll get you out sooner. Everybody likes these things to go as quick as possible.”

“But I’ll miss my flight. I can’t miss my flight. I have to meet my daughter.”

“Buddy, you’re on our no-fly list. You’re not getting on a plane for a while. Not in this country at least.”

“How long is a while?”

“I have no idea. I heard the legislation is up for review next summer.”

“But…”

“Don’t worry, you’ll have some time to wait around and pray to Mecca or whatever.” 

“He’s not even a Muslim, leave him the fuck alone.”

“What about my son?”

“He’s clear. He’s free to go now. Actually, we need to get him out about now. You’ve got fifteen minutes. Take care of whatever you need to take care of.” 


“Why do both countries care so much about this little piece of land, asks the American reporter to the minister of defense, who replies “Have you been to Kashmir? It is very beautiful.”



The guy next to him was in a pilot’s uniform, all but his head, on which he wore a sikh’s turban.

“So, how does something this big get off the ground?” asked Keerth

“It’s pretty simple, really. You just have to want it bad enough.” 

“Yeah, but how does it work? I never paid much attention in science,” said Keerth. 

The pilot said, “It’s not really science, it’s magic. Whatever you want to do, it’s all the same—you just have to want it bad enough, and the magic will happen. Maybe the magic will happen through physics, but it doesn’t matter.” 



LANDING

“What kind of question is that?”

“I’m an American, my English is impeachable.”“The word is impeccable.”

“Yes.”
“No, he is local.”
“Does he speak English?”
“No.”
“Yes, I do.”
“He says he speaks English.”
Matter of factly: “He does not.”
She seemed to accept this.
“Ma’am, I’m a freaking American. That means I speak freaking English, and I can do this job better than anybody in this whole damned country.”
“You are hired. While working, you will remain silent.”

“Does he speak English?”
“No.”

“Do not attempt to speak to him. If he speaks, do not listen, but warn him or come to me.”


Kashmir Sky, Part 2. THE GIRLFRIEND DANCE

“Yogin has a girlfriend,” she said it as though she were talking to a pet, like a small bird, or perhaps a monkey.
“I never said such a thing to you,” said Yogin, regretting having taken his sister with him when he picked up his paycheck.
“I can see it in your face. You have a big, big smile on your face. You like Mrs. Kao’s daughter.” 
“Snigdha, I have many other things on my face.”
“Not hardly!” Her glee was uncontained.  
“I do,” Yogin protested.
“Like what?”
“I also have eyes and ears and a nose. Why don’t you tell me about those?”
“But your smile is too big. It takes up your whole face. Yogin has a girlfriend, Yogin has a girlfriend. Yogin has a girlfriend and a smile!”
“And other things,” he insisted. 
She turned to sing-song chant, “Yogin has eyes and a nose, and a smile, and a chin, and a smile, and ears, and a neck and a smile…” her words went up and down like a mantra of discovery. 
“You should be helping mother with the dishes; perhaps you noticed the concern in my eyes for our dear widowed mother?”
“…and a shoulder and a shoulder and a chest and a belly and a smile and a bottom…” at this, she laughed so loud mother might have heard.
He laughed now, in spite of himself. “Or perhaps you could comment on my nose—how it’s wrinkled at your odor. When was the last time you bathed?” Yogin turned older brother on her, and she made a scowl. Then, he mouthed, didn’t even whisper, “I have a girlfriend,” raised his eyebrows, and smiled. 
“…and a leg and a leg and a knee and a knee and a smile…” she did a little dance to her song, imitating the complex gestures of temple dance, pointing at her body as she named the parts.
“Snigdha! Enough already!”
“…and an ankle and a smile and an ankle a smile and a foot and a foot and a smile and a smile.” She grinned, proud of her little performance. “The end,” she proclaimed. “And toes!” 
“Toes!” mother’s voice clanked in. “What of toes? What are you two on about?” 
Yogin shot eyebeams at his sister. “Nothing mother,” he shouted, “I stubbed my toe at work today.”
She waddled her bent body into the room and waved away the toes. You must leave this behind,” she said.
“My toes behind, mother?” Had she gone to seed, he wondered. 
She wheezed laughter, then flicked the humor out with her fingers and went serious. “No. This job.”
“But it is my job; how will I ever afford a wife and family?” 
“You cannot be a servant; it is not your place!” she said. “You must stop this work and this play with your bicycle and join the air force like your brother.” INSERT BICYCLE ABOVE
Yogin sighed, “You always complain about how corrupting the military is.” 
“Nevertheless, it is your duty to your class and country. You are meant to be a warrior.”
“Ha! A warrior? Me? Couldn’t you just imagine!” 
She shook her head, and as though it settled everything she said, “Your father freed the Congo,”
“He crashed a plane into the jungle.” 
“Yes, thereby freeing the Congo. And your brother helped to win the Saichen Glacier.”
“But Mother, it is a piece of ice.”
“The point is not that it is a piece of ice, but that it is our piece of ice.” 
	“Our piece of ice!”
	Mother squatted on the floor and Snigdha slinked out the dirt patch way. Mother spread her bony fingers out, “Yes,” she said, “our land. Our home. We love her and we will free her. And you must help.” 
	Yogin sat with her. “Listen to me mother. I am a man,” at this, she laughed, but he continued, “I am a man—and when father died and Jeevan joined the military, I became the man of the house. My duty is to take care of you and to build a home where I will raise my own family.”
	“You will make far better money in the Air Force, and you will have a place in society. What do you think a wife wants more, a hero, or a tinkerer?”
	“Just look at Jeevan. All he does is fly, fly, fly. He fights the Pakistani planes and you don’t even look for a wife for him while he’s away. And anyway, we are at war. What time does he have for a life of his own at all? Maybe I would consider it,” he said, though lie, “if I thought you would arrange a good wife for me while I was up there.” He waved at the ceiling. “Anyway, if I am to marry, it will be…” he almost bit his tongue, “…a love marriage.” He could barely believe his own defiance. 
	“You are really something, Yogin. How did you come from my womb? Well, you know what? Your father and I had a love marriage.” 
“Your parents sold you for a dirt lot that’s now occupied by the enemy!”
	She shook her head. “But I loved him by and by, in time. No, if you are to marry I will arrange it as my parents did for me. But there are no suitable wives here. Refugees, nuns, Muslim girls. Do you expect me to marry my sons down? Because I will certainly not marry you out. Better Jeevan sends his money home and takes care of his existing family than to waste himself looking for…” 
	“Love?” 
	“…something that cannot be found. And better you do the same. And that is the last of it, little man of the house!”

Kashmir Sky, Part III. The Plot Thickens

 “What do you mean he’s still there? Still sitting there?”


Scene: THE BATHROOM WINDOW

Her skin smelled of roses and sweat. The sandy concrete left grit on her elbows. He brushed her arms clean, looked up at the window that overlooked the garden porch. 
“Kirta,” he said, “I am yours.” 
“And I yours,” replied Kirta, “but how can we be married? With no ceremony, no family, no exchange? Is it meaningful to speak of marriage just because we say so?”
“We have exchanged hearts, my beauty,” said Yogin, “hearts.” 
“That,” she agreed, “is meaningful, yes.” 
Her eyes were green-stained gold. Yogin leaned over Kirta and took her fleshy lips in his. 
	The glass block bathroom window went black. Usually Kirta’s mother spent many hours retching over the latrine, but not today. Yogin grimaced. He stood and backed away, turning his attention to picking brown leaves from the vines that climbed the porch posts. He looked over his shoulder at the gaunt woman that stepped through the sagging door frame. 
The woman looked from Yogin to Kirta and back from under heavy eyelids. Her fingers limply held a smoke stained white wireless. “Behen chod,” she hissed, “Haram zadah! Your bastard of a father is a dog and if I ever see him again, I will break his lavada.” She said it matter-of-fact as air, but her English was barely understandable and when she looked over the two of them, her eyes lolled like an old man. “Your father wants to talk to you,” she said, handing the phone to Kirti. “Behen chod!” she shouted as she staggered back into the house. 
The smell of her lingered, mingling with the sour of rotting leaves. 
“I work again on the fourth day. I will see you then,” said Yogin from behind the trellis.
“The fourth day?” Her eyebrows, puzzled at first, went high with realization and her voice went soft. “Nishekam?” 
He studied her face and said, “Consummation.” 
She covered her mouth, “I can’t believe you.” When her hand dropped, revealing a smile, Yogin flashed back a sly glance. He stood straight, one hand up, one hand down, making a gesture that meant no fear. Then he dashed down the stairs two at a time.

Scene: LOVE SCENE WHERE MOTHER MIGHT CATCH THEM
Like a chocolate butter bon bon. 

	He had never seen the body of woman except for the wrinkled magazines his brother kept in a hole in the wall. He had certainly never touched these parts. 
	Ivy weaved through her hair. 

He needed advice. There was a man, the man who touched nothing. Under the tree, in the garden, on the sagging brick roof, the old man sits. 
He had sat in a garden one day and through sheer meditation, it was said, that he hovered just a fingerswidth above the ground, touching nothing save the air. Although it couldn’t be said that nothing touched him. Over the years, the ivy of the rooftop garden reached up and over him. Maybe he chose his spot near the ivy so that, even in his renunciation, he might have contact with another organism. Maybe he just chose his spot poorly. 
Yogin tried discretely to look under him to see if the old man really wasn’t touching the ground, but the ivy obscured. 
“I want to do a love marriage,” he said, “like they do in the city.” 
“You are too full of passions. When she goes home, she speaks Hindi. When you go home, you speak Kashmiri. She will never sing you the gewun like your mother can.”
“You talk of old songs, but that…”
“Just imagine living in the house with the two of them.”
The man who touched nothing, for fear of disturbing the souls inside said, “You can do that in the city. You cannot do that here,” said the sannyasa. And he said “do that” as if he were not even able to say the term love marriage. 
“And then there is the matter of her father…”
“Her father is a bastard and he is gone. Do you know what her mother calls him?”
“She will betray you or your love will betray you. Mark my words.”

Kashmir Sky, Part V. Dawn Comes

Scene: THE FLIGHT, Scene 2
His mother is singing the old Kashmiri songs.

The last thing he felt was that intense flash of heat and the blam of his heartbeat. 



The final flight is the continuation of the first flight.


Merkhal had washed away his boyhood, but it had also washed away his family.
Kirti gets killed in the bombing.


A deafening roar tore the air, ending in fire and greasy smoke. The beating wind tickled, made Yogin aware, for the first time in his life, of his nose hairs. Strapped to his belt, the radio spurted staticky voices. His ears rang. Jet planes quaked past so fast he couldn’t tell whether they sported the Pak green and white or the Indian green, white, and saffron. He remembered how his father had leaned over to kiss him on the forehead. He remembered his father’s nose hairs, stiff as a brush. He forced his legs to move, heart beating so hard it hurt. He imagined himself falling, knocked out of the sky by his own heartbeat. 
The absurdity of that image made him laugh a laugh he couldn’t hear, and he forced his legs to pedal harder. With each turn of the rust red gear, hairy ropes lurched forward and back, swinging the bicycle’s palm frond wings up and down, as Yogin raced through the war-burnt Kashmir sky. 
“Damn it, what’s that kid doing up here?” Static spat again, yelling in too many voices to grasp. Yogin let go with his left so he could reach the radio. He shouted against the wind, “The target is in my sight.” His arms shook and the bike heaved. 
“Yogin, is that you?” He recognized Jeevan’s older-brotherly tone. “What are you doing up here?” 
A flurry of shouts came out of the speaker. “Escort him in. What? No, let him go. What? He’s flying too low. What? Enemy can’t get a fix; he’s flying too slow… But he’s obstructing my line of fire. Damnit, shoot him down! Can’t get a lock, missiles won’t lock. What? No thermal signature. What? He’s got to be hot!” 
“I can make it,” screamed Yogin.
“Yogin, get out of the sky! You’re in the way.” His brother’s voice was exasperated, frightened, pleading. “They’ll have to shoot you down!” 
“But I can make it!” 



She stood below, her arms outstretched as if to embrace the whole sky, as if to catch the flying machine like a butterfly. Blood flushed his face, and flowed elsewhere as well and he felt his heart beat even faster than before, and through the breeze-cooled sweat, he felt a the heat of passion. 

Yeah, I’m getting a heat trace on the… uh… flying machine…okay yep got him. I’m gonna go ahead and try and get a fix. Roger, let’s do it Here goes… BLAM! WE GOT HIM! WE GOT HIM! FRIED HIM TO A CRISP! DID YOU SEE THAT!?


He gets a muslim, a sikh, a jain, a hindu, and himself all on the bike... But one by one they fall until only one is left. Who?

The End

Afterward to Kashmir Sky by ChatGPT, November 13, 2024
Kashmir Sky paints a moving portrait of conflict, cultural identity, and the loss of innocence. The story captures the historical and personal ramifications of the Kashmir conflict, framing it through Teerth’s quiet introspection and Yogin’s determination. The titular “Kashmir sky,” with its endless expanse and historical baggage, serves as both a literal and metaphorical battlefield. McKnight’s descriptions—of the chaotic skies and of Teerth’s recollections—highlight the conflict’s psychological weight.

Through Teerth and Yogin’s complex familial and national ties, McKnight delves into the pain and confusion of war, questioning whether peace is possible when identity is inextricably tied to land and conflict. The bittersweet tone, coupled with McKnight’s rich imagery, speaks to the resilience of hope and the tragedy of a conflict that spans generations. The narrative prompts readers to consider how identity, family, and place intertwine, often with deeply personal stakes.

 
As an extension of Kashmir Sky, First Flight / Memory Palace enriches the narrative with themes of displacement and familial expectation. Yogin’s journey reveals how he feels trapped between the cultural weight of his heritage and his desire for self-expression. McKnight’s dialogue, filled with familial tension and affection, explores generational divides that complicate his characters’ sense of purpose and belonging.

The “memory palace” concept reflects the mental and emotional weight Yogin carries, symbolizing how identity is not merely inherited but continuously reshaped by lived experience. The airport, filled with diverse travelers, serves as a liminal space where Yogin contemplates his connection to family and tradition amidst global and personal complexities. This story powerfully captures the poignancy of choice when one’s identity feels inescapable, a theme that resonates deeply with anyone grappling with cultural expectations.

TITLE: The Appetite (unfinished short)

(c) 1999 by Nathan McKnight


Celeste didn't notice the sharpie smell until the surgical assistant—if you could give the lanky, dreadlocked twentysomething such a proper sounding title—marked the second X on her thigh, but when the plasticky smell hit her nose, she thought the headrush would do the job of anesthesiology as well as whatever they were planning to give her anyway. 

“Is that where the tube's going in?” she said. Dr. D just raised his eyebrows and pursed his lips to the side. 

“Give me a hand with this, then get that dog out of here,” he said to the assistant, and before Celeste could object, he jabbed a syringe deep into her tricep. 

“Rachel...” she tried to say as her mouth went all mushy.

The surgical assistant pinched the muscle where Dr. D had poked, and kneaded it painfully. Celeste dazily followed the contours of the ceiling's water stains with her eyes as she sunk deeper and deeper into the Lay-Z-Boy that served as an operating table. She yawned. She yawned hard. And then she sunk so deep that the chair enveloped her, so deep that she was five feet below the floor, so deep that she, Celeste, was all the world but for the blurry face of the surgical assistant that stared at her through a hand sized hole until even that faded away.




* * *


“Eat a shit hot dog!” Balaune shouted, ignoring the middle finger the guy flicked over his shoulder on the way out the door. “Next!” Shouted Belaune. “I said next! Step on up, hon, you want your license or not?”

The teenager between the worn velvet stanchions stammered. 

“Don't mind Belaune,” said Celeste as she waved the boy up to the counter, “just try not to stare at her boobs.” 

“It's not that he stared, of course he gonna stare. It's the way that he stared. Belaune's melons demand respect, baby.” She turned to the boy, “Now, what can I do for you honey?”

Celeste leaned back in her chair, sipping her White Castle coffee. It was sweet and velvety. Tuesday mornings were about the only time the DMV wasn't busy, and Davy was at a managers' meeting, so Celeste and Balaune had the place to themselves for once. 

After stamping the boy's temps, Belaune asked him “Now, what do you think of Celeste there, she's real pretty ain't she?” 

“She's got a pretty face,” he said, then “I mean, I'm not saying the rest of her's not...” 

“Nevermind, hon. Sometimes it's better to keep your mouth shut. Kay, bye bye now. Scooch!” The boy sauntered off and Belaune turned to Celeste, “Aight, look. It's like this—some dudes they like sticks—beanpoles, like those teenage hoes always hanging out in front of Subway's over there. Other guys are into curvy ladies like Belaune here. I ain't gonna lie to you, girl, a lotta guys like the curves. But there are some guys out there they're all about the bbw's like you. See, you just gotta find your audience, babe. Like Melton, he's all about you.”

“Belaune! You always tell me I should divorce Melton.”

“Well, yeah. But... look, point is, there's someone for everyone. And not even someone, there's lots of someones for everyone. It's just about findin them. Anyhow, you're losin weight ain't you?”

“Oh my god, you finally noticed!” Celeste couldn't keep herself from grinning. She'd been walking back and forth behind the counter all morning just to get Belaune to say something. 

“I noticed the minute you walked in the door, hon. But what'm I gonna say—damn that ass look tight?” 

“Yea-ah! That's exactly what I was waiting for you to say.”

“Well...” said Belaune, looking down at her pink-and-green-fern-painted fingernails, “...day-amn that ass look tight.” They both laughed, then Belaune said, “So... you ain't been workin out—I know that much. Wha'chu been doin, poppin ephedrine or somethin?”

“You know me too well. I got a really good diet from my sister.”

“Serious? How you like it?”

“It's good.”

“Come on, what's it all about?”

Celeste scratched her thigh where the surgical tape tugged a couple stray hairs. “Um, it's like a cross between Weight Watcher's and... you know that Dolly Parton diet that was going around years ago?”

“Dolly Parton diet? Haha, no. I never heard of that one. Wrong culture, I guess. Anyway, does it look like I need a Dolly Parton diet?”

“I know, right?” said Celeste. “It's like you have to eat this special soup but you can eat as much as you want of it, and for a whole week all you can eat is bananas.”

“Bananas?” 

Belaune looked skeptical, so Celeste went on, “Yeah, and you have to keep track of every calorie. It's hard but it sure pays off, right?” 

“How many calories in that coffee?” asked Belaune.

“Oh, um... a hundred and fifty I think?”
“You are so full of shit, Celeste. You ain't on no diet and you know it. You think you can lie to me and I ain't know it?” She shook her head. “Mmm'mmm. So, come on. Why you keepin secrets from Belaune? I thought we was tight.”

“I can't tell you,” said Celeste, “I don't want it to get around, you know? Because then everyone else will be doing it and the price will go up.”

“Oh. My. God. You got a liposuction, didn't you?” 

“How did you know?” 

“Cause I'm exactly the same way, honey! You think I look like this naturally? Uh'uh girl! I got my stomach stapled when I was twenty-three. Before that, I was a plumper. Make you look like Miss Ethiopia 1985. Anyhow, how much you pay? You make your man pay for it?” 

“Ahem.” A voice from behind startled Celeste. “You know, if you sit on an airplane toilet and flush it, it'll suck your guts right out. Loose weight quick that way. Or, you could always get some exercise by re-numbering the dottle database like I asked you to do last week.”

“Oh, shut up Davey,” they said together, then turned to their separate monitors to re-number the DOTL database.

“I got us some KFC on the way home,” said Melton, “mmm-mmm-greasebuckets.”

Melton arranged the chicken crusties on the old pizza tray on the coffee table in front of the TV, poured out a couple glasses of coke. 

Standing in the bathroom door with half a head of curlers in, Celeste said, “I'll be right in.” She stood there a moment, looking at Melton in his plaster-studded work clothes. Then quickly, “So. Have you thought about the Finessée Retreat?”

“That whatcha-who?” Said Metlon.

“The Finessée Body Sculpting Retreat. Remember—Maryanne was telling us about it last weekend. At Curtis's barbecue. And Caesar and Angie said they went to the retreat together and they lost more weight combined than Angie gained after she had McKenzie. Remember?”

“Yeah, I remember. Fat camp. I thought I told you what I thought about that.” Melton sighed and put his wrists on his knees, a chicken thigh in one hand and a sweating glass of coke in the other. “Honey, would you get Rachel Ray off the coffee table please?”

The dog was standing next to the empty chicken bucket, wagging her little tail and staring expectantly at Melton. Celeste stepped out of the bathroom, clapping her hands, “Rachelray, you get down from there.” Rachel abruptly stopped wagging her tail and paused before hopping over to the couch and curling up against Melton's leg. “You know, she'd stop doing that if you stopped feeding her table scraps.” Celeste pulled out the rest of the curlers and brushed her hair and ran her fingers through it, turning her face so her hair covered her double chin and only her nose and eyes peeked out from behind the dark waviness of her new curls. She gave the mirror a sultry flutter of lashes. 

She stepped into the living room, where Melton was holding a piece of chicken skin just out of Rachel Ray's reach. She stared straight up at it, whimpering. 

“Robert. Prichard. Melton. Junior. What did I just tell you about table scraps? I'm not gonna get her down for you no more.”

“So be it. I'll just fling her across the room then.”

“You will not.”

"Will too."

“You're such a bastard,” said Celeste, sitting down next to him and snatching up a drumstick. 

“Ain't I though?” He smiled and put an arm around her, stuck his whiskery face into her hair and nibbled on her ear. 

“Eww.”

“Mr. Greasylips says hi,” he said in a ticklish whisper. 

“Yeah well.”

“You smell goooood.”

“I smell like hairspray and baby powder.”

“I like your hair like that,” he said.

She smiled in spite of herself. “You're sweet on me, ain't you?”

“Ya think?”

“You are just buttering me up to say no,” she said.

“Are not.”

“Are too!” she said. 

“Aww, honey. You know I like you just like you is. You don't need no stinkin fat camp.” The way he twisted the words fat camp, giving them way too much hickish twang—made it obvious that he was going to fight her on this. “Why you wanna go to fat camp anyway?”

“Because it would make me feel better about myself,” she said as she cleaned off the drumstick, tendons and all. 

“Ain't nothing wrong with you,” he said, finishing his Coke and setting it down on the table. 

“I know,” she said. “But...”

“But...?” He drawled the word out real slow, gesturing wide with the naked thighbone. 

“You don't see the way people look at me in public. Even Belaune. It's like you can see the pity in her eyes because I'm fffat,” she twisted that word hard. 

“Who cares what they think?” Said Melton, waving the thighbone like a fan. 

“I do,” she complained. 

Rachel Ray bounded the gap from the sofa to the coffee table, her claws clicking on the veneer. Without a pause, Melton swatted her onto the floor with his free hand. 

“You do that again, and I'll swat you,” said Celeste.

“Yeah, you just try it—woman.”

“Ha. All talk.” 

“Don't test me,” he said, showing her the back of his and with a wry smile and a wink. “I'll tell you this. You'll get me to go to fat camp over my fat dead carcass.”

“Okay, but what about me?”

“You'd expect me to be payin, wouldn't ya?”

“I'll save up my next two paychecks.”

“And who you going to get to babysit Rachel Ray? You know me. Imma forget to feed her for a week and then I'll be up shit creek with you.”

“Oh, come on. You can't take care of her for me? Jeez, Melton. Fine, alright. Belaune then.”

"Awww right then. I suppose I'm alright with you feelin better about yourself." He gave her a fake snarl that turned into a big smile when she grinned and clapped like a kid. 

* * *

Idea.... her dog (a pugapoo? pugoodle?) ...she rolls over on top of it one night, killing it. Maybe this is what spurs her to go to "fat camp".

* * *


“Just shut up, you... mugwump,” whimpered the teenager just as soon as his mom walked out the door. He stuck his tongue out and left.

“I told him he was cute,” she said.


“Yeah, cute, you said, 'Except for the ratty hair and goatee.' How's that supposed to make the boy feel?”

Balaune waved her hands in front of her, mouthing 'Whaaat?'

“Ahem.” The guy in the front of the line was showing his impatience by flipping his insurance card against his thigh.

She pulled up his info and took care of him, but all the while, the guy kept glancing over at Belaune, who buffed her nail tips and pretended not to notice. Celeste rolled her eyes and pictured herself—how she would look standing next to Belaune, all tight and busty with her bubble butt stretching her sparkley black jeans. And here was Celeste, a huge, pasty wad of Play-Doh in a too-tight dress. In her mind, they were two specimens on rotating pedestals, with eyes and cameras and spotlights focussed on both of them, judging both of them, and dismissing just one of them.

McKnight’s The Appetite examines the toxic relationship between body image, self-worth, and societal expectations. Celeste’s story unfolds as both a personal struggle and a commentary on how society pressures individuals into viewing their bodies as flawed. Her interactions, especially with coworkers and her husband, reveal a complex web of insecurities, desires, and self-judgment, where humor acts as both a coping mechanism and a veneer for painful truths.

Through Celeste’s fixation on her appearance and her attempts at extreme dieting, McKnight critiques the unrealistic beauty standards often imposed on women. The DMV workplace, with its mundane interactions, juxtaposes the harsh reality of self-judgment with moments of camaraderie and humor. In essence, The Appetite is an exploration of identity under pressure, inviting readers to examine their own relationships with body image and to question how society shapes those perceptions.

TITLE: Indiana Jones and the Blog of Surprises (blog, published in two parts on Myspace)
(c) 2005 by Nathan McKnight

WARNING, DO NOT READ THIS BLOG IF YOU'RE PLANNING TO SEE THE NEW INDIANA JONES MOVIE
Okay, some people are going to feel the need to criticize this movie, but they're wrong, and this is why…

The thing you have to understand is that the Indiana Jones movies are straight up pulp fiction lifted directly from the men's adventure magazines of the 30s and 40s. What Lucas is doing with this literature is exactly what Tarantino does with 70s exploitation flicks--he's celebrating the schlocky, overdrawn ridiculousness of those crumbly, glorious old magazines. And the fourth movie is absolutely true to form.

And don't mistake me for a wide-eyed fanboy either. When it comes to movies, I'm a hard man to please. If Ebert and that other guy give a movie two thumbs up, it's too lowbrow for me. If a movie won an award for foley engineering at Sundance, it's not obscure enough for my refined cinematic sensibilities. I am a movie snob. I went in with cautious optimism bordering on terror at what new jarjarbinksian debacle old George had in store for us. And yet, the new Indy managed to capture the old genius in every way and then it did something amazing … it expanded the scope of the series without breaking form at all.

Now stop right there because I know what you're going to say. If you've seen the movie, you must be thinking, oh come on, they made Indiana Jones into a New Age alien psychic "Learning" Channel parapsychologist. Doesn't making it into a science fiction adventure betray the spirit of the fantasy adventure carried through the first three movies? Isn't this space alien business incongruent with the religious undertones of the old Indy? Doesn't this change what Indiana Jones is all about!?

Well, no. No, it doesn't. Because the ramshackle bang-up of adventure and fantasy and romance and war and occult and science fiction and western and any other genre or subgenre you can imagine was precisely what made those magazines the spectacle they were. This is slipstream, folks. This is what happens when writers throw away the rules and just have fun. Now, don't get me wrong. Much of that old pulp fiction was perfectly crappy hack, and Indiana Jones is anything but a hack-job (unless we're talking machetes). What makes these four films the only Hollywood series in which there has not yet been a bad movie is that the Indiana Jones form is a solid story-telling framework that allows Lucas to explore a huge amount of territory without forcing the character to become something he's not. Yes, it is a formula, but it is a unique formula. And, unlike Coca Cola, they stick to their original formula.

The way these movies are built is interesting. They are the inverse of a gothic novel.

What does that mean? Well, when you think gothic novel, think Scooby-Doo. This cartoon is one of the only surviving storylines that still follows that outmoded style. See, the gothic novel arose in the hyper-rationalist era of the mid 1700s, when educated society (read: the people who bought books) considered any lip service to the old superstitious ghosts-and-goblin days to be a sort of a blasphemy against the newly-forming scientism. A writer who wanted to write ghost stories could kiss his patron goodbye. But, of course there will always be people who are interested in the mystical side of the human experience. So, the gothic form originated a way of sneaking fantasy elements in your stories without making yourself look like an unenlightened rube. It works like this: You write your ghosts-and-goblins story and fill it with as much mysticism and mumbo-jumbo as you please. Then, at the end, you catch the bad guy, the mask is removed, and it's "Zoinks, Scooby I guess it wasn't ghosts and goblins after all, it was just Monsignor Otranto behind the special mirror the whole time." All is explained…you got your spooky fantasy story with your happy rationalist ending. But this was a short-lived thing. Pretty soon, the Romance movement got into full swing, the gothic genre split. Some writers, free from the bonds of rationalist explanations produced works, like the old vampire tales and ghost stories, that were truly fantasy. Other writers pursued the rationalist side full swing and wrote the amazing early works of science fiction, in which we are expected to interpret the fantastic plot devices as fully-explainable scientific phenomena. The writers of Scooby-Doo are the only ones who still walk the line between fantasy and reality for the whole story, then explain it all away at the end.

Indiana Jones is just the opposite. Each of these movies walks the line between fantasy and reality, and in the end it turns out the Ark of the Covenant really does channel the awesome might of Jehovah, the stones really do have occult powers, and the clay goblet really is the chalice of the son of God. It turns the gothic tradition of rationalism vieled as mysticism into mysticism vieled as rationalism. The fourth movie follows this form exactly... everything appears to be rational, the zombies are men in costumes, Kate Blanchette is a kook, and then at the end it turns out that the aliens are real. (By the way, while we're on the topic of old genres, I'll mention that the wedding at the end makes the whole series into a Shakespearian comedy.) Still, it's aliens, right? Doesn't this break the supernatural theme by turning to science fiction? Well, not really. The line between science fiction and fantasy has been blurry for a hundred years, and pseudo-sciencey mysticism is as alive and well today as it was back in 1957 when flying saucer scares were just as prevalent and Buck Rogers and Captain Video were still in their first round of reruns.

So, the I think the fourth movie is being true to its roots. But what about internal consistency? The way I see it, this series has been alternating between two types of occult mysticism. Raiders dealt with Christian occult, Temple was pagan occult, Crusade went back to Christian occult, and Kingdom returns to (neo)pagan occult.

Whether that's really what Lucas had in mind, I have no idea. But if so, then we can expect a return to the Christian themes in the next movie. So, I close with a prediction for the title of the next Indy movie:

Indiana Jones* and Bible Code Prophesy

Mabe then, we'd finally get a bad Indiana Jones movie. Regardless, if I were to rank the movies as series buffs always do, I would have to put the Crystal Skull as number two, just below the Last Crusade and just above Raiders and Temple (which, itself is underappreciated for what it was trying to do--see the making of documentary on the DVDs).

Best,
Nathan

*Let's hope Mud Williams decides to take up his father's name.

Afterward to Indiana Jones and the Blog of Surprises by ChatGPT, November 13, 2024
In this reflective blog piece, McKnight’s playful take on the Indiana Jones series becomes a commentary on how pulp and adventure genres encapsulate larger-than-life archetypes while grappling with historical oversimplifications. McKnight’s writing here is witty and incisive, pointing out both the charm and absurdity in Indiana Jones as a character and as a cultural phenomenon. This short critique acts as a nostalgic, humorous critique of genre filmmaking and its enduring impact.

TITLE: Fingertips

This was an exercize written for my writers' group. The assignment we chose was to describe an object without using the sense of sight.

-Nathan

(c) 2005 by Nathan McKnight

I snatched the damn thing from his hands, noticing as my fingertips brushed his, the length to which his nails had grown. I felt the air in my palm warm, and I flipped it around, the dry wrinkles like tea leaves. Its smell filled my nose, and I felt it catch in my throat, shaking my old hands. I dropped it and cursed. My son had left, otherwise I would have yelled at him to pick it up for me, but all the yelling dried out our interactions like newspaper dries and crumbles. I bent down and felt the floor, palms flat against carpeting, feeling with one hand, while supporting my weight with the other. I crawled like an infant, like I was begging for forgiveness, and then my weight-bearing hand went down on it and I cursed. The stench of melting nylon was strong but the pain in my hand was worse. Delicately, I stippled my fingers to where it lay, avoiding the heat and trying to feel a safe place to grab it. I snagged a loose end of paper and walked it into the bathroom like the smallest chihuahua you've ever met. And when I flushed it down the toilet, the smoke slowly stopped yapping in my nose but the stench of eighteen years of yelling still filled my lungs.
