Author's Intro to The Gilgamesh Golem

The following story, "The Gilgamesh Golem", was originally published in "the tloating liars' club", a slick DIY fiction magazine with a circulation of about 300, which ran for three issues, plus one finished but unpublished issue between late 2002 and mid-2003. The magazine was published by Nathan McKnight, along with Joe Bellomy (his father and sometimes coauthor), Shannon Rampe (a writer working in Arlington, VA) and Ben Crites (a fantastic writer we've kind of lost contact with over the years, apart from a couple of serendipitous bumpings-into). The four wrote almost all of the stories, with the exception of a couple of pieces written by close friends Jade Seven and Lauren Rampe. McKnight took the lead in pushing the writers' group to come together to showcase their talent. Bellomy did almost all of the art (with the exception of one story illustrated by Jade Seven--"Save the Kildans", an extremely experimental science fiction piece by McKnight). Bellomy's color cover art is raw and beautiful, rough but detailed, and his black and white interior spot illos range from haunting to playful. 

McKnight started this story in the wake of 9/11. See if you can spot the political allusions in the text. Hint: If the religous subject matter doesn't say it all, look closely at the names, remembering McKnight's often subtle--but often not--wordplay. 


TITLE: The Gilgamesh Golem
(c) 2003 by Nathan McKnight



 This may be the last story ever written. I write it to God, who would leave me alone on this Earth where all the nations have worn down to plains. The minerals that compose me come from the beginning of the world, shaped by wind and time. But the spirit that inhabits this clay form is human, shaped by other humans. I've waited the lifetimes of races to be discovered by some visitor or some god, and perhaps my hope will yet be borne out in the short time I have left. But, if the end-times sun undoes the magic that made me or bakes my limbs into useless branches, you should know that it is the same kilny sun whose light I count on to envitrify my words into the soft clay before the salt-laden tides of the last sea smother them out.

The story begins in Jewish Harlem, before the southern blacks replaced the Jews, before the Hispanic migrants replaced the blacks, before the Arab refugees replaced the migrants, and before the Israilis replaced them…

* * *

Paul's mother was displeased in the extreme when she found out that her money was coming from boxing. She had stopped taking the blood money, as she called it, when word got around the neighborhood that Paul "The Maul" Stein had ended a fight by clocking one of the Italian boys and blinding him in one eye. Consequently, Paul and his fiancée had a modest savings.

They had planned to get married in grand fashion, possibly even in their own semi-luxurious apartment whose neo-Egyptian façade was shaded during the hottest Scene of the afternoon by the shadow of the new Empire State building, where Holly worked as an assistant executive secretary. It was the nature of their respective professions that they spent most of the day apart. Their only contact was at dinnertime, after Holly had spent a long day in the office without a break and Paul was readying himself for the evening's fight. Sometimes, the famished couple would go back to Harlem where Yiddish and Italian and Irish voices filled the air with the restless spirits of the old countries, voices that complimented Mother Stein's cooking like the spices of the east. Holly would impress them with anecdotes of rich foreigners who always seemed to make extraordinary demands of the secretaries or, on lucky days, to bestow upon them ludicrously large tips in exchange for everyday kindness.

It was on one such occasion that Paul first heard the name Israel ben Muhammad. Holly normally paid scant attention to changes of leadership in the company, but the buy-out by an Arabian oil baron precipitated an especially large shake-up among the employees. The stars portend opportunity, Mrs. Stein had told her son knowingly. Indeed, Holly ended up filling her own supervisor's newly vacant position as lead executive secretary, handling the appointment books of ben Muhammad himself. Paul learned of her new boss's real estate aspirations on the same day that the change in management for the East Central apartment buildings took effect. Notices were first posted to inform the residents on that very day. They were on all the walls and in all the mailboxes. They were given by hand to every occupant, sometimes more than once. The postings shrouded the fine sheeny wallpaper with a quiltwork of mottled parchment. In order to keep the "criminal element" at bay—some anti-Arab graffitoism had taken place in the otherwise immaculate children's daytime care facility—all residents were asked to carry punch cards. In addition, for reasons that were poorly explained, rent would be upped for certain occupants. Paul and Holly would have to pay more than triple the normal due.

The old landlord's chair and desk were replaced with a velveteen paisley dos-à-dos, and a new door had been erected, making an office of the adjoining suite. Paul defiantly pounded on the door, though he knew he wasn't even supposed to be in this office-cum-foyer. Hey Mac, that area is off limits now, one of the janitors had explained to Paul, whose punch card was already scuffed and bent from being tried in all the newly locked doors that metamorphosed the building into the impregnable Egyptian tomb its lotus trumeaux had always bragged it was. The janitor had diffused Paul's evident rage when he punched his own card, as payback he said, for the five bucks he'd won off last week's fight. I wouldn't mind if you gave that new landlord a good right hook anyways, Slugger. But here he was, pounding on the door instead of the landlord.

A scratchy electric voice, like those at the larger boxing arenas warned that unappointmented queries may result in fees or even termination of the lease, and Paul argued with it momentarily about the appropriateness of his visit until he slipped the note under the door. The rent envelope came on the 28th of every month, and this one had contained instructions on how to contact the landlord for a lease review. Why the lease needed reviewing was second among Paul's concerns, which were dominated by the improbable restrictions on his options for an appointment: Anytime after 4pm on a business day before this month's rent is due.

"Mr. ben Muhammad will see you now," said the speaker.

The suite was air-conditioned—a luxury even for such a fine apartment building. The secretary directed Paul around the corner to the landlord's relaxation chamber. The conversation did not go well. Paul spoke, facing away from the landlord, who lounged in a freestanding washbasin, smoking from a long meerschaum pipe whose smoke did a better job keeping away the flies than the peacock plume he switched them with. Ben Muhammad sat quietly until Paul had exhausted his lexis in a jeremiad plea that touched on the wedding, elderly parents, and the potential for injuries in his line of work.

"Please return to the foyer while I consider your case, Mr. Stein," said ben Muhammad.

Paul did as he was asked to, but he found himself scratching the extravagant gold leafwork from one of the dos-à-dos's arms. The repetitive motion calmed him.

"You will be billed for defacing the furniture," the secretary's voice itched over the loudspeaker. "This fiancée you mentioned; she lives with you here, does she? In that case, we will have to relocate one of you to the nearest vacant apartment until after the wedding. You will, of course continue to pay the increased rates. If you are unwilling to comply with the rules of this building, you are encouraged move into any of the other East Central apartment buildings, some of which retain independent management. That will be all, Mr. Stein."

"Excuse me?"

"One moment Mr. Stein…Mr. ben Mohammad says that he will be claiming your fiancée as well."

"Just what...?"

"Mr. Stein," it was ben Muhammad's voice this time. "Because of the problems you've caused here today, your tenancy has been terminated. The lease will be transferred to the name of Miss Landis. She will stay here with me, where you may trust that she will be in good hands."

"Just who in hell do you think you are?"

"Sir, please try to understand. She is mine. Since before you were born, I've dwelt on that face. I realize you love her, but I loved her first. You see, when my father brought me to New York as a boy, the beauty of American women astounded me. But there was one in particular. Her name was Honey. The wife of Milt Landis, her face caused my heart to weep. It was the memory of that face which I followed back to your country. Now, I've seen that face again, and it is Holly, the daughter of Milt and Honey Landis. She will be my bride. Please leave the premises now. Do not attempt to reenter your apartment."

"That will be all Mr. Stein," concluded the secretary.

On the way out, Paul regretted not provoking the guards who stood by to see that he boarded the elevator to the lobby. As he stepped out the front door, a voice sounded from above.

"Hey, you're the sparrer. Pauler the Mauler, right?" An overalled man squatted on the top rung of a ladder, holding a pile of gold foil in one hand, and a jar of glue with a paintbrush jutting from it in the other. He spoke with a British accent. "Do you live here?"

"Not anymore."

"That's too bad. I suppose you don't like the management? Well, a lot of folks round here feel that way. I can't say as I share the opinion, though. If it weren't for Mr. Mohammed, I wouldn't have a job. My family and me flew all the way from Norwich and Mr. ben Muhammad paid for the Zeppelin tickets. He says he's going to make these apartments into Utopia. I feel like I'm working for the pharaoh himself."

Paul threw his body against the ladder and lifted the little man by his collar. Several onlookers fled, but one of them—the janitor from upstairs—swung a fist at the air.

Paul bloodied the artist's nose and lips, dropped him and boxed his ears; he would have killed the man if he hadn't seen the Tommie-gun-wielding guards appear. As he left the chaotic scene, he looked over his shoulder and the janitor shouted out. "Way to go slugger," he said, only to be beaten and dragged off by a gang of security guards.

Another guard hollered, "Cut him off, he's heading across the street."

Paul and Holly were supposed to meet at the foot of the Empire State Building, but there was little chance ben Muhammad would allow that to happen, so instead Paul hopped a jittery trolley into Jewish Harlem. Ghetto faces lined with cracks stared at Paul, like ancient pottery sitting alongside a museum reproduction designed to imitate the beauty of newness. Mrs. Stein's tenement was infested with age, from the ladies with pepper pot figures to the tall-hatted Hassidic men who discussed philosophy over chess. Even the Irish boys who played stickball out front seemed oddly wise for their age. Paul had grown up here, but he felt he had pampered away his street wisdom. He no longer belonged.

"What should I do, ma?"

"Stay here with me. Holly will know where to find you."

It took Paul a few days to realize that his mother was wrong. He had taken out his anger in the boxing ring. He told his mother of the Micks and Negroes he had beaten, no doubt winning his fans more than a few dollars. He didn't mention the Jewish boxer that would never again be able to bite into a fresh apple with his own teeth because of Paul's callused fists. One night, Paul went back to the apartment building dressed in his late father's ragged work clothes. Paul and Holly's apartment was on the seventh floor, and he could barely make out shapes moving in the golden light. He stared for nearly half an hour before he saw Holly poke her head out the window. He shouted up to her, but she didn't seem to hear.

"Paul the Maul! You don't look so good, slugger."

The janitor had a huge can of garbage that he dragged towards the alley. He said that ben Muhammad had allowed him to keep his job after he had promised not to talk to a single person while on duty. In the quiet of the alley, they talked about Holly's coming and going and the increase in modern conveniences, and the accompanying increase of security at the building. The whole place was air conditioned now, and the elevators were automated, but many of the tenants had been evicted to make room for newcomers who could afford the forbidding rent. Paul told the janitor that he was afraid ben Muhammad was holding his fiancée against her will.

"There's a fellow might be able to help you," said the janitor. "He's a rabbi."

Paul's mother was shocked. "Rabbi Samson's only a rabbi in title. The man is a lush. Don't you go near him." It turned out that Samson was a lascivious whoremonger who was rumored to masturbate at the talkie theaters. "Uncut Samson," they called him. He was, however, also a renowned mystic and that's what made up Paul's mind. Nobody doubted the Rabbi's powers.

After a particularly bloody match, Paul delivered himself to a doorway lit by a gas torch that stood below street level only two blocks from his mother's apartment building. The door had a bronze plate inscribed with Hebrew letters. With the streaked green corrosion and Paul's limited knowledge of Yiddish, he could only tell that it said something like "All Are Welcome."

Samson was a fat and greasy slob whose once-exquisite accommodations had been used beyond their capability, and now exuded a pungent fetor that seemed to waft out of the holes in the carpeting. The Rabbi glanced at Paul senior's clothing and said, "You can't afford my prices." Paul told him that there was plenty of money in the ring for anyone tough enough to claim it. Samson laughed at the implied threat, but waved him inside anyway.

"I know of Israel ben Muhammad. He's obsessed with himself, like most of the world's better men. Your fiancée would be well off to stay with him rather than a low-born boxer from the ghetto." Samson left him alone long enough to reconsider his decision, but before Paul's mind could make sense of these awful circumstances, the Rabbi emerged from behind a curtain-wall. "It is done. The girl will be yours. Now give me whatever money you have and go away."

Paul didn't know what to expect, if anything, but he couldn't stand to waste time in actionless wondering, so he walked as fast as he could to the apartment building whose neo-Egyptian façade dripped silver rain like mercury in the bright moonlight. Holly was there, waiting in the alley. She was silent. Paul tried to talk to her, but she seemed not to know him. She didn't respond at all unless she was asked a direct question or given a command.

"Are you okay?"

"Yes."

"What's happened to you?"

"I don't know."

He told Holly she would have to come with him, but she followed at a snail's pace, and when he carried her, he wasn't able to move much faster.

The walk back to Harlem ended as the sun was rising over Mrs. Stein's apartment. Though he collapsed with exhaustion in his mother's spare bedroom, his mind was restless with angry confusion. Holly lay open-eyed next to him, and when Paul finally gave up the fight to keep his eyes closed, she accompanied him arm-in-arm through the foggy morning street.

The Rabbi's door appeared to be stout but it only took one solid blow to unhinge it completely, allowing a cold draft to douse candles and to bear scrolls off tables. Uncut Samson was taken by surprise, nude, piggish, and shaking, when Paul grabbed him by the arm, sinking strong fingers into the flab. A sucker punch to the face was stopped when Paul felt a paralyzing blow to his own spine.

"Stay out of this. I'm going to kill him for ruining my bride," said ben Muhammad in a fearsome voice.

Paul turned on ben Muhammad and they began a dirty wrestling match. However, their battle was not yet to be. The fleshy slap of each strike quieted to nothing while the apartment filled with thick incense smoke until both men collapsed in a desperate embrace. They awoke lashed together and suspended over a smokeless fire and immediately resumed the struggle against each other, and now against the ropes, and against the flame.

"I will pay you," ben Muhammad yelled in between curses, "Holly, go get the money in my safe." Paul stopped slamming his skull against the other man's in time to see her run out the open door. Holly's departure left both men relieved enough to stop struggling. "If you think I want Miss Landis's body, you're mistaken. Soon, I'll have the body to take the women I wasn't strong enough to take in this body. I'm glad you both showed up here, but it's a pity that a golem fit for a wizard has to be wasted." He swung a lumpy arm towards an unrealistically muscular clay sculpture of ben Muhammad. "It took a long time to find a body sturdy enough to suit my tastes." He stroked the arm of the sculpture, and snickered. "You should be thankful. This magnificent creature is beyond value. It was fashioned in 1654 by Dutch Jews, fleeing the Portuguese in South America. They offered it to the Governor of New Amsterdam to protect the city from the Swedes, but instead he arrested the Jews and put the golem up for auction. It sat in a Brooklyn root cellar for nearly three hundred years until I found it. Soon, the two of you will fulfill the purpose of watching over this city—my city—at my bidding. You will inhabit a body with quite a pedigree, I'd say. Personally, I would rather have the real thing." Samson's cheeks reddened and he approached the men, gazing away from their eyes. "When I have your body Mr. Stein, I'll be as strong as my golem, but still human enough to enjoy it. And when I have your face, Mr. ben Muhammad, I will control your wealth. How much are you worth? Millions? Billions? As I said, it is a lucky coincidence for me that you have both decided to show up just as I'm begining my ritual." Samson began chanting in a tongue neither of the men would have recognized by name. He bent himself and fanned the flames with his hands as he alternated between prayer and silence in cyclic rhythms. On one cycle, he looked up and addressed the two men. "Have you ever heard the tale of Gilgamesh, who would split a baby in half so that two fighting women could share motherhood?"

"Isn't that the story of Solomon?" Said ben Muhammad "Shut up," The corners of Samson's mouth turned slightly downward and his eyes widened. "I told you each that you would have sole power over this woman, and I never rescind a promise." "Don't kill her, please," said Paul

Samson waited for the end of the chant to reply. "Idiots, you think I would kill your girl? You two can continue your fight over her as long as you like, for all it matters to me. Killing is a sin."

The rising flames drew Paul's eyes. He could see now that they were hanging above a burning bush. "Lord God," he screamed, "please help me!" Rabbi Samson laughed. "Yahweh," he addressed the bush by God's ancient name.

"What do you require of me, great Samson?"

"Give me the wealth of the oil baron and the strength of the boxer."

"Shall I cast their souls into hell?"

"No," he said even as his body was being reshaped, "I would like you to ensoul the golem doubly."

As he spoke, the fat seemed to melt from Rabbi Samson's body, and his skin seemed to darken, become more youthful. Paul had trouble focusing on the Rabbi. The scene became a jumble of images and he could see his own body from the outside, writhing in exact complement to ben Muhammad's. He could see both of their bodies starting to dissolve into a wisp of smoke over the burning bush. Soon, the Rabbi's body stopped its distortion and solidified into a chimera of the two vanished captives. Paul could tell he was still in the room, though he had watched his own body fade out of existence along with ben Muhammad's. He tried to move stiff limbs, but they felt clunky and he seemed unable to decide which way to move. His emotions too were a jumble. They were still filled with hate for Samson and love for Holly, and hate for ben Muhammad. But there was self-loathing now as well. Ben Muhammad was present inside him.

"Yahweh, partition the golem." With the Rabbi's words, the chaotic admixture of souls ended in pain. A line burned down the center of the clay form, singeing both souls and demarking a boundary that kept ben Muhammad in the left and Paul in the right.

"Enjoy your prison, children. I'm off to dabble in real estate." The Rabbi tore down the curtains and cloaked his new body with them, leaving the apartment through the still-open doorway.

Paul tried to stand, but he only had control over his—the golem's—right leg. The left was still. "Damn it," he said out of the right side of his mouth. "You damned idiot. If it wasn't for your greed, I'd have my wife and you'd have your riches." He began punching the left side of his face, chipping greenware knuckles and cheek. The left arm tried to do the same to the right side, and the golem struggled with itself until both eyes wept in tearless despair.

Eventually, the left side of the golem's mouth said, "Stop it! Stop it, Mr. Stein."

The left arm flung itself limply to the side while the right continued to work at destroying them both, pounding deep cracks in the hand and wrist, neck and face.

"I really do love her."

"You don't know what love is," said Paul bitterly. He took a breath and put the hand to his forehead, feeling dust and bits of clay slough off underneath his fingers.

The golem sat solemnly in front of the smokeless fire.

"Help me. All I want is to hold my Holly again, even under these circumstances," said the right side of the Golem.

"What can I do? Robbed of my body and rendered in clay, what good can I be?" asked the left.

"I don't know. I can't think. I can't do anything—I can't even cry," said the right.

"Can this god of yours help?" asked the left.

Paul considered this for a moment. God wasn't supposed to be Scene of his vocabulary. He was a man of the flesh; a man of the fist.

"Of course!" Paul started to lean forward with the right side of the golem's body, and ben Muhammad followed his lead with the left. The fire rose at the movement. "Yahweh, help us. Give Holly her free will back."

"I have made a covenant to give each of you command over the girl's will. I cannot breach that covenant." "Then, please give us back our bodies."

"As I said, I cannot contravene a covenant once made. Only Rabbi Samson can give you back your bodies. Perhaps I can help you in some other way."

"If you can't restore our souls, what can you do for us?" Asked Paul.

"I must do whatever you command that is within my power."

"Then give this body the strength to overcome Rabbi Samson, Yahweh," said ben Muhammad.

The flames grew, and with them the golem's body. Over the fire, the golem took on a vitreous sheen. The surface became covered with glass scales and the bulk continued to expand. Soon, the ceiling bowed against the golem's shoulders. Then it broke and debris fell from the upper levels of the old building into the basement. The fire consumed it all, and the golem had to step away to keep from hardening into bisque. As the golem grew greater and greater, the partition grew thinner and thinner, stretching imperceptibly thin between the two souls.

* * *

It was in that fire that my story begins. Though the Rabbi had divided me in two, I had to work as one being. Oftentimes, I wish I had continued to grow and grow until my body formed a world unto itself. Alas, that was not to be. I was compelled towards the glass and steel pinnacle where my lover would be opening a money-clotted safe on an upper floor. I stumbled and fell along the way, rending ironwork and bits of pavement under my weight. By this time, my feet were the size of trolley cars, my stride the length of a city block. I walked to where the shadow of the Empire State Building pierced the daylight on the street. People fled in terror before me, but by then I didn't care. My cares were torn between love and hate briefly, when I saw the tiny Rabbi scurry for cover into one of the East Central apartment buildings. I ignored him and grasped at the Empire State Building with an enormous hand, breaking windows and bending steel. I climbed until I could look with my left eye into the office where Holly carefully removed money from the safe without emotion or haste.
"Holly," my voice was hoarse and inhuman, large as a nation, but she responded anyway. "Come here," I said.

I wrapped one arm around the side of the building so that I could lift the other and give Holly somewhere to stand. She obeyed my every word, climbing out the window into my giant clay hand. As she did so, the building started tipping and screeching. I stepped down in time to back away and watch it fall like a tree, tiny people diving hopelessly from the windows. The collapse obliterated all of the East Central apartment buildings in one long, billowing crunch. The city was alive with sirens going every which way, and maybe no one noticed when I took a deep breath and did a backstroke into the cold Atlantic with Holly—or the mindless body that bore that name—sitting comfortably on my chest.

* * *

As I write this, the waves of the Atlantic are still crashing on gently sloping shores, though the shoreline has receded and the ocean has been split into two strings of slushy salt puddles running along either side of a vast mountain range. New York city is now long gone, as are the ever changing peoples of that ghetto called Harlem. When the sun has expanded and scorched me to a frozen statue, you may find my body on the peak of a mountain that was once an island. There, I will be lying alongside a pile of ashes that were once the bones of my dear Holly. But before I retreat into my private eternity, I must complete my tale…

The golem switched hands with the petrified mangrove tree he'd been using as a writing utensil, and then started a new column of inscription. "In glory to the Prophet (blessings be upon him), I write this tale in a sincere attempt to inform the reader—should there be a reader—of the true history of my creation, if it is Allah's will that I finish before the end-times sun undoes the magic that made me or bakes my limbs into useless branches…"

The End

Author's Afterward: So, what's your personal "Land of Milt and Honey"

<<<END OF STORY / START OF NEXT>>>


TITLE: Nellibeth
(c) 2012 by Nathan McKnight
A shorter version of this story was originally published in "For the Love of Ponies", an anthology of Bronies stories, published in 2012 by Kazaa press. The only review of this story that I've seen contained the greatest compliment: "Arguably doesn't belong in this book". No shade to the bronies, and I'm thankful for the publication credit, but really this story isn't aimed at bronies in particular. I hope they liked it anyway! The full review of the story is in the afterward below. The full story is available on Kindle. 








	I could tell Poula was hiding something. I knew that look, that guarded play-it-straight look. I'd practiced for hours in front of the mirror trying to erase that same look from my own face. 
	“So, you grew up right here on a cattle conservatory?” She gestured with hands covered in dirt. 
“More or less,” I said. “Gene's my uncle--we've always just called him him Unc. I think I was in the third grade when I moved in with him. Been ranching ever since.” I jammed the trowel into the ground and let my haunches settle into the dirt. 
“I like it,” she said. “I bet the woods are beautiful in the fall.” 
She had put a daisy behind her ear. The flower was a strange intruder against her pale-olive cheeks and coaly eyeliner. 
“So, you're going to buy it?” I asked.
“I wouldn't have spent the last hour helping you put in a flower bed if I wasn't going to stay and enjoy the flowers.” She made a sort of snurf noise that must've been a laugh. 
“You got a deal worked out with Unc?” She made a noncommittal head-shrug. Of course not, I thought. Unc would get my go-ahead before he did anything firm. “You don't much look like a rancher,” I said. 
Snurf. “Well. I raise animals sort of.” She stopped as if that were a satisfactory answer and looked down at her hands, awkwardly packing in a row of phloxes. 
I wanted to like her, but something triggered my suspicion reflex. I didn't think she was a sinkfarm scout--we'd had a handful of those come through already, wanting to strip the land, put in carbon repositories. She didn't seem right, but she didn't seem that sort of wrong. Their shiftiness usually had a missionary quality about it, trying too hard to make a good impression on everybody, not content with taking your land, they had to convince you of their holy mission too. Hers was much more of a skittish shiftiness.
“Look,” I said. “Me and Unc, we just want to see the ranch in good hands. While we've been running the conservatory here, we've always sheparded the land carefully. We let the wild plants and animals do their thing and we make sure the pastures and gardens don't dick up the wilderness too much. So, tell me what you've got planned and I'll tell you if we can do business. Unc'll back me up.”
She brushed the loamy soil off her hands and frowned. “Fair enough,” she said. And after another of her interminable pauses, she said, “Do you know much about...I guess you'd call it...meattech?”
I laughed out loud. “You've got to be kidding,” I said. 
“Look, if you don't want to--” But I cut her off.
“No, no. It's okay. Why didn't you just say so? Here I was thinking you were some kind of prospector.”
“Me? A prospector? Hardly.” She wiped her face with her wrist, leaving a smudge along her graceful jaw. “So...you're okay with me doing tissue engineering here?”
“Okay? Hell, we've been synthies for years. I would've showed you the vats in the shed, except that tends to scare away some buyers.”
“You do tissue engineering out here?”
“You can't not. I mean, we keep it to a minimum, but it's practically illegal to sell truly organic meats anymore.”
	“And that doesn't bother you?” she asked with raised eyebrows.
“Mmmm--ten years ago, you might've gotten some argument from me, back when the regulations were first creeping in. It was a hard transition, especially for old-timers like Unc. But anymore, hardly a day goes by we're not taking core samples so we can tweak the mix.”
“Core samples? That's you call it?” She snurfed herself into a full-snort laugh. “Cow core samples? Ha! You're terrible.” 
She shook her head and scrunched her nose like I'd made a horrible pun, but a full-face smile split her lips and her dark eyes skewered me. 
I spread my hands and did a little head bow. “Glad to be of some amusement.” 
“So...you'll put in a good word for me with your uncle?”
“We'll see,” I said with a wink. 
“Well. Thank you. I'm glad we got that cleared up, but unfortunately it's about time for me to head back home,” she said. “Let me give you my card. I teach classes at Nutrielle. I'm not sure how applicable it would be for a ranch lab, or even if you want to keep up with tissue work after you sell the place, but if you're interested...”
“Moi?” I said, taking the card. 
After she rinsed her hands under the brittle old garden hose, she said, “I don't suppose I could bother you for a lift to the train station?” 
#
On the way back to the ranch, I kept thinking back to the smiles she gave me as we planted together. Poula was young, no more than about twenty, and maybe I was reading her wrong, but I swear there was something between us.
When I pulled back up the gravel drive, I saw the flowerbed she'd helped me finish. The front, where Poula had worked was precise, meticulous. In the back of the bed, along the barn wall, her neat ranks of peonies gave way to my free-form clots of lambs' ears and wildflowers. 
Unc was sitting on the astroturfed front steps, sipping soda from a juice glass. 
“Nice work,” said Unc, waving the glass at the flowers. “Although, your half's a little messy.”
	“Ain't that the truth,” I said. “You're looking pretty rough yourself, old man.”
“That's 'cause I'm old. What's your excuse?”
I flipped the John Deere cap off his head when I walked in the door and chuckled as I left him cursing in the twilight. 
When I got inside, I examined the card she'd given me. It was a classy translucent white with a repeating threedi of a butter knife slowly slicing through a steaming fillet mignon. It read:  
	Poula E. Santiban
Nutritional Tissue Consultant
Serving the Five Burroughs of New York
Teladdress: @SantaPoula2033
Scribbled on the back, in pen that made the animation fizzle and crack:
@NutrielleLLC
#
	I left a message in Nutrielle's callbox as soon as I got inside, but my phone immediately chirped back an auto-reply telling me all the classes were filled. 
When Unc came in, clutching the hat, I wiped the frown off my face and asked, “How'd it go?” 
He made a humorless snort, and said “Well, it seemed like they might take the financial hardship claim seriously until her attourney brought up the fact that I'm selling the ranch. He'd printed off a bunch of those classifieds we took out. The judge said he'd consider the request again in six months.” 
“That's good news,” I said, trying to cheer him up. “All we have to do is not make the sale, then I'm sure he'll grant us an exemption and we can put it all behind us.” 
“Maybe,” he said, shaking his head. “But that's not the point. Y--we owe her,” he said, the frustration in his voice apparent. He should have said, almost did say: You owe her. 
I couldn't say anything to that, so I just said, “I'm going for a ride.”
“It'll be dark soon, Mac. Come on. What are you thinking?” 
“I know, I know. But that's just it. I have to do some thinking,” I said.
“You ain't been drinkin, have you?”
“Never touch the stuff,” I said.
“Uh-huh,” he said. “Go ahead then. I can't stop you.”
#
The spring air had cooled off, and I could see my breath and hers as I saddled and mounted old Nellibeth. I trotted her out to the tree-edge. 
Nellibeth knew the drill. She brayed and launched herself into the woods, arrowing between the trunks of the dewy oaks. Moon-stippled litter crunched underhoof as she deftly cantered over roots and rocks. She moved quick, and I had to hold my body hunched like a motorcycle racer to keep from getting hit by low-hanging vines. I didn't need to see where we were going. That was Nellibeth's job.
When Unc had first brought this beautiful brindled mare home from a 4-H meeting, I'd wanted to name her Nelly--after Nelly Square, who sang the theme to Adirondack & Quentin, my favorite twodi kidstoon at the time. Unfortunately, it happened to be the one weekend a month when my step-cousin, Rayne was visiting, and Rayne had thrown a hissy. For some reason, she'd wanted to call the horse Beth, and the two of us ended up in a muddy brawl over which one of us was Unc's real kid, even though we knew damn well he wasn't really either of our dads. 
Unc had split the proverbial baby and christened our new mare Nellibeth. 
The moonlight broke wide through the trees when we came upon the creek. The wild in Nellibeth broke out, as it always did. I loostened her reins and held tight to the gullet as she galloped through shallow water. The splashes gleamed, sparkling in the corners of my wind-watering eyes.
Nellibeth slowed after a time and grazed in a marshy sandbar. When I rode at night, I usually allowed my mind to wander where it would, and usually I allowed Nellibeth to lead the way. But tonight, it happened that Nellibeth was wandering away from the creek, starting to descend one of the numerous slopes towards The Way I Didn't Ever Want To Go Again; the path where I had my drunk horseback riding accident so long ago. It sounds funny to say it that way, but it's a serious matter. Serious as a dead cousin, sister, lover. 
I yanked the reins, and Nellibeth slowed her descent down the Oak Hill path until she was moving slow enough that I could turn her around. 
And as far as my mind was concerned, I didn't want to think about anything but the little animated card I'd left on my dresser, and the way Poula's dark eyes had pinned mine when she'd given it to me.  
#
As usual, Unc was sitting over the old threedi set when I got up, still in his longjohns. There was a Steelers game on, and he pounded his knee with the heel of his hand, sipping his a Budweiser straight from the pouch and yelling at the little players who faded in and out as they dashed across the worn metal screen. 
“A little early for a beer, don'tcha think?” I said.
	“You're one to talk.” 
	He leered at me as I plodded into the kitchen to make a pot of coffee. When I went to fill up the coffee machine's reservoir I saw that, out of habit, I'd left my phone on the sink window sill. Poor thing chirped plaintively because it wasn't getting enough sunlight there this late in the spring and the battery was about dead. 
I scrolled through my messages as the pot was brewing. Calls about the ranch had picked up. Most of the teleddresses were unlisted, probably scouts, so I reflexively deleted them. I didn't want to see the place go into a sinkfarmer's hands. A couple of the messages were private numbers, and I almost started listening to them just as the coffee maker finished dripping. 
Fuck it, I thought. 
“I don't want any y'all to have it,” I muttered and emptied my callbox, then poured myself a mug of coffee and had a breakfast of a couple spicy dill pickle spears and a week-old powdered donut. Afterwards, I went out to the shed. 
Our setup was just a little cubby with a fume hood and fan. There was a twodi screen sprayed onto the dusty wood, hooked up  to a cracked glass pane for magnifying, but we never used them anymore. Marred by white scum, a small window afforded a view of “the Fleet,” as Unc called our herd, studding the hill behind the horse stable. We used to corral them up over the hill to the Knudsen's place for slaughter but Dale closed shop a couple years back and now we had to pay to truck them out to Oneida. I pulled the long syringe tips out of the dewar flask, noticing I'd need to head into town to top off the nitrogen soon.
Me and Unc didn't do much as far as tissue engineering was concerned, just seeding a few core samples with engineered cell lines and innoculating the cattle a couple times a year to keep their meat uniform and lean. I had only once made a batch of fully synth steaks. 
When he was gearing up for the switchover, Unc had first bought the wrong starter kit and we had a bunch of unused armatures and nutrient mixes laying around for a while. Eventually, I'd figured I'd give it a shot and see how they'd turn out. After a week's work, I'd had a gross of tenderloins ready for slicing. Unfortunately, they didn't turn out picture perfect like the ones stamped out by the millions in the industrial labs. We froze a couple to eat ourselves, and slapped the rest with an Artisinal label. They didn't sell that well, but at least it hadn't been a total loss. I snapped the cores onto the agar-loaded syringes and put them in the incubator. Tomorrow was the big day. 
A couple of the town boys who had helped us in the past were coming over to give us a hand wrangling the cattle, but we'd probably still have to drive over to the hardware store and pick up some day labor. It would be a good opportunity to top of the nitrogen.
I was lugging the dewar onto the bed of my pickup when Unc came running out, waving something in the air. 
“Mac!” he shouted. “Get over here. Don't make your old man run.” 
This was as excited as I'd seen him in years. 
I figured the Steelers must've won, but he panted, “It's that girl. Says she's been trying to get a hold a you all day. You shadowed your phone again, dint'ya? Anyway, she wants'ta make an offer but said she had to talk to you first.” He handed me the phone he'd been waving wildly, and jabbed a finger towards me, saying “You make sure this doesn't fall through. This is your recompense young man.” 
Poula? Trying to get a hold of me? I sat the dewar down and took the phone, shaking. I hadn't realized until that moment how much of an impression she'd made on me. I'd barely talked to a girl since Rayne passed away.
“Hi, MacElray?” she said. “So, I was glad to see you called about the classes. Your name's on my wait list for next semester, but I wanted to tell you...there's an opening in a special training class I'm running here for employees of one of our subsidiaries, and I figured since you already had some experience, you'd fit right in. They're all from different branches and they're all relative new-hires, so you could even audit the class for free and nobody would be the wiser.” 
“Um,” I was a little foggy-headed and I wasn't sure how to reply. 
“Sure, I guess. Yeah.” I said.
“Great. Show up at the Nutrielle building at eight sharp. You know where it is?”
“Wait, tomorrow?”
“Yeah, sorry. I forgot. That's the only catch. But this could be a really good thing for you. It might even be a foot in the door here or at BetaSynth, or wherever.”
“I wish I could, but I can't make it into the city tomorrow. We've got a lot of work lined up on the ranch,” I said.
“Oh, okay.” She sounded disappointed. “Well...think about it. If you show up, you're in. If not... Maybe some other time, I guess.” 
There was a long silence, then we both tried to speak at once. “Um...I was going to ask you to join me for dinner afterwards.”
“Wow,” I said, astonished. 
“Don't answer now. Just think about it, okay?” 
“Yeah,” I said. “Okay.”
	Another long pause, then I said, “Unc says you wanted to make an offer on the ranch?”
	“Oh, right. I, um...” And just then, the usual hesitation which I'd almost started to appreciate in her voice turned a corner and that skittish shiftiness came back. 
	She said, “I'm not a hundred percent certain, but I, I think I can offer seven-fifty, with maybe twenty percent down. The thing is, I've got some...research and they're going to buy it and usually they pay so much and they really like my work and I've got a meeting lined up and...well, I should know in the next couple days.”
	“Alright,” I said, again at a loss for words. 
“We'll talk about it tomorrow,” she said. “Either on the phone or, I hope, over dinner.”
#
?	Unc threw a fit when I told him I wanted to go into the city while he was here inoculating the Fleet. 
“What do you mean you're going to the city?” He said. “You can't leave me here to run the inoculation alone. I don't care how many extra hands we hire, I can't lead them all on my own. We don't keep the Fleet in shape, we don't make our money, we don't fix up the property, we don't sell the property. You don't get it. It's important, son.”
“That's why I'm doing it, Unc. That's why I'm going. Because she wants to buy the farm and she wants to talk about it over dinner,” I yelled. “Don't make it about me and you. So yeah, I'm going.” 
“Bull. Shit. What kind of dinner lasts all day long, MacElray? This ain't about the ranch, or she'da made a for real offer already. And this sure in hell ain't about you and me, Mac. This is about you. Just you. So, fine. Fine! Go.” 
I pushed over the dewar and let its contents sizzle out, sending ice-cold steam across the gravel.
“I'm takin the fuckin horse out,” I said.
#
This time, me and Nellibeth rode all the way down the creek to Oak Hill path and kept going. It was still early, and though my memories were crisp, the midday sun had the effect of washing out some of the pain. During the day, it was just a thing that had happened. At night...
I shook it off. I remembered the late steed Peanut Butter, after me and Rayne and Lizzie had ridden through the creek. The horses shaking off in the clearing here, sending water over the three of us. 
Me and Rayne used to sneak Budweisers out of Unc's fridge in the barn whenever she visited. Usually, the two of us got drunk and took turns daring one another to climb on the roof of the stable or run buck naked through the cow pasture. But Lizzie was younger, and when we offered her a Bud, she must've been afraid to touch the alcohol stash of a man who was, after all, a stranger to her. Rayne followed her friend's lead and I'd just figured more for me.
Night riding is always a blast, but the alcohol had raised the lid for me. It was ecstatic. When we broke out into the starlit creek, with stars made of water flying by on both sides, and real stars above reflecting in the water below...
It was like flying through space. 
After the horses shook off, we dismounted to wipe our faces with our hands and some kind of drunk humor made me want to fingerpaint with mud all over Nellibeth's flank—I think I'd been thinking something like, see if you can shake this off. Well, she shook alright. 
Lizzie got down, ducked right under Nellibeth. Stupid. Nellibeth sat right down on top of her, and although I heard the popping crack that might have been her collar bone, I hadn't recognized it for what it was, and I couldn't help but burst into laughter. Rayne had tried to pull her friend out, but Nellibeth had other plans. She rolled over, smashing Lizzie's face into the mud and knocking Rayne onto the rocks, unconscious. The mare had wiggled around on her back until she was satisfied she'd gotten all the mud off, then stood and trampled Lizzie as she ran off into the darkness.
Even in my drunken stupor, I knew better than to move Lizzie. She'd been broken up too bad. I didn't think to check if she was breathing. I just lugged Rayne onto Peanut Butter and rode back to the ranch as fast as I dared.
Me and Unc had left Rayne on the porch and called an ambulance, then we'd gone off on foot to get Lizzie. 
After I don't know how long hiking--all the while Unc muttering about how he should've put a goddamn lock on that goddamn refrigerator--we found the spot and when we shined a flashlight in Lizzie's face, we saw her mouth and nose jam-packed with mud. “Ever ride a horse through the forest at night?” I asked her. “It's amazing. Terrifying. Like flying through space.”
“I never rode a horse,” she said. “But I will I hope. It sounds dangerous.”
I told her about Rayne and Lizzie, reluctantly at first, but once the words came, they seemed to drain my soul, pouring out of me in a feverish whisper, my voice turning gravelly, like Unc's. I told her everything except the one detail I couldn't bear to admit--that I had been drunk. 
I couldn't tell you why I did it, I should have just said I didn't want to talk about it, but when it came to the fatal moment, I hesitated for a long moment, too long.
Then I said: “Rayne must've been drinking; she started rubbing mud on the horse, and I guess Nellibeth was trying to rub it off when she rolled over on top of Lizzie.”
	#
In the morning, I opened my eyes to diffuse morning light shining through the window at the head of Poula's bed. The glass'd steamed up in the night and drips of water dotted the window sill. I tossed aside damp bedclothes and put my bare feet down on dewy carpeting. No wonder it smelled like she had a dog. There must've been mildew creeping into every crack and crevice in the place. 
	Brushing aside annoyance, I tiptoed to the bathroom, which I found at the end of a short hall by the front door. There was another door, which might've been a closet, covered with a blue tarp that seemed to breath, as though somewhere behind there, it was open to the outside. 
	I used the pot, and on the way back to bed, discovered the source of the apartment's pervading moisture. At the foot of the bed, a humidifier had been left running all night. I couldn't find the switch, so I unplugged the thing and, deciding I'd slept long enough, I started poking around. Poula was still snoring lightly under pink paisly covers. There wasn't much to it, just a tiny bedroom adjoined to an even tinier kitchen. 
	My curiosity took a back seat to my zombie drive for coffee, and soon I was poking around the kitchen. Not even instant? I thought. The cabinets were empty and, aside from a half-full five pound bag of agar powder, so was the fridge. 
	Poula still hadn't roused. That's why I'll never care for city life, I thought. Wreaks havoc with your body's schedule. 	
	Coming up empty on the coffee front, I reluctantly went back to bed, trying not to disturb Poula's sleep. Now that I'd noticed the doggy smell, I was having a hard time not noticing it. I laid awake for what seemed like hours, tracing patterns of water stains on the ceiling with my eyes. 
	I must've dozed off eventually because before I knew it, I was roused again by a tap on the window. I half-dreamed the sound was the cracking of collarbone against stone, and just as fast as I was out, I jerked wide awake. I held frozen, senses buzzing alert, heart trying to punch its way out of my chest. 
	“What the hell,” mumbled Poula, sleepylipped.
	“Shhhh...” I said, wiping a clear swath across the cold, wet pane. 
	The park was empty, except for one figure, on the sidewalk directly across the street, and even though he was distorted by the veins of water on the glass, I recognized bathroom boy, with his big glasses and twisted legs. His upraised arm dropped and I figure he must've seen me, because he turned and started walking away and I thought, Kid, you better walk away.
	I muttered, almost inaudibly, “I'm not even fuckin joking,” and I wanted to disassemble that brat.
	“What is it?” Poula whispered. 
	“Nothing,” I said. “It's nothing.” 
	But my fists were working open and shut, wondering if I'd really scared him off. I slid out of bed and into my now-damp clothes as quickly and quietly as I could. I let myself out and steeled my nerves for a confrontation.
	Downstairs, I glanced back and forth, crossed the street and looked across the park. The place was empty but for a young girl exercizing an enormous mastiff in the dog run. I loostened my fists, deflated and turned back towards the apartment.
	Sure enough, bathroom boy was there, huddled in a darkened doorway a couple doors down from Poula's place. I sprinted across the street, and grabbed him by the shoulder. 
	He was obviously startled, and he yammered until I said, “Listen to me.”
	He wore a loose jogging suit, and I had to twist the kevlon jacket around my hand to hold him still.
	I told him, “Look,” I said, “I get it. I do. Nobody likes to lose a girl. I understand that better than anybody, but you need to stop this. You need to leave her alone and let her make her own decisions. And if you can't do that on your own...“
	He hesitated a second. The dull eyes behind those glasses searched my face, then he straightened his demeanor and said, “Get your hands off of me, boy. I'm your boss's boss's boss, so ” 
	His voice wasn't a kid's voice. He growled with as much gravity I'd seen in a man his age, sounded like Unc more than anything.
	I thought it'd be better not to tell him I wasn't one of his employees, so I just said, “I don't care who you are. If you wanna get in her good graces, you're goin about it all wrong way.”
	He shook his head, not vigorously, in defiance--but in what seemed like pity. 
	“You should thank me,” he said. “Look at me. Poula made me like this.” 
	He waved his hand over his body, his hunched back, his bowed legs, his wonky blue eyes. 
	“She changed me,” he said. “And I tried to change for her. I did change for her. And then she left me. So go ahead, go back to her and just wait. She'll leave you like she left me. The only difference is, she doesn't need you. She needs me.”
	The sudden change in this little boy--this man--made me take a step back, literally. I didn't know what to say, hadn't even quite absorbed all he'd said. 
	He sneered and backed away, and I heard Poula's voice, turned, and he was gone. 
	“Mackle Ray,” I heard. “Mackle Ray! What are you doing out here?” 
	She was wrapped in a paisly bedsheet toga, and she leaned out the glass door as far as she could without moving her feet from the tile inside. I turned, glancing once more quickly around, then followed her back up the stairs and into the apartment. I winced at the bitter-tart odor, but she dropped her wrapping and pressed her nakedness against me.
	“Does that Doug guy throw rocks at your window often?” I asked. 
	“Oh Christ,” she said, and then she started what sounded like an apology
	I interjected: “I don't think he'll be bothering you again.”
	“You're so sweet,” she said, rubbing a her head against my neck and running a hand through my hair. “...and muscular.”
	“Workin on the ranch ain't all bad, huh?” 
	She giggled and we rushed back to bed.
	#
	“Well, I have to say--that was the best surprise you could have given me,” I said, all thought of Doug long behind me.	
“Oh,” she said, snurfing, “but that wasn't even the surprise.”
I raised my eyebrows and she went on: “I wasn't kidding when I said I wanted to show you the project I've been working on.”
“Come on,” she said, bouncing on the bed. “Let me show you my special laboratory.”
We got out of bed, both of us still naked, and walked across the wet carpet towards the bathroom, down the hall. 
I was confused, and my face must've showed it, because she put her finger on my lips as if to say shhhhh, then smiled. “It's okay,” she said.
She pulled back the blue tarp from the door in the hallway and a blast of sharp, cheesy odor nearly gagged me.
“Sorry about the smell,” she said. “I hardly notice it anymore. I just don't have proper ventilation here.”
The door opened onto a walk-in closet filled with...well, I couldn't tell at first what I was looking at. The first thing I identified was another little dome-shaped humidifier. The rest was a wet, tangled mess.
She bent and lifted a tile covered in a yellowy gel that took me a second to recognize as agar. She snapped it neatly into place on the wall, saying “Four hundred pounds of agar doesn't go as far as you might think.”
The whole closet glistened urea-yellow, and there was big globs of meat--whole, flexing muscles fastened down with straight pins and stretched out with zip ties. There was a pair of lungs, billowing open and closed. Everything was netted in a fine mesh of veins and a delicate tracework of white lace that must've been nerves. All of it writhed and pulsed, and it slowly dawned on me what I was looking at. 
Succored by the nutrient mixture, the wall was a whole living creature, splayed out like a diagram. 
Was this... and as the thought formed in my head, Doug's words came back to me, and my skin prickled. Was this...Doug-2? I couldn't get the sound out of my mouth, my stomach heaved. 
“What, wh...” I babbled incoherently.
“You'll find out,” said Poula. “That's the surprise.”
“What is it?” I finally said.
“Think about it, you'll figure it out,” she said. 
Sprigs of tendon and sinew sparkled along the tiles, muscles flexed, lungs billowed and collapsed like ship's sail in a strong wind. She picked up a spray bottle and spritzed a huge pair of discarnate, violet eyeballs, then snurfed. 
#
I left in the afternoon, giving her an unfeeling kiss. After she'd showed me the lab, we hadn't talked. It took me a long time to let it sink in. She's building another Doug, the thought had just kept going through my mind. She'd acted coy, like my confusion was funny, and that'd just made it worse.
The ride back home was frought with nightmare visions that the daylight failed to wash away. I smacked the seat, punched it harder and harder, cursed and eventually I just cried, driving home by feel when the tears blurred my vision too much to see. 
By the time I got home, my tears had dried, leaving powdery trails down my cheeks. I realized Doug was right. She doesn't need me, I thought. She needs him. 
The thought pierced my heart like a straight pin, and I gritted my teeth to stop myself from crying again.
Unc tried to get me to talk at first, the unnatural silence of the ranch echoing between his words. Eventually, he gave up and turned on the threedi to watch a game stoically. 
I didn't call Poula back. 
#
For a few days, I tooled around the ranch house, watching threedi and draining pouches of beer. Unc put up with my sulking for almost a week before he asked me to take the dewar for a refill.
The trip to the store helped my mood a little, and when I got back, I went to work feeding the cattle and cleaning the stable out one last time. By sunset, I was almost feeling normal and I even joined Unc for a Steeler's game. 
The phone rang that night, just as I was laying down for bed. It was Poula. I didn't answer and I almost deleted the message but, like prodding at a fresh wound, I couldn't stop myself from pushing play. 
Her voice was small. 
“I'm sorry that I upset you. I want to give you time, whatever you need. I love you, and I wish...” she trailed off. “The only reason I called is to tell you I didn't get the advance from Nutrielle. Their lawyers say they already own the patents because I worked for them when I made them. They just gave me a two thousand dollar bonus and recommended me for an honorary doctorate. It's bullshit. I don't know what I'm going to do, but I still want to buy the conservatory. I think if I talk to Doug--”
I hung up and went to bed.
#
The next morning, the full brightness of the sun was shining in my window as I tossed and turned, but I couldn't will myself to get out of bed. I sobbed into my pillow, trying to keep Unc from seeing my useless state, but he interrupted my pillow-drowned darkness with a nock on the door.
“You look bad,” he said.
I shrugged, then sat up with the sheets pulled over my shoulders.
“I just had a long conversation with Poula,” said Unc. “She was grilling me about you.”
“She's here?” I sputtered.
“Over the phone.”
I wasn't sure if I was feeling relief or disappointment, but I nodded and asked, “What did you tell her?”
“I didn't tell her nothin. We talked about the ranch, and told me she had the funds and wanted to buy it.”
“You're selling it to her?” I said with a dead flat tone.
“We'll talk about that,” he said. “The thing is...she asked about you and Rayn's accident. She told me you said Rayne was the one who'd been drinkin.”
I closed my eyes.
He went on, “It didn't occur to me that you would lie about such a thing, so I didn't even think to try to back up your little fib. I don't mean to hurt you, but I just told her what happened as best I knew it.”
I took a deep breath.
“What did she say?” I asked.
“Nothin. Not for a long time, then she said she had to go.”
Unc waited for me to say something, but when I kept the silence, he said: “I accepted her offer. It's not as much as she thought she was going to offer, but it's enough for me to buy a little house maybe. Probably not enough to pay our--your--debts.”
#
Poula came out with her lawyers to finalize the purchase with us and our lawyers, and I managed to drag myself out of bed and look presentable. 
I was surprised to see they came in three separate vehicles, with a horse trailer in tow. I wished I'd been surprised to see bathroom boy get out and stand alongside Poula.
She introduced her three lawyers, and we introduced our one. 
Finally, she said, “And you know Doug.”
“Shouldn't you say, Doug-1?” I asked, bitterness clinging to my teeth.
It couldn't have come as a surprise that I knew, but she looked confused. 
“Did you finish Doug-2 yet?” I spat.
She still shook her head, and I said, “He's obviously one of yours. Did you sew up that Frankenstein thing in your closet, or was it another failed experiment?”
Doug looked back and forth between us, then I saw him tilt his head back, forming his lips into an 'O' of recognition. 
“Stop,” he said. “No. Poula didn't make me.” 
He almost laughed--incredulous snurf, but not the adorable sound Poula made. 
“I made myself like this,” he said. “I used her techniques, but I did it myself...to look younger. I know it's wrong to blame her. I was mad. But it's my fault, not Poula's.”
Poula stood agape.
“You thought I made Doug?” she said. “Jesus, Mac. Oh my god, no, no, no...”
The herd of lawyers stood by like they were trying not to interfere with a barroom brawl.
“Then what was that monster in your closet,” I said.
“I was going to start with a cow, but you inspired me. It was supposed to be a surprise, like I said,” she said. 
She tapped Doug on the shoulder, pointed over her own shoulder. He nodded and went around back of the trailer. 
“She won't be perfect,” said Poula. “There aren't vats big enough, so I had to make her the old way. But at least Nutrielle--” she gave a sour glance to the lawyers. “Nutrielle is going to set up a new lab, right here. I'll be able to put my techniques into production.”
Doug backed a horse out of the trailer, walked it around, awkwardly. He obviously had never handled a horse before, but they made a good pair, him with his glasses and bent back, and the horse...well, she was beautiful. Her ribcage was a little wide, and her ears were too perfect. But her eyes were beautiful violet. 
Poula said, “I was going to name her Nellibeth-2.”
	# 
“So,” I said, “that's how I got into edible tissue engineering. The judge ended up awarding Lizzie's family almost twice what Doug Lenzler bought the ranch for. I can't let my uncle pay that on his own, and that's why I'm looking for work. I see her on the threedi a lot, so I guess Poula must be doing well.”
I leaned back in the reclining chair and spread my hands in a there-you-have-it gesture.
	The guy behind the desk looked slightly agog, and when he regained his composure, he said “I appreciate your honesty, MacElray. We'll message you in a few days to let you know our decision.”
As I stood, he added, “I can't begin to understand how you must feel about her, but having Dr. Santiban's name on your resume puts you way ahead of the crowd. Good luck.”



Afterward: Review by Fred Patton of Flayrah.com
(Excerpted from review of "Bronies: For the love of ponies", edited by L. Lambert Lawson)

“Nellibeth” by Nathan McKnight, the final story in the anthology, is both the longest and the only one about a normal, non-magical horse. In fact Nelibeth, an old mare, is barely a supporting character. In the slightly far future, “Uncle Gene”, the elderly owner of a livestock farm in New York state, is relying on his young nephew MacElray Dresch to advise him whether to sell out to Nutrielle LLC, a large corporation engaged in nutritional tissue experimentation. Mac is wooed in more ways than one by Poula Santiban, Nutrielle’s charismatic “Nutritional Tissue Consultant”; a combination of a head project scientist and the company’s leading recruiter of research staff.

Poula both wants to add Unk’s farm to Nutrielle’s holdings, and to add Mac to the new class of promising trainees. She gets close enough to Mac to show him some of her private research into experimental tissue growth, which goes beyond developing new edible meats into creating new life forms. Mac has been looking forward to developing new foods for a hungry world, but playing god is something else again. Are you wondering where Nellibeth comes into this story? Don’t worry; she plays a very small but very important part. “Nellibeth” arguably does not belong with the fourteen prior stories, but it is a very good science fiction story and I thank Bronies for making it available to us.
