#
 
[BACKGROUND ON THE CONFLICT IN SAKHA]
 
The conflict in Sakha is driven largely by environmental and technological changes which are reshaping Siberia’s biogeography and demography. Genetically engineered crops, especially those featuring more efficient chlorophyl allows a dramatic increase in agricultural products, and an accompanying population boom. This creates a shift in the power dynamic in which local and regional governments whose newfound economic importance feel they have the muscle to push for greater autonomy. This plays into the ethic tensions that simmered under the surface for centuries, particularly resentment at the Russification which has proceeded apace since the 18th century. While the actual extermination of the colonial era subsided, the waves of Russian colonists from the west and the cultural assimilation of remaining ethnic minorities led to near extinction of many local cultures.
 
Usually referred to as “The Magadan Conflict” after the city in which hostilities first broke out, even though the stakes of the conflict primarily involve the independence of the neighboring Republic of Sakha. The name of the territory is in dispute. Under Russian authority, it’s been referred to variously as the Republic of Sakha, or the Autonomous Republic of Sakha. However, the independence fighters refer to the area as Yakutia. There have long been rumblings for independence from the indigenous Sakha/Yakut people, but it wasn’t until the ethnic Russians, who dominate the area, decided they want independence from Moscow that the conflict really boiled over. These Sakha Russians take on the mantle of Yakut independence in their propaganda and  but generally treat the yakut people as a political soccer ball. Thus the sides, broadly speaking, are: Moscow-loyalist Russians vs Pro-independence Sakha Russians. Ironically, most Yakuts are now against independence. Yakut ethnic extremism was once a rare, very fringe phenomenon, but it was very violent when it did happen. However, in the decade before the Sakha rebellion, the Yakuts were radicalized by neighboring Buryats whose own independence movement flared on and off for years before burning out with no resolution due to sheer exhaustion of forces. Despite promises to the contrary, Yakuts generally fear the Sakha Russians will gain even more power in an independent state. However, some Yakuts, especially young men, do side with the independence movement and form a large chunk of the fighters. Many of them realize they’ve gotten in over their heads. Various other Siberian ethnic minorities have mostly tried to stay neutral, although many individuals may side with one group or another. These include the (thoroughly russified) Itelmen, who tend to side with Moscow, the Evens who mostly side with the Yakuts, and the Koryaks/Chukchis who have largely tried to keep out of the fray.
 
Beyond the main conflict, a variety of other players have been involved, and the independence movement is only the nexus of a continent-wide political and social upheaval.
 
The breakdown of infrastructure in Sakha and surrounding areas has provided an opening for rival powers in the broader region. While the far eastern regions of Siberia remain nominally loyal to Moscow, the conflict zones have effectively cut them off from western Russia. In this power vacuum, Chinese interests have prevailed. The Chinese (PRC) government has maintained official neutrality, but haven’t been entirely covert about encouraging Chinese nationals to take advantage of trade opportunities in the now cut-off areas. They call these policies “emergency trade rules” and assert they are only in place for humanitarian reasons. In practice, however, the policies seem to be an effort to undermine Russian authority and assure Chinese domination in the post-conflict world, no matter how the conflict turns out. The reality on the ground is that the vast majority of “Chinese trade envoys” (ostensibly meaning private merchants encouraged to open up trade into Russian territories) are, in fact, members of organized crime groups whose most obvious activity—“hostess clubs” (brothels)—are largely fronts for moving resources and stolen goods out of Russian territory. Diamonds are the most obvious example, but everything from various mineral ores to jewelry and silverware are being extracted in a virtual torrent of goods. Meanwhile, on the way into the region are arms, drugs, armies of sex workers, enormous gambling establishments, and liquor (most notoriously knock-off tar/kymys (aka airag/ayran/kumis)—fermented mare’s milk favored by Yakuts, but often mass-produced with cow’s milk—laced with formaldehyde, heavy metals, and who knows what else). Along with the crime, an increasingly large transfer of population from China is happening as roadside stands become permanent fixtures, and up-and-coming Triad leaders take over small villages with their newfound economic muscle. In the countryside located well away from the hottest conflict zones, it’s not an uncommon occurrence to see parades of SUVs celebrating a young Chinese mobster’s wedding to an even younger Siberian bride, rolling past brand new McMansions with freshly-filled in ground pools.
 
In contrast to the Chinese interest in solidifying central Asian hegemony, the Japanese government maintains a truly hands-off approach. But not to be outdone, Japanese organized crime has taken a keen interest in the area. Centering on Vladivostok, the Triads have set up a similar array of grey- and black-market activities. Since their interest in eastern Siberia is really just a cover for involvement further west in Sakha, they don’t really care about the Triads so long as they keep their activity restricted to the eastern coastal area. Thus, the Triads have had free reign in a narrow strip extending from Amurskiy Bay to the coast across from Sakhalin island, rarely venturing farther inland than the Republic of Amur. A visitor could be forgiven for believing that the Yakuza now ruled Vladivostok outright. An additional wrinkle has emerged as the conflict drags on. Chinese Triads have ceased to be satisfied with restricting their own activities to the conflict zone proper, and have expanded southwards into Mongolia, along the main contraband thoroughfares, and east towards Vladivostok. Recently, it has become clear that there is a simmering turf war between the Chinese and Japanese gangs.
 
The interests of organized crime generally align with each other, leading to competition. Chinese Triads want some order because they rely upon an effective infrastructure to move massive amounts of contraband, but they thrive under diminished rule of law. Similarly, Japanese Yakuza like the relative order of eastern Kamchatka, but need the Triads to remain occupied in the conflict zones. The long term goal of the Triads—and perhaps the Yakuza—is to have the the Sakha government firmly in their pockets, regardless of which side wins.
 
NGOs have had a limited humanitarian impact because the Chinese government has argued their own efforts at encouraging trade are sufficient. To their credit, they have actually underwritten many of the itinerant merchants who supply most staples and durable goods. Whether this underwriting actually translates into lower prices is a matter of widespread debate. Of course, the PRC’s ulterior motives are fairly apparent. Instead, NGOs have focused on conflict resolution and communication support.
 
Another major player is the Almazy-Rossii Sakha Diamond Company (ARSDC), which has vowed strict neutrality. Along with humanitarian NGOs, Almazy-Rossii is among the few entities who have official dealings with both the Moscow authorities and the Sakha provisional government. ARSDC is trying to consolidate its monopoly, but management conflicts have left the company teetering on the edge of a breakup. They’ve been determined to continue production, and they hire partisans from both sides (since the outbreak of open conflict, their near-poverty wages are considered very good). Their operations are the closest thing to a neutral zone in the region. “ARSDC” is jokingly referred to among the NGO crowd as “Arse-Dick”. The Mirna Diamond Mine is the company’s primary regional home base.
 
The conflict has also seen a great deal of demographic shift as populations have fled the area, while other populations have streamed in. Sakha has become increasingly Yakut in demographics as Moscow-loyal ethnic Russians have fled westward. Ironically, the remaining Sakha Russians have tightened their grip on power in the Republic as the de facto government has made greater and greater wartime concessions.
 
Interestingly, there has been a dramatic increase in Koryak population. This started before the conflict, when warming-driven expanded grazing lands led to a resurgence in traditional pastoralism. More and more urban Koryaks sought to return to the traditional lifestyle, often mortgaging houses and pawning valuables to purchase reindeer and yaranga (yurt) materials. Coinciding with this return to the land, many young couples chose to have children, to complete the traditional lifestyle, and for the first time in living memory, the Koryak population experienced a baby boom. But the traditional ways require traditional wisdom, and few were able to transition successfully into self-sufficiency. Looking for ways to keep solvent, many turned to selling reindeer meat, reindeer kymys (fermented reindeer milk, aka airag, ayran, kumis), and crafts to tourists in urban centers. But a sudden glut in the market, combined with a decrease in tourism due to the conflict in the neighboring territories, led to a wave of bankruptcies. Many Koryaks caught up in what was to become only the first wave of bankruptcies, sold or rented their herds to other up-and-coming wannabe pastoralists. Those who didn’t, sold to banks, who turned around and re-sold. The second wave of bankruptcies hit even harder, and the now swollen ranks of neo-pastoralists faced a debt crisis that ended in widespread poverty, crippling two generations of Koryaks, with no end in sight. Alcoholism, suicide (and sometimes familicide), and crime skyrocketed. Though they had a recently-strengthened tradition of fierce independence, a significant minority of Koryak men were swept into the ranks of the Sakha rebels. Others, sensing more opportunity with the wealthy regime in the west joined Moscow-loyalist fighters, leading to brother-against-brother warfare where both brothers are proxies for outside forces.
 
The Koryak language had been nearing extinction in the 20th century. Most individuals spoke Russian and the one-time linga franca of the region, the closely related Chukchi language, even at home. However, the resurgence in neo-traditional culture led to a generation of Koryak monolinguals. This linguistic insularism further exacerbates the economic and social problems that now plague the Koryak people.
 
PRO-INDEPENDENCE: Most Sakha Russians, some Yakuts. Unofficially, China (PRC) who hope to provide technical assistance to the booming mining industry, as well as a big cut of the profits.
ANTI-INDEPENDENCE: Some Sakha Russians, almost all other Russians, most Yakuts, Sinophobic Chinese expatriates
SEMI-ALIGNED: Chinese Triads, Japanese Yakuza
MOSTLY UNALIGNED: Itelmen, Chukchis, Koryaks, Evens, Evenks, Buryats, ARSDC
 
ARSDC
Russian Name: Алмазы-России Саха Даймонд Компания
Transliteration: Almazy-Rossii Sacha Diamond Kompaniya
Translation: “Diamonds-Russia Sakha Diamond Company”
Acronyms: АРСДК/ARSDK/ARSDC
Pejoratives: Arse-Dick, Assdick, The Cartel, The Company
 
#
 
[LUCE’S EXPERIENCE IN SAKHA]
 
Straight out of college, Luce went into the Peace Corps. Because of his interest in the Russian language, he was stationed in Chersky, in the Nizhnekolymsky district of extreme north-eastern Sakha, far from the locus of the conflict, which was largely limited to the south of the republic at the time. He had originally hoped to be stationed in Ukraine, and was disappointment with this appointment. Because the Corps is (mostly) perceived as an arm of the US government, he found he wasn’t able to develop genuine relationships with most of the Russians he came into contact with in the economic development work he’d originally been tasked with. However, a small part of his work was delivering meals to elderly people living in rural areas of Nizhnekolymsky. Some of these were the last speakers of the Yukaghir language, which Luce ended up learning. Quickly becoming disillusioned with the Peace Corps, Luce left and moved back to the states, eventually getting a job with the United Nations as a Russian translator. By the time he was 25, Luce had secured another overseas job, also in Sakha. This time, he was stationed in the capital at Yakutsk was with a UN-affiliated NGO, where he worked as a translator and conflict resolution consultant. His appointment in Yakutsk coincided with the escalation of the conflict to full-blown war. Because of the shortage of human resources and Luce’s language proficiency, he quickly transitioned into a role as a junior negotiator. He quickly learned the Yakut language, though it isn’t related to Yukaghir or Russian.
 
While initiating a negotiation at a private residence south of Yakutsk, in the rural Aldansky Raion, Luce was captured by Sakha rebels. It seemed quiet, but one knock on the door, and all hell broke loose. The only things he's good at are languages, and conflict resolution. But of course, this means…no conflict, no resolution. At the story’s opening, Luce speaks English, Spanish, Russian, Yukaghir, and Yakut. He’ll eventually learn the Spanish-based Banguingui Creole, two dialects of the Jovian language, Brazilian Portuguese, a little Mandarin, and (perhaps) the Rogue language. Luce is able to get the Yakuts and Russians to treat him well as captors because he’s able to befriend them. He thinks of this as “negotiation skills” but in reality, he’s just a personable human being. He doesn’t recognize this in himself and instead sees himself as a socially awkward loner. The rebels’ commanders find their chummy relationship with their prisoner to be unacceptable. Eventually, a monolingual Koryak is chosen to take charge of the camp where Luce is held, in large part because Luce doesn’t speak Koryak.
 
Luce was held hostage for a total of about thirteen years. His release was finally negotiated by the US government through Moscow, as an exchange for a number of Sakha rebels. He didn’t know it at the time, but he eventually pieces together that the actual details of his transport back home were arranged by his captors with the Chinese Triads. It would appear that the Koryak was double-dealing, leading the Triads into a trap for ransom. Somehow, the Triads got wind of this, and decided to liquidate the Yakuts, along with Luce and the Koryak. The Triads needed to dispose of the bodies of their liquidated operatives and captives because they're on their way to the US, and the Koryak is a known threat in the intelligence community, while Luce cannot be allowed to verify the connection between the Triads and the rebels.
 
 
[ABOUT THE KORYAK]
 
Igiklav Nutankavavovich—Luce doesn’t learn his name until later—was Luce’s nemesis since his second or third year of captivity. He was beyond Luce’s negotiation skills because they didn’t share a common language, and because the Koryak treated prisoners as cattle. The Yakut guards were young, and could be creatively cruel in an immature way. By contrast, the Koryak wasn’t cruel. He was blunt and forceful. He wouldn’t tell Luce to get up and move, he would bash him in the face and point. If Luce was too slow, he would bash him again, often until he was knocked unconscious and could be dragged. The young Yakut guards feared and respected the Koryak, and encouraged Luce to do as ordered (mainly because they didn’t like dragging an unconscious prisoner around). They also whispered rumors about the Koryak when they thought he wasn’t around. This stopped when Nutankavavovich summarily executed two of them.
 
Luce should make enemies that persist through the novel. The Koryak should be a constant presence in Luce’s mind, but there has to be a more tangible threat. Perhaps, as the FBI and CIA are investigating the Triads and their relationship to the Sakha rebels, they tip somebody off to Luce’s continued survival, and a price is put on his head. This could be a strong motivation to pursue a new career path far away from the world’s conflicts.
 
#
 
[Luce starts by attempting to synthesize his thoughts about the conflict, but quickly proceeds to pouring out his experiences in a gush of emotion.]
 
The details I explained weren’t a tenth of it, I thought.
 
“How does it end?” she asked.
 
“It doesn’t end. Either one side dominates and enforces misery on the other, or it just keeps going for centuries, slowing down and flaring up. It’s two guys beating each other to a pulp in the middle of the street. Eventually, both sides get tired, and the blows get softer and softer. Right now, they’re hitting each other pretty hard. But you can’t sustain that energy forever. Unfortunately, you can sustain it for generations. But not forever. Look at Northern Ireland, Israel, South Africa. The conflicts there are almost points on a spectrum. Hundreds of years into the conflict, and Northern Ireland healed. Hell, I remember when it became a tourist Mecca. But then you had the Catholic Knights, and it’s threatening to flare up again. Sakha will go the same way, and if we’re lucky, it won’t spill over into Irkutsk. But you see, it’s already starting to. Homogenization helps. Hell, it’s already Russians vs Russians in Sakha. The Yakuts are along for the ride. The Itelmen and Koryaks are barely pawns in the game. The ebb and flow of history eventually mixes us together until you can barely tell who’s on what sides. Homogenization may be the best way we have to beat the cycle. But tell that to Northern Ireland.”
 
"I always knew you would grow up to help others. You were always so full of emotion.”
 
“I didn’t really fit in with this family, did I?”
 
“I’m sorry for that, Luce. I really am,” she said. “My sister always said midwesterners are bottlers. I guess ‘canners’ would be more appropriate. In each of our souls is a shelf of dusty Ball jars filled with mushy pickled emotions.”
 
They were my foster parents so briefly. She was just being kind. 
 
Liquid ran down Mr. Stang's leg. He sat there with his mouth gaping, a long string of drool sticking to his collar before continuing down to moisten his trousers. 
 
"Baby, you're making a mess," said Mrs. Stang, putting a hand on his knee. "Johnny, Jonny?"
 
His mouth was like the giant pingoes of the northern steppe. Just enough of the auroralike lighting illuminated the inner edge so you could tell it was deep, but the deep interior was so shadow-veiled that you couldn't see the bottom. It might've been infinite.
 
Mrs. Stang shook her husband's leg, and he stammered to wakefulness.
 
[Mr. Stang mutters something in Bahasa Malaysia. It sounds kind of like Tagalog, a little bit of which he picked up while at sea with he Banguigui and their allies, and Luce thinks he recognizes a couple words. He interpolates some interesting meaning. But if somebody were to actually translate it, the real meaning would turn out very bland. Maybe it’s “Mari kita pergi ke tidur.” or “Saya harap anda faham sesetengah teh lebih dari kedai.”]
 
Mrs. Stang turned to me and said, out of the blue, “If long life is your goal, then the best you can hope for is to eventually be the oldest person in the world. But in achieving that goal, all you've accomplished is to watch everybody you know die, one by one. Better to hope for an eventful life. Or better yet, a meaningful life.”

“Wh…why are you doing this for me?”
“Are you going to drive yourself?
I laughed. “Maybe,” I said. “It looks fun.”
He gave me an oh, really? look, so I continued: “I’ve conducted jet-ski raids on floating islands. I taught the leader of the Bangungui how to pilot Chinese bullet sub. I think I can handle a car.”

But suddenly, his limbic system kicks in, and drives straight for the ground. Luce is practically in a trance. Ry wrestles back the wheel, shaken.]

[He spreads out a sheet, and sleeps on the wooden floor.]
 
 
 
 
Chapter 00: Marzipan
 
[Luce finally realizes there's nothing here for him and goes into a downward spiral, becoming a recluse, stim addict, and couch potato.]
 
[Eventually, Luce’s sister shows up. She has to be mentioned earlier.]
 
“God Lucy, you’re such a mess..”
I just looked at her, trying to make sense of the familiarity. No. It couldn’t be… Really? Kendra?
“Sorry—I’m so sorry; I know you’ve been through a lot; I shouldn’t have. I was just answering the message from Ramloco.”
Without thinking, I stepped forward and she tried to jerk away, but I put my arms around her and pulled her tight, tears dripping from my eyes.
I hugged her and buried my face in her hair. The sounds coming from my wide open mouth were inarticulate, like a laugh strangled by a yawn.
She pulled away and I feared she would flee. But instead, she put her hands on my face, digging her fingers into my beard and leaning her forehead against my chin.
“Luce, I don’t know where to begin,” she said into my chest.
I pushed her back a little so I could look into her eyes. My sister. I wagged my head in disbelief. My real sister.
The rims her hazel eyes welled with tears, and soon we were both crying with joy and loss and I couldn’t take it. I crumpled and bawled my eyes out on all fours on the wooden floor while she rubbed my back and shhhh-shhhh’d.
 
“Seriously though, we’ve got to get you changed.”
I looked at myself. It was surprising how dirty you could get yourself when there was food and things around to dirty yourself with. I wore my old clothes for so many twos and fives until I couldn’t remember when I’d worn anything different. But I never once spilled anything on them. Every morsel of food went into my mouth. More than anything, they had felt dusty, dead.
Kendra whistled, “They’re really trying to butter you up, aren’t they?”
I wandered into the bed room. She was surveying the closet.
“Look at all this,” she said. “I heard about your reception. Looks like someone’s going to be expecting you to attend a lot of those.”
“Oh god, please no.”
“He speaks!”
“Hmmmph.”
“I was beginning to think you’d lost the power of speech.”
I just stared at her.
“I’m sorry, I’m sorry,” she said, “I know you’ve been through a lot.”
I passed my had across the clothes hanging in the closet. There were so many fancy suits.
“Got some black tie events planned?” she asked.
“I have nothing planned.”
“Good, then we can go out for lunch,” she smiled. “If we can find something normal for you to wear. I am not going to take you to [diner in Portland] wearing a tux.”
 
We found what Kendra called “a hell of a track suit” and left the apartment despite my pathetic protests. I felt like I was wearing silk pajamas.
 
“What’s he doing here?” asked Kendra as we passed the company man.
“They left him here to watch me.”
“I’m here in case Mr. Fagan needs anything.”
“What, like his butler?”
[The guard shows interest in Kendra, asks for her number. But we don’t find out until later that he gets it.]
“I’m a certified personal companion and supporter.”
“Do you want him here?” she asked me.
I didn’t know how to respond.
“You heard the man. Your services are not required.”
“Miss?”
“Get out. Go. Shoo. Don’t you think my brother has spent enough time under lock and key? Go.”
“Well—what if he—“
“What if he what?” she said.
The man turned to me and they both stood silently.
“Luce. Do you want him to guard the door for you?”
“Nobody at my door?”
“It’s up to you, bro.”
“Nobody at my door?”
“It’s up to you.”
“Okay.” I felt unmoored, back at sea. Free.
“Alright, get out.”
“Ok, if that’s what you want,” he gathered his belongings.
“No,” I spurted. “No, stay.”
They both stared at me again.
“Stay,” I said.
 
She called a ground car and it pulled around the corner almost instantly.
“Want to listen to something?”
I sat in the car in silence as Kendra thumbed across the screen.
“You heard about the reception?” I asked finally.
“It was all over the news. You’re kind of a celebrity, you know.”

I nodded and we parted ways.

I slept long that night. I felt…safe.

[Luce blossoms fully into life as a couch potato, figuratively eating up everything from popsci takes on neuroaesthetic reichology to local news cooking segments, and literally eating up whatever fast food can be delivered fastest. He’s especially fond of his marzipan treats, and watching coverage of Joviana, eagerly looking for anything involving the Jovian language. But, there are almost never any actual recordings of Jovians speaking. To the extent that it’s discussed at all, the Jovian language is thought of as a quirky, somewhat simplistic babble, likened to birdsong or gibbons’ chittering. Luce will later discover otherwise. (But will he recognize his own discovery when it happens?)]
 
I woke up in a sweat; when I stood, shakily, I could see the wet fetal outline sticking the sheet to the floor. Had I dreamt?
I staggered to the closet and pulled a blanket around myself. The sweat in my clothes still clung coldly to my skin, tugging all the hairs of my right side.
I shuffled to the door and stuck my head out.
I looked conspiratorially at my assistant: “Can you pick me up some of those marzipan turnovers?”
He laughed and said, “Come here,” pushing his way into my apartment.
He looked around the living room, pulled a plastic contact sheet off a shiny panel next to the bed.
“See this,” he said. “Watch.”
He punched a finger at the panel and it lit up pinkish orange.
“Come here,” he said. “Watch.”
Impatiently, he showed me how tap on the room’s screen and tell it where to appear on the wall. He turned on a gesture control and gesticulated at the screen, navigating to a shopping site.
“Here, now just tell it what you want and where to get it.”
I leaned in, awkwardly.
“I want a marzipan turnover. They’re in the park.”
The guard leaned in and added “[name of park].”
Twenty minutes later,
“Have you really been locked away that long?”
“They had Can-Deliver in Toronto when I was a kid, but I never got to use it.”
Thereafter, I ordered ten turnovers at a time, and whatever else I wanted. If I didn’t know where to get something, the guard would help me search.
I spent most of my days watching television.
And what I saw was amazing.
 
At first, I watched mostly cooking shows and comedy, but soon I spent several hours every morning watching the news reports. So much had changed.
 
#
 
The room had a small kitchen, and I was busy trying to make macaroni and cheese without burning it again, when I heard a report on Jupiter come on.
It was an interview with one of the researchers assigned to liaising with the floaters.
In the background, there was a window at which another researcher sat, tapping down notes onto a screen as a…thing behind the glass writhed in pale haze.
I waved the view to zoom into the researcher’s tablet.
 
Ahrm har ahrm raha, it said.
 
“Ahrm har ahrm raha,” I said. “Ahrm har ahrm raha. Ahrm har ahrm raha.”
 
Ahrm har ahrm raha.
 
[Luce’s pronunciation will turn out to be very wrong. Eventually, Luce will develop a proper orthography, and he’ll re-parse the phrase as A’hrmhau a’hrmraugha.]
 
#
 
 
I ordered a notebook and pencils, which came that evening. In the meantime, I set up alerts for anything related to the Jovians. Especially anything related to the language.
In the first few results, I learned that the Jovians were  apparently intelligent, but had no material culture. They lived their whole lives floating in the clouds. Because of the huge pressure differential in Jupiter’s atmosphere, they were only able to interact with humans if they floated uncomfortably high while the human researchers hovered uncomfortably low. The research base handled differential pressures, but the human vehicles that plunged into the atmosphere always ran the risk of failing.
There weren’t very many good pictures of the Jovians themselves. Just tentacle-like projections coming out of foggy air behind glass. Their orangy-pink…skin, I guess — seemed hazy itself, sometimes more warm in color, sometimes verging on lime green.
The researchers interviewed gave dry lectures on biochemistry. It seemed they had a lot of biologists and chemists, but very few cultural anthropologists, linguists, or diplomats.
I had a feeling that was going to bite them in the ass eventually.
Sadly—stupidly—half of the news coverage seemed to focus on the technology of the research platform…how it maintained buoyancy, what the docking procedures were like, what kinds of food the researchers ate.
I watched a two-hour documentary called “Life in the Clouds” which barely mentioned the Jovians at all.
After watching all day, I concluded, One: Only a handful of organizations bothered to send anybody to Jupiter, or even to conduct interviews of their own. They mostly just repackaged content from the existing programs. Two: Comparative linguistics was a dying field, if it wasn’t already dead.
That should’t have surprised me. Although at the UN, I was surrounded by people who specialized in the few remaining minority languages, my college career was marked by vain appeals to cognitive theorists to help me write grants for field research to record endangered tongues. Being theorists, however, they were little prepared to help me make a case to navigate the messiness of real-world language politics.
 
#
 
By the time my notebook and pencils arrived, I had exactly three programs queued up with moments of spoken or written Jovian speech. One was the report I first saw—turned out the person transcribing the alien speech was a telecommunications technician who came in and tried to talk to the Jovians as a hobby.
The main guy in charge of the cultural studies group, who appeared in the other two clips I’d uncovered, was—you guessed it—a cognitive theorist. It seemed he spent his time holding up mirrors and concocting object-recognition tests. The Jovians in the clips played along, but all the while, they were emitting audible vocalizations. They were trying to talk to us and he was having them play jumping jacks with hula hoops.
Although the audio quality was bad, I did manage to record a  couple pages of what I thought sounded like distinct words or phrases.
Their vocal apparatus made sounds surprisingly commensurate with human vocal anatomy.

[In order to help Luce function, he’s fitted with a transdermal appliqué which allows him to access the equivalent of the internet.]
 
[A day or two after he gets his transdermal, a Triad leader appears as an apparition in his mind. It’s a long rant threatening him and encouraging him to kill himself, saying that if they catch him then suicide will seem like a gift by comparison to what they’ll do. It goes into detail, and loops when it’s done. Luce desperately tries to block it out. Eventually, he says “help”, and the transdermal enters a help mode. From the help mode, he's able to navigate a series of spectral menus. He can do all sorts of things, from changing the avatar to one of several default avatars, or custom avatars culled from his own memory. The only thing he can’t do is actually shut it off. He switches through many different avatars—his sister, Mrs. Stang, Noe. Eventually, he almost accidentally tries the Koryak. It’s strangely comforting.]
 
[Eventually, Luce figures out that he can leave the avatar in the help mode menu, and it will simply repeat variations on "How may I help you?" "May I be of service?" instead of going on the Triad leader’s rants. He switches the default language to Swahili, which he doesn’t speak, so at least he can kind of tune it out. It’s still maddening though. Now he’s stuck with an image of the Koryak saying “How may I help you?” in Swahili, in Triad leaders voice.]

Eventually, Luce sticks his head inside the apartment's safe. When angled just right, the specter of the Koryak disappears, but he has to remain standing.]

What are you doing, they ask, trying to evade bill collectors? He doesn't tell the truth, worrying that revealing what he knows about the Triads and the Koryak and others could lead to more unwanted attention. After doing some research, he figures out the idea of a Faraday cage (a mesh that acts as an electromagnetic barrier). His first attempt is to cut out the screen from his apartment window, but it’s not metal, it's polyplex so it doesn’t work. Next, he tries borrowing a sieve from the apartment complex's kitchen. This seems to work a little, but only by using several sieves does he manage to kind of block the signal. He duct tapes them around his head, and gets some rest, but they fall off in his sleep. Finally, he goes to a hardware store and buys some metal screening, ignoring the salespeoples' stares as he wraps it around his head. It works! He spends the next several months at home, with a metal screen wrapped around his head.]

Linguistics team? I thought. More like a bunch of amateurs.

[Luce goes on a  mission to get cleaned up, but dieting is too hard, and he strains an ankle trying to jog.]

#
 
The stim patch tucked behind my ear prickled wickedly. There was always slight nausea at this point, as I felt my head lighten and buzz.
My heart heaved oceanically.
The room brightened.
The air crisped.
I snapped up my papers and slapped them on the kitchen counter. I placed them in a precision grid covering the counter and stovetop. Top to bottom, left to right, with about a millimeter of separation between sheets.
My notes had been scrawled in my usual perfunctory handwriting. But even that seemed somehow neater now. The words looked almost embossed into the paper. Had I been stressed writing them? That seemed impossible.
I started reading from the top, saying each word until my pronunciation exactly matched my now-perfect memory of the alien tongue on the news reports.
My Sakha came back to me too, along with my Russian, and the bits of Yiddish I’d picked up at NYU. Somehow that part of my mind that sponged up grammar and sound became hyper-awake, and oddly, the other languages felt like they loaned themselves to the puzzle at hand.
It felt like each language was trying on the Jovian phonology like it was in a changing room at a clothing store.
  	None of them fit the structure of the sounds I’d transcribed, but something funny happened.
Between trying the Jovian sounds in each of the language structures, a new shape emerged. As if only by putting your pants on your head, and the your arms, and then your butt, could you feel out the actual shape of the pants.
I didn’t think I had anything like the real grammar of the language yet, but certain arrangements of utterances felt right and others felt off. Maybe there was just an internal logic to the words themselves that had eluded me up to now. Or maybe I was seeing ghosts.
But I read the words on the page aloud, and without knowing what I was saying, I thought I knew what order to put them in so they held meaning.
 
#
 
[Luce soon gets an offer to interview as a Jovian linguistic researcher. He manages to get himself shaved and changed and fashions his metal screen into a liner for a baseball cap, and this seems to do the trick. It looks kind of dorky, and he’s not sure he wants to go to an interview in it, but it’s all he has. He arrives at the interview on time and dressed in a tux, which was all he had left in the closet. But the interview sign-in sheet includes an agreement to test all applicants for stims, and he chickens out.]
 
I was feeling pretty good in my powder blue suit.
The secretary seated me and handed me a tablet to fill out the application.
She seemed annoyed that I didn’t have the implant so I could just autopopulate the form, but I ignored her glares and tapped out all of my info. I put Brian Lumiere and Ambassador Carter as references, although I barely spoken to either, and I didn’t have their contact information.
Five pages in, I stared at the screen.
I was expected to a drug test.
“Excuse me,” I said, “Is there a restroom?”
The secretary pointed me out the door and to the left.
“Good,” I said, then dropped off the tablet and opened the door. “I’ll be right back,” I said, and left.

[Luce is devastated more than he would have thought possible. He's determined to get strong again, and find another way into the Jovian research facility. He decides to get out and start living life again, looks for a job, and eventually is able to parlay all of his hours watching cooking shows into a job as a short order cook. He loses a couple more pounds, using stims increasingly heavily, as the other wait staff are heavily into the drug scene.]
 
#
 
I blinked through the job offerings, and there were quite a few of them, though most were positions based on Callisto or Io. Research jobs were rare. From what I gathered, the consortium that managed trans-belt research had been deadlocked on funding issues since the first hostilities that led to the outbreak of the Sino-Brazilian war eight years ago.    	Representatives from both countries were on the board, and most of their backers attached riders to funding streams prohibiting their donations and investment capita be used towards facilities that employed “enemy civilians.”
This was’t a big deal for the mining facilities, since they were largely staffed and managed by individual countries, but it wreaked havoc with the research side, which had always been self consciously transnational. As it was, most of the researchers on the Jovian platform were Brazilian, and of course, most of the researchers were Chinese, so the same labor relations that ruled dirtside, ruled in Joviana.
With all the jobs on offer, it was daunting just sorting through them. The consortium was hiring a ton of engineers—probably ninety percent of the positions—so I could scratch those off the list pretty quickly. But that left just under a hundred other offerings. There were positions for clerical staff, which I though I could do, but would probably have me sitting in an office on an orbital station somewhere, and I’d rather be sweeping floors than be a desk jockey.
I briefly considered enrolling in pilot school so I could apply for a one of the handful of assistant copilot positions. It would be a few years of work, but a pilot definitely wouldn’t be stuck in one place. The only thing that kept me from pursuing that line of thinking was my uncertainty about rising through the highly specialized ranks. Driving a mining rig wouldn’t necessarily qualify one for piloting a drop ship, and a passenger ferry may or may not be the right kind of pilot job to get me close to the Jovian cloud platform. Besides, from what I’d seen on the Trans-Siberian railway, those with very particular specializations had a tendency to hold on to the positions for a long, long time. I talked to a Chuvash crane operator who’d been in the same position for twenty years and still going strong until a freak wind gust knocked him onto a rail spike and sent him into chronic pancreatitis. Nobody else in northern Asia was trained to operate that particular make of crane, so the dockyard was crippled until somebody completed several years of training, or the company could buy and install an new crane.
After an hour and a half of sorting, I’d whittled the list down to a dozen positions. There were vacuum technicians, logistics coordinators, a cook, a few radio operators—that was getting closer to what I was looking for—and even a dermatologist. Apparently, hydrogen sulphide exposure was a major problem on Io. But none of the jobs was really up my alley.
I powered down the screen and got dressed. I needed to get some air. And maybe a marzipan turnover.
But on my way out the door, I passed the mirror and realized my corduroys were stained from crotch to cuff. I looked like shit. And I looked like I smelled like shit.
So, I got undressed again, ordered a razor rush delivery, and showered. The buzzer arrived at my window as I was toweling off, and I nodded at it as I took the razor. It was charged and ready to go, and after filling the sink with gloppy piles of foam and whiskers, I washed off my face and was astonished with how old I looked. I looked like my dad, and absent the beard, it was clear I even had my dad’s double chin.
I walked, naked, back to the bedroom. This time, taking care not to look in the mirror, and went through the closet.
I wasn’t going to wear that stupid track suit again, and the only jeans Ramloco had supplied were marred buy an ugly orange floral pattern on one leg, and huge block letters on the other: MAREM.
Ugh.
That left a tan suit with suede-like elbows, shoulders, and knees, or half a dozen tuxes.
I went with the tan suit.
 
#
 
  	A crack snaked its way across the wet sidewalk, the storefront's luminescent ad loop catching its edge and making it flash like lightning.
I waited in line behind a tall man in a leather suit, holding hands with an indecisive toddler. The three of us—me, the leather man, and the marzipan guy—watched news on the sidewalk.
The sidewalk projection was buckled across cracked old pavement with asphalt patches. It blared travel advisories and showed footage of an ocean floater, a big one with a now-crumpled modern take on an old-fashioned crow’s nest. The report said it had been scuttled off the Oregon coast.
“I used to be king of one of those,” I said.
He looked me up and down, spat on my shoes, and walked away.
The marzipan salesman shook his head.
“You looked more kingly when you had the beard.”
“So, you recognize me?”
“You’re my best customer.”
“But I haven’t seen you in months.”
He chuckled, “I’ve seen you. Those buzzers have eyes. You’re looking better.” He paused, “Were you really on one of those artificial island things?”
I nodded. “Not one like that.”

Hatchlings, Part III: Joviana

[The Jovians need to navigate the magnetic field lines to "power" their nervous system. It's mainly passive, like an insect's circulatory system, pushed along by the magnetosphere.]

[Jovians are very creative.]

[Luce talks someone's ear off on the Sakha conflict once he opens up and realizes he has a lot to say about it... but the other person feigns interest for a while until finally... "Shut up!"]


[Luce distrusts the head of linguistics, but this is only his projection.]
[One old timer has Manganism]
[Deinococcus radiourans gene therapy]

 All of the news feeds were shot in press rooms or laboratories. But the flight to the research platform was adventure in itself.
We—the other new cook, and a medical intern—had transferred from the interplanetary transport system to a Jovian freight truck in orbit around Ganymede via automated ferry. Both the truck and ferry were completely automated. There are no human staff on board. I found that comforting.
The interplanetary was aging, but immaculately maintained. Buy contrast, the ferry to Ganymede gave me my first taste of the Jovian system. Gunpowder-smelling fines sparkled on the walls. I nodded at a tall low-g woman who was getting off as I was getting on, and I sat down in her seat because it had already been wiped clean. The others just sat down in the grime. By the time we were shuffled off the ferry, across a short expanse of the black ice moon, we all looked like we had been shaded in pencil strokes. Everything associated with the planet seemed to be coated in dusts of various colors and textures and odors. In the truck, it was sulfur-stink yellow and slightly sticky. Hand prints and finger-drawings decorated the otherwise barren metal walls up to the height of a man. Luce was here, I offered.
The truck took us to Amalthea, and I spent the whole time staring out a tiny port, punched through arm-length shielding, at the face of the giant planet. We were coming in close. From here, Jupiter looked like a large beach ball [= 43 degrees = 86 x Moon] sitting on the heavily-curved horizon of Amalthea. The base was a kilometer away, and it just poked over the horizon of the long axis. The near axis horizon seemed significantly closer. If you ever wanted to feel like you were standing on a giant football… or maybe a giant ridge. Many segments of the horizon were bitten into by escarpments falling off into craters.
Amalthea is a little chunk of oblong debris, a tiny compass needle pointing straight at the heart of Jupiter. When the truck sat down on it, I could feel the ka-chunk of the braces locking into the moon’s surface.
We walked along a tether to the hangar.
There were no windows on the deep drop ship.

Kitchen staff, apparently, were being replaced all at once. Which is to say, the two of them were being replaced by the two of us. Their contracts had expired and ours had begun. So, I guess that meant we were going to have to train ourselves. Bad planning on someone’s part.
Another piece of bad planning was that there was nobody in charge of the kitchen. My partner, Felix Pinho, was amicable enough, but neither of us seemed to want to be the boss. We both reported directly to the platform supervisor, Dr. Adamsky.
On our first day, Felix asked Dr. Adamsky, “What about alien food?”
The man suppressed a chuckle.
“You don’t have to worry about that,” he said. “Down in their strata, they filter feed other airborne organisms and inorganics. Up here on the platform, they bring what they need in the form of dried—uh—feed, if you will.”
“Is it edible to humans,” I asked, curiosity getting the better of me.
“Probably not,” he answered, this time chuckling outright. “Anyway, you probably wouldn’t even recognize it as food. They say they have a variety, but it all looks like shredded plastic bags to me.”

I was exhausted after pulling my first kitchen shift. I didn’t yet know how I was going to worm my way into the research staff, but I did know I was going to have to get my notes in order.
I sat down to work under the pale entryway light, spreading my papers across half the floor of my small cabin. Too dark to work, too dark even to stay awake.
I pulled the thermos of coffee out of my pack, poured a cup and sat with my back against my bed.
 
I jumped awake with a start. My coffee had spilled on the cabin floor and soaked my inner thigh.
Damnit. I missed my stims.
 
Back on Earth, spending time with cooks and waitstaff, it was hard not to do stims. It wasn’t unusual, after the tip account was divvied out, to walk out with a thousand dollars cash in your pocket.
Here, everything was locked down. Hell, I had to be given key card access the flatware cabinet.
The whole platform was repurposed from the mouth of a gas scoop. Unlike most vessels, built to operate in placid interplanetary space, the scoop had been built to take the pressure, and all the battering of a violent Jovian atmosphere. The problem is, they stuffed its gaping maw with the guts of a government traffic control station. Hence, all the outdated security equipment and locks on every door.
Which meant, I had to talk to people. So, who to talk to?
The kitchen staff were mistrusted. We had ask permission to gain access to our own balls. So, who else was there? The research staff didn’t seem the types. These were obscure academics used to leisurely pursuit of data for government consumption, not the eager career minded publish-or-perish types you find at university.
Management.

MEETING THE JOVIAN
 
 The surface of the tentacles looked like frosted glass underlain by streaks of orange and red. Looking closer at the alien’s skin, I saw it was covered with tiny, barely visible velvety protrusions, barely thicker than a hair. Each one split about halfway down its length to form a Y at the end. And they roiled and rippled like water.
Once, when I pressed my forehead against the glass, the creature tensed and a fine mist of something that looked like pollen puffed from all over its surfaces, forming dense swirling puffs where folds gathered in the skin.
I opened my notebook, and tried a few words.
“Ahrm har ahrm raha,” I said.
No reaction, so I tried another, “Larngsheh.”
I  tried again, louder: “LARNGSHEH. AHRM HAR AHRM RAHA.”
I felt like an Americanrican tourist in Laredo.
I pressed my face against the window, and shouted: “Ahrm har ahrm raha. Ahrm raha.”
The words squashed out, distorted. From here, the creature was abstract beauty. That delicate diaphanous peach fuzz, changing at angles from yellow through orange, then into blue and tiny flashes of deep mauve. The split ends fondled the air like a million slow-motion snake tongues.
Frustrated, I gathered my papers and backed out the door.
 
But as I was leaving, the alien thumped at the glass with a pair of tentacles. I froze, then ran back too the window, shouting what was probably nonsense. But it felt like I was speaking to it in its own language.
I jammed my cheek against the window, reading aloud at first, then shouting from memory.
“Ahlanla elhren raha san,” it said.
“Ahrm har ahrm raha,” I said.
It shivered pollen, then said, “A’hrmhau a’hrmraugha.”
“What?” I asked, astonished.
“A’hrmhau a’hrmraugha.”
“A hrma a hrumaha,” I shouted.
“A’hrmhau a’hrmraugha.”
“A hrmau a hrmagha.”
“A’hrmhau a’hrmraugha.”
“A hrmhau a hrmraugha.”
“A’ a’ a’hrmhau,” it said.
“A’ a’…” I started, but it interrupted.
“A’hrmhau.”
“A’hrmhau,” I said.
“A’hrmhau! A’hrmhau! A’hrmhau!”
“A’hrmhau! A’hrmhau! A’hrmhau!” I laughed.
I got it! I got it.
But what had I got?
 
#
 
Nothing prepared me for the full scale of the Jovian ecosystem. I had no idea.

TRIP TO IO

Instead, they invented a whole new language on the fly in order to communicate with humans. It’s nearly impossible to tell, but it’s meant to be an imitation of the sounds of Mandarin, Portuguese, and English. After speaking with Luce, Ehrmfell learns to imitate English fairly well. They call this pidgin language “long talk” as contrasted with the sounds they use among each other, which sound more like whistling. Once he gets it, Luce’s opinion changes immediately. I cracked the inner hatch, and was struck by the eggy scent I’d heard about on Io.
The Io station was downright extravagant. The walks were all glass and smoke. And there was movement. A shadow in the haze. I stuck my face up close to the window to see what lay behind.
 
  	I jumped when there was a wet smack on the glass.
  	There was a great "Allllooooo!"   like an elephant trying out its French.
  	“Ehrmfell, you beat us here!”
  	"Mr. Ehrmfell?" asked Lumiere.
  	”Eyrimifyolll," hem corrected, "Walgome, fffrent."
  	Ehrmfell rubbed hes floating body on the glass, looking for all the world like a puppy snuggling its master.

We’d had to get permission from the Mining Coalition to travel to Io, or really to travel anywhere, since their interests took priority over everything in the solar system. It wasn’t usually a big deal, just a matter of scheduling routes that didn’t conflict with the big mining haulers. But since Io station was new, we had strict limitations on our movement here. 
Ehrmfell didn’t seem to care about any of that. He gleefully took us on a tour of the whole station. It was the first permanent station built to accommodate both Jovian and human workers. Io was a good proving ground for the technology, because the volatile mining operations involved exposure to a wide array of substances, some of which were irritants, or even deadly to humans but did not harm Jovian tissues at all. And the opposite was also true. So, the Jovian prototype mining teams took on portions of the operation the humans couldn’t, and vice versa. So far, Brian had explained, the system was working better than predicted. 
“I expect they’ll extend this model to other mining operations,” said Brian. “It turns out the Jovians have a great deal of talent for the work, even apart from their different exposure sensitivities.” 
Following Ehrmfell through the halls of the station proved a little confusing, since the Jovian pressure tubes didn’t always match up with the human corridors. 

Ehrmfell ended our tour at a great window overlooking the Ah Peku test mine. 
“I guess this sight will become familiar throughout the solar system over the next couple of years.”
Brian nodded.
“But it won’t look like this,” he said. 
Ah Peku Patera stretched into the distance. A volcano erupted a white plume in the distance, veiling the sky in a snowlike haze. 
“Amirani is erupting,” said Brian as my eyes lingered. “It’s hundreds of clicks away, but the plume reaches high in the low gravity.”
I knew we were on the rim of a vast volcanic crater, but from here, it looked like a dark gray-brown plain all the way to the horizon, with cliffs stretching to eternity to either side. Vesicles of golden wax seemed to seep from the cliff walls, piling into delicate pinnacles on the ledges below, reminding me of the illustrations of an alchemist’s candelabra I had seen in the childrens’ horror books Lehan read to us at night.

[Tragedy strikes.]
 
It was one of those almost-bright Jovian days. Brian had called in a pinprick jet in Ehrmfell's dome-seam, and I was rushing down, longtalking the whole way to calm hem. The open sky in the Hall sickened me more than usual, I think because the clouds' canyon walls had taken on the texture of tripe. I felt I was in the maw of a whale. 
I wondered if Noe ever got his turtle scute. 
I turned the corner into the Quad and jumped back from a sudden huff and blow that almost knocked me on my ass. 
I ran toward Ehrm's dom, methane-woozy. When I got through the vapor, I saw Brian struggling against the pane. Ehrm's loofah twitched madly, caught between the pane and the floor, bright orange from oxygenosis. My testes ached in sympathy. My stomach turned queasy.
Jovair spurted like flatulence. Ehrmfell made the yawning sound I associated with highflight--but I think it was a scream. 
"Get the exercise vest online," I shouted, "put it underneath him!" 
I flickered surprise, but when I spoke again, I continued with the insult: "He's going to stick to the ventilation if we don't get something underneath his dorsus."
Ehrm's translucent brains were pressed up against the pane and I stared into them to distract from the exertion of every muscle in my body straining to keep the dom from exploding.

[Add a short description of Jovian anatomy. Six thick, tapered appendages protruding out from under a translucent jelly-like globe attached to a thick trunk with a lightbulb-shaped balloon  rising above. When they speak, a spongy organ protrudes from underneath the limbs. This organ is nicknamed the “loofah” and functions both as the reproductive organs and the vocal apparatus. Jovian speech is surprisingly similar to human sounds given this exotic vocal tract. The mouth is used only for filter-feeding and is located above the brainsac.]

  	I turned out that the Jovian cultures fell primarily into five bands—two in the belts north of the equator, and three in the southern belts. Within each of these five primary cultural groups were several “moieties” defined in part by perceived seniority of lineages. At some period in the past, the northernmost of the southern banders, forced by aggression from the dominant southern middle zoners, had successfully executed an invasion across the equator.

They develop a little bit of a relationship, and Luce feels like he's finally become himself again.]

Chapter 00: Knock Knock
 
[When they find the porthole into the moon, Luce is "afraid to knock" again. He tries to prevent contact at first(?) ("I'm the wrong person for this," he says).]
 
[How do they discover the portal on the moon? How do the aliens come out of it?]
 
[Logistics of discussion…how to communicate? They invent a language…an artificial pidgin. They agree on some kind of time unit for the pidgin only—maybe the orbit of the rocky moon?]
 
[What are the Rogues time units? They agree to be subsurface for a particular amount of time.]
[The indigenous names for the dominant culture of the Rogue world mostly translate as "The Metalworkers" and carry with them an air of awe and fear. Their metalwork--all cold worked--makes them the hyper power under the ice, and the revelation of human materials technology causes a shudder of terror to roll throughout he ruling elites.
[The Last Portal Battle ended with a pressure bomb planted at the Portal entrance on the planet surface, leaving a crater. The moon dwellers convince the humans to help them build a new entrance structure and connect it to the Portal—the Jovians (and Robin) notice the Rogues are observing a little too closely and urge caution.]
[It’s decided that Luce will accompany the Rogues into the subsurface of the planet. When Robin learns of this, she freaks out.]
“Do you have experience with high-pressure diving?” shouted Robin.
“I’m an ace submarine captain,” I said.
“Really?” she sighed. Her shaking subsided and she cracked an almost-smile. “Impressed.”
“Sure—I’ll take you around in my Chinese bullet sub sometime.”
“Wait, bullet subs?”
“Yeah, a bullet sub is…”
“No, I know what a bullet sub is. But those are most definitely not high-pressure divers.”
“What do you mean?”
“I mean, that’s shallow stuff. The pressure a bullet sub has to deal with is maybe like a few hundred kilos per square centimeter, tops. Below the surface of that planet down there…it’s probably millions of kilos. We’re talking thousands of pascals.”
“Okay, fine. I guess I'm not deep enough. Why don't you tell me how it's done.”
“Me? I have no idea. I just know the mathematics. And I know that you haven't faced gigapascal pressures before. Not on Earth. Not unless you've been a deep crust miner in one of your many past lives. How deep did you go in Jupiter?”
“Three atmospheres.”
She looked as though I’d just told her her mother was diagnosed with inoperable bone cancer.
 
[As soon as the tube closed I realized my mistake. i could feel myself being upended, slotted into the portal…]
 
[When Luce arrives below surface, all hell breaks loose and he’s helpless inside his sample tube.]
 
[Its only by looking at their (tactile) etchings that Luce gets a sense of how the Rogues see their world. What’s translucent, what’s transparent, what’s opaque, which way is up and which down. ]
 
[Things that float—the less dense the more buoyant—appear “heavy” to them, and things that are dense appear light. As such, sample containers from the oversurface are extremely heavy and difficult to move, requiring large metal attachments to lift. ]
 
[When they arrive subsurface, there is a battle that Luce only gets a hint of in the tumult of being tossed around. The tube is eventually it turns out the moon metaleers lost the battle and were captured or killed…only one was able to negotiate and get releases…who then gets Luce released (the “under” thought he was an implosion bomb and almost destroyed the tube. The one who gets Luce released is in contact with the remnants of the earlier ruling class, who want the moon dwellers’ help retaking the citadel. …eventually, Luce is moved and spends significant time in view of the darkness of the subsurface ocean, quietly communicating in simple phrases with his keeper. The bioluminescence is everywhere, and he learns that it is the motion of the animals that the Rogues are picking up on, not the bioluminescence. He learns they can’t see the light but decides to test their perceptions by showing a light to them. When he moves it, it seems alive to them—but when he shows the a moving picture without moving himself, they freak out because they think he’s dying. He realizes motion is associated with life, whether there is bioluminescence or not, but bioluminescence increases the emotional content. They can’t see.]
 
[How does he interact with the Rogues? Do they rig up some special room? How does he eat? Eventually, he’s just getting by on stims and losing weight rapidly.]
 
As I felt myself start to upend and slide downward, I panicked. I reached for the latch, felt its glossite-like surface, started to pull out of fear, but simultaneously expecting the safety catch to prevent me. The tube jiggled slightly, and with a sudden hiss, my ears popped. Shit! I shoved the handle back in place. Of course they didn’t design it with a safety latch! My heartbeat pushed back against my popped ears. I had almost imploded myself with that second of panic. 

I had to hold it tight, but I realized I wasn’t ready for this. The pressure and the unknown were one thing, but the darkness was quite another. The ancient sample tube rasped lightly against the freshly minted portal seal. The darkness change was more felt than seen. The darkness inside the tube went from feeling hollow to feeling solid. I clutched my chest, arms shaking. My heartbeat filled the small space, as I felt my stomach fall out from under me. 

The sound of this darkness was like car wheels on a wet highway--the smooth kind of highway like we had in Canada when I was a kid. 

I turned on my handset, relieved at the dim white light. I hadn’t thought to ask about how long the descent would be. Who would I ask? The Rogues? Robin? Neither would have been able to answer. How thick was the ice? How fast was I traveling?

Though the distance between me and the surface was rapidly increasing, I could hear tube after tube being loaded and launched, and I felt some relief that I was part of a long caravan. I shined my screen light around the inside of the container. Robin had inspected every seal and latch, and assured me the interior would withstand the pressures the Rogues led us to expect. But given her impassioned attempt to talk me out of it, I assumed her assurance was just her way of making my life easier. She had hesitated, and I took that to mean she was only trying to ease my fears. Or was she trying to talk herself into letting me go? How did we know the aliens knew the actual pressures we would encounter. I mean, they live down there, but I live on Earth, yet I’d be hard pressed to tell you the difference between the pressure at the bottom of the Marianas Trench and the top of Mount Everest. 

My breathing was conscious. Slow, long breaths. 

My light should get me through the thirty or forty hours I expected to be gone, but somehow I was worried I would use up the battery if I kept it on for very long so early in my descent. 

No, damnit. Stop being neurotic. I wasn’t going to let myself second guess every decision. If I had to ration my battery, it would be later. Now was the time to occupy my mind with something--anything--other than the descent. 

The latch handle that had almost killed me--no, let’s not think about that. Don’t need to be neurotic, but there was no reason to be morbid either. Argh. Stop. Focus. 

Breathe.

The light-bathed latch handle had felt like glossine, but looked metallic. It wasn’t designed for an occupant, of course. It was just the backside of the mechanism intended to be triggered from the outside. It was unnervingly loose in its housing. The unfortunate thing about this tube was how monochrome it was, even for the lack of good lighting. The washed-out pinkness of my fingers looked bright by comparison to the gray handle, the gray walls, and the gray webbing Robin had installed for me to clip into after removing the baffles and specimen holders that had originally lined this overblown file cabinet. 

There were something that resembled pop rivets running down the back side of the tube, and slots for shelving running down the sides. This was the first piece of alien technology I had ever examined up close, and I was only the second or third person to do so, after Robin and maybe Captain Moos. I was surprised at how un-alien it was. What it reminded me of was looking a long-outdated version of something with which I was already familiar. Like an old telephone. Its purpose was obvious, but the design considerations were just not the same as those of a modern handy. The tube was clearly a sample container, complete with viewing window and a spot for a label. But the viewport was on the end, and the label was a translucent circle right in the middle of it. Obviously, they didn’t mind their view being cluttered the way a human would. 

The rivets that held the viewport in place weren’t flattened and flush with the glass--or whatever material this was--like the ports on our ship. Instead, the glass had a series of scallops cut out of it, against which the rivets fit flush. 

Oh god, how long am I going to be in here?

Ok. Think about something else. 

Robin. No. Ehrmfell. No. 

Kendra.

Fifteen years old, and sitting on the bridge at Humber Marshes. She didn’t want to be there, and neither did I. I was maybe eight or nine. She looked like a contestant on one of those kids’ game shows. All glossy lipstick and curled hair. Her coat was puffy. The girls at the home didn’t look like this. They were simple, basic. Like me. We were strangers. It was the first time we’d been reunited since I was born. I guess I wouldn’t have been a stranger to her, not exactly. I was her baby brother, all grown up. It’s funny, but I don’t think I ever considered how she must’ve experienced it until now. I imagined our parents bringing me home to a kindergarden-aged Kendra. She must’ve known the infant Luce, for those few months before we were separated. I understood that she’d been left with an aunt who refused to take care of me. I had spent years imagining what my parents looked like--in my young mind, they had been an interesting pair, improbable by anyone else’s standards, I’m sure. My imagined father a white-haired man with a pointed beard, my mother thin and willowy but with a determined brow. I knew I also had a sister, but that seemed irrelevant. So when she showed up, at the behest of our aunt, I almost refused to go with her. 

I remember her words exactly: “Come on, we don’t have to hang out long if you don’t want to.” With some prodding from one of the staff members of the group house whose name and face I forget entirely, I was convinced to take a trip to the park with her. 

Lehan had packed us both lunches, her motherliness knowing no bounds. 

Humber Marshes was familiar...it was one of the few places us kids got to “vacation” once in a while. Every year, we’d go camping, tromping through the woods, creeking. Funny, I’d almost forgotten about that. The first meeting with Kendra was the only image of that park I’d ever thought about since. 

Now I wondered what Kendra had made of her little brother sitting on that bridge. I must have been dirty, sticky, smelly. That’s how I remembered the group home. Too many hands making messes. Too few cleaning them up. 

We had sat there for a while, a warmish autumn breeze still too cool for my tastes. Hah! To think, there was a time when bone cold wasn’t second nature to me!

I don’t remember what we talked about….

A jolt to the tube knocked me out of my reverie. Were we there already? 

But no, the sound of tires on wet pavement continued. I pictured the stretches of ice we’d already traveled. When had it turned to “we”? I had felt so alone. Now, I remembered I’m going somewhere. Who will I meet? What will I say? Can I say anything? I may be walled off for now, but if we’re really at the end of our journey into the body of this cold planet, could it be that I have to dust off my linguist’s hat one more time?

The sound of the descent continued without change for long enough that I convinced myself the journey wasn’t finished. How long ago had it begun? This tube was small, and I was unsure of how long I would be in it. 

It was as uncertain as when I was hooded and led into the Siberian wilderness so long ago. Uncertain but for the familiarity of uncertainty. 

Heh. 

Okay, time to be pragmatic. I’d planned and packed my rations, but only now did I have the time to double-check. Apparently. 

I started crying. How had I gotten myself into this situation? I started laughing the tears out of my eyes. Had anyone in the history of humanity ever deliberately placed themselves in such a precarious position? Alone in the dark was status quo for our species, I suspected. But no, there was always companionship...spooning apes on a darkened savannah. Our ship wasn’t a bright oasis far from home. We were but scattered dust, to be brushed from God’s shoulders. 

Oh god, I thought, bursting out one more time into laughter. The sound of my voice shook the container. My ears popped again, and gasped a tiny, surprised gasp. I pressed my hands against the walls of my...my...my tomb. No! Not so morbid, Lucient. Stop now. Breathe. 

Breathe.



Breathe. Now, let’s think.

Why?

Shrug. 

What else is there to do?

What would a normal person do when confron… I screamed. AAAAAAAAGHHHHHHHHHH. That felt fake. I let it all out, aaaaaaaaaaaaaaa. Aaaaaa. 

No. I cried and laughed and screamed and shook, and felt a chill of almost electrical intensity run up and down my body. I was hyperventilating, but I was happy to be letting myself feel. Not letting myself. Being gripped by all that was happening. What the hell is happening to me? 

Taking stock: Plunging into pressures that wouldn’t just crush the volatiles out of my body, but would compress them into solids. My guides into the shiverless coldness were more unfathomable than the thoughts of an octopus. An octopus was downright homey by comparison. More unfathomable than than the sensations of a jellyfish--an oyster--a coral. I started hyperventilating again, but caught myself. Okay, I’m farther from Earth than any human being has ever been. So far outside the realm that we were built to inhabit that… Well, actually that would describe Robin and Brian and Moos. So far from only place in the universe that isn’t entirely destructive to human life that a small crack in the ship’s wall could pop them like a bubble. And now me, here I was, having decided to take a foray outside that tiny outpost, outside the outermost outside of nowhere. And into someplace not only not meant for humans...a place where Earth life, where matter from Earth right down to the dust under your fingernails was never meant to be. A bit of germ from a distant planet wriggling my way into a far-off planet to the incredulous laughter of some extra-universal observer spying in on this little droplet of frozen water in deep space. I. Lucient Fagan. Contaminant. 

Somewhere deep in the Sahara, a wind blows, carrying billions upon billions of pieces of dust from between the sand grains. Most fall elsewhere among the dunes, but some are carried off to sea, to be dropped in rapidly shifting dunes on the waters of the Atlantic. Some tiny fraction remain aloft, carried high into the stratosphere, to be distributed around the world. A thousand, a hundred, a dozen, ten, five, one. The last mote settles on the leaf of the last marigolds planted by an old lady in Montana before she passed away last month. So far from home, that one Saharan mote. And so close compared to me.

I. Lucient Fagan. Contaminant. 

I wondered what Kendra was up to right now. And when I would see her next.

[Pressure = rho*a*d where rho is density of liquid, a is gravitational acceleration, and d is depth. For example, Europa’s g is 1.32 m/s^2, and water density is about 1.0x10^3 gm/m^-3, so at 5 miles  (8.0x10^3 m)…density of frozen water is ~92% as liquid.]
 
[We don’t realize what the Rogues living in the moon are planning (an attempt to reconquer the subsurface empire that was overthrown a hundred and fifty years ago by the underclasses). ]
 
[The ocean is warmer than we’d expect. Neutrino annihilation might thermally regulate iron-cored water-worlds, sustaining habitable subglacial oceans.]
 
“They use something like sonar to detect the positions and motions of objects around them. Living especially things trigger all sorts of nested verb systems to describe various kinds of movement and intention and interaction by individuals and among groups. Their language is based on emotions and movement…extremely, extremely complex verbs of motion. So, they speak of various movements of objects and at the same time, these verb complexes are influenced by their bioluminescent patterning. Only, I don’t think they can actually see the patters. It seems like their emotional states change when exposed to various lighting patterns, but they aren’t able to describe or consciously sense anything to do with light. You can shine a light right on them, and they’ll recognize it as an object but they won’t be able to tell if it’s on or off. And it’s the same with their own body coloration. They respond but don’t see. It seems too be entirely subconscious.”
“We can see their emotions but they can’t?” asked Robin.
“What a great strategic gift!” said Brian.
“Sort of. We can see their emotional change,” I said, “but they're extremely complex. It’s like looking at a high resolution brain scan. Useful, but it'll take an army of researchers to decode those details.”
“We don't necessarily need a lot of detail about their internal emotional states. What we need is to see how they react to specific inputs,” said Robin.
