TITLE: Chewie
(c) 2005 by Nathan McKnight
A story in 7 scenes.


Intro to "Chewie" by ChatGPT

Step into the grotto of Stig, a gnome with a peculiar devotion and an even stranger purpose. With vivid prose and humor dark as the underground, McKnight weaves a tale of faith, folly, and the mysteries of a world all his own.

Scene 1. THE GROTTO

	Stig sat up in bed, shaking and ashamed. The scantily clad beard-nixies fled as his dream haze cleared. The room convulsed. The diorama he'd made from buttons and plastic dinosaurs fell, taking his felten cap with it. He yipped a feeble "Oh my!" then snatched up the cap, clutching it to his chest as the quake subsided.
	Leaning back with a sigh, Stig picked up his old jimson pipe from the nightstand.
	He would have to clean up after morning smoke, he thought, stroking his century-long beard and lighting up. He suckled the acrid smoke and sparkles infused his vision. Images of nixies danced in the harlequin tapestry behind his eyelids.
	Then a lurch and shudder knocked the smoke out of him and the nixie visions fled again. 
	"This is quite definitely out of sorts!" he protested, hopping out of bed with a scowl. 
	He rubbed the sleep out of his eyes, troubled by a disturbing click-clickity-click echoing down from the grotto.
	He padded through the soft corridor, trying to sidestep piles of moosh, but the shaking knocked him to the floor and he had to use his hands the whole way. By the time he got there, he was exhausting his repertoire of ancient gnomish curse words. When he finally made it to the end of the corridor, he wiped his hands on his overalls and yanked on the tail of the hairball dangling from the ceiling.
	He pulled himself up, whispering the familiar oath: "Mistress Cindy, please forgive me my unclean dreams. For your slightest whim, I would gladly give my--" but he was cut off by yet a third tremor.
	The hairy rope swung and Stig held tight. 
	He climbed on, squishing his way through the tight passage in the ceiling. Fluid treacled into his eyes, and he held his breath, reaching through the squishy passage, wriggling himself upwards until he emerged, surfacing in the Reflecting Pool.
	Sallow bile lapped at the base of the imposing Bezoar, his hand-woven monument to beloved Miss Cindy. Stig's nose twitched at its embarrassing unfinishedness, as it had been doing now for too long. 
	The clickety-click echoed around the chamber.
	But there was something else--Stig furrowed his eyebrows--an odd glint on the waves.
	The Reflecting Pool had ever changed from blue to pink to green with the sweet rains that poured down. But this, this un-color glow was altogether different.
	Her Greatness's grotto's lovely darkness needling with preternatural light, filtering down through the crenellated dome ceiling... He held up his hands and though his eyes were closed, he could clearly see the bones of his fingers.
	Could it be? 
	It was!
	At long last, sign from Cindy!
	"Hallelujah," he breathed, voice jumping to a shout, "Hallelujah! Praised be!"
	He clutched his overalls and to his surprise, the medallion he kept tucked away in his pocket began to warm against his chest. His fingers prickled through the fabric as he grasped its dull edges. He'd prayed to the medallion nightly since he found it, but since the hairfalls had lately failed, he'd begun to doubt its power. 
	"Dearest Miss Cindy," he said, kneeling.
	A last click, cut him off, and the light snapped out.
     Stig splashed to the quivering base of his beloved's Bezoar.
     "O Miss Cindy! I have seen your magnificent light. Your revelation shall not go unheeded!"
     "This is a great day in the Reflecting Pool," he whispered over the quiet drip-dripping.
	Stig put his hand to his mouth and looked around, shooting glances all about the waves. Surely, the ethereal light had occasioned a hairfall as the cool sweet rains always had.
     He ran his hands through the slippery liquid of the pool. Sure enough, there were some hairs. But just a few pitiful bits.
     Stig pouted.
     Where was the angel hair? It had been weeks since a bona fide hairfall. Had he not witnessed a miracle? How was he to complete the long-delayed bezoar?
     With one hand, he clutched the hairs, and with the other, he tugged his beard. He had worked so long to repay his boon from Miss Cindy. The great lady had always supplied the material for his work. He'd thought it certain that this Bezoar would in the end be the finest shrine ever built by gnomish hands.
     Now here it lay, unfinished.
     Now the golden fleece rained no more.
     Now Stig sagged, rubbing his brow.
     Might this have been something other than a miracle? The troubling thought crept across his mind. Could the tremor and glow not be taken instead as...as a warning?
     Goosebumps stippled his neck.
     "No," he said firmly. "I shall not have dark thoughts."
     He looked at the wisps of angel hair, frowned.
     Maybe--he reshaped his mind around a more hopeful idea, a shrewd expression crawling across his face--I wonder if maybe these few golden strands won't just suffice.
     He cursed his faithlessness.
     Yes-yesss, that was it! The Lady had provided him with the workings of a true miracle; one to magnify Her glory.
     Stig bunched the wisps of hair in his palm, then scoured every oozing surface of the Reflecting until he was certain he had gathered every last hair, long or short, regardless of frizz or fray.
     Holding the golden fleece high, and with great reverence, he made a most sincere effort at weaving the them into the Bezoar.
     His fingers were deft, fluttering over the split ends, hoping gently to mend and extend them with his stroke.
     Trying at it for a long stretch with bated breath, all he succeeded in weaving was a sickly straggle that marred the delicate curlicued surface of the great unfinished bezoar.
     Stig frowned.
	"That won't do," he muttered into his beard, "No-no n'no! That simply won't do at all."
	He put his hand to his face.
     "Dearest Cindy, what ever shall I do?"
	Lo these long years had he lovingly woven the bezoar--from its first spindly fibers, braiding in humble thanks to the merciful Miss Cindy, whose sacrifice had saved and elevated even as he was smoked out of his previous home.
	He closed his eyes and canted gnomish cants.
     "One last rain of your angelic fleece would be quite enough, I think," he said to himself. "Enough to adorn your Bezoar with a proper mantle--though it be pitiful, it is holden, I think, of such promise. Such promise."
	He turned his face upward.
	"A I so unworthy? My Liegess...give me a sign!"
     He folded chants into ancient Gnomish tongues, once, twice, thrice--then oncemore, twicemore, thricemore.
     And as he began the third three, a rumble rolled from above. The crenelated ceiling bulged inwards.
     The ceiling was ever in flux--he gasped--but the scale...


Scene 2. SAD SNUFFLEPUSS

Cindy yanks socks and pillows off the floor, tossing them on the bunk. 
"You're too old for that nasty stuffed animal," says mom.
"I'm not looking for Snuffles, I'm looking for my crocs," Cindy frowns. It's true, she isn't looking for him. She already knows her so-called brothers had thrown him in the laundry again. Stupid jerkwads.
She mopes to the font door where Clay and Leigh and mom eye her expectantly.
"What?" says mom, whose overstuffed raffia bag holds a pair of tan suede crocs. 
"Can I borrow yours?"
"No, you may not," says mom. "Just put on your flip flops and come on."

On the car ride, she is Mme. Laser Fingers, slicing the buildings as they pass, their smooth-cut surfaces sliding languidly apart.
"What are you pointing at?"
"Nothing."
"Then why are you pointing?"
Her laser fingers turn off, the city rests safely again--for now. 

*   *   * 

	She scratches her head, tugging at the short curls.
	She could resist the urge to pick and pull if she could just rub her head against her Sad Snufflepuss's sad snuffle.
	She winces, remembering every time Larry said that name. He hadn't made the distinction between Cindy and her stuffed snufflepuss, using the name for both of them. And though he was jovial about it, the subtle taunt brittled her.
	The name had stuck. Even after she'd rubbed the one-time Pound Puppie into an unrecognizable ball of fluff.
	Cindy stretches out on the back seat, kicking Clayton, who pushes her feet away and says "You get back seat next time, Leigh."

*   *   * 

     The orderly pushes her bed past mother and Larry and Clayton and Leigh. The hospital room buzzes morning fluorescent. Cindy absently runs her fingers over her eyebrow hairs, feeling one in particular poking out in the wrong direction.
     Clayton and Leigh's customary fight over the front seat is spilling over into the hospital, and mother's glare cuts through the sickly light.
	Briefly, Cindy pretends to be scratching her forehead before tucking her hands under the baby blue bedsheets.
     Mother is holding Leigh's hand, while her other hand rests gently on Clay's shoulder.
     Cindy is ready to get it over with. The angle they have her propped up in the bed makes the uncomfortable feeling under her ribcage all the more pronounced. She presses her hand against her short ribs and rolls the muscles of her abdomen.
	Hard to believe the persistent inner nudge will really be gone in a few hours.
     "Are they really going to open up my belly?" says Cindy.
     Mother rolls her eyes and shoots the orderly a glance. She puts her hand on the bed rail, and says, "What did you expect, Cindy?"
     Mom, she almost says.
	But then she sees Leigh shooting her a glance. She tries to read her brother's face, but it's stony. He mouths: Shut up, Chewie.
     "Mom," she sputters, "Leigh..."
	"Oh no you don't," shoots mother. "You leave your brother out of this. You have to take responsibility for your own actions."
	Then, with almost a hint of tenderness she adds, "It's time to grow up now. Cind--"
	Larry taps mom's shoulder, and waves her away, no doubt to the cafeteria. 
	She shakes her head at Cindy then turns to go, leaving Clayton and Leigh to watch over.
     Leigh leans in and whispers, "I get pull her stitches out."
     Clayton shrugs.

*   *   * 

	Cindy sits alone in the beige and the gray.  
	Clickclickclick. She tries to think of a clever comeback to the x-ray machine, but it just clickclickclicks at her again.
	"Okay," shouts the nurse from the other room, "one more to go."
	Clickclickclick.
	Cindy steps out of the x-ray room, taking off the lead-and-plastic apron. She bumps into the Nurse Noris, who's on her way in. 
	"Oh!" yelps the nurse, yanking a long, manicured fingernail out of her nostril.
	The nurse's smile is so wide her teeth seem perpetually on the verge of coming out of her face, it curls her lip high enough for Cindy to see the bump in the middle of her gums. Cindy stairs at her rainbow hair-scrunchies and savors the cloud of coconut smell wafting around her. A couple of stray orange-crush sparkles sparkle high on her cheeks, and Cindy can imagine her dancing downtown all night with some soft-handed boy.
	"It's okay," says Cindy, "I used to pick my nose too--when I was little." 
	Cindy smiles, but the nurse's smile fades. 
	"Mmmm hmm. Well. Let's get you to your room," says the nurse. 
	They walk, and for a second, Cindy is confused because she hears her crocs squeaking even though she's not wearing them. Then she realizes it's not her that's squeaking, it's Nurse Noris. 


Scene 3. TORTURE CHAMBER

	The dome undulated above, glistening. Stig patted the medallion, feverishly whispering words whose meanings had long been forgotten. 
	Of an instant, a gargantuan silven blade sliced a gash through the dome, narrowly missing Stig's nose--but lopping off a lock of his head hair.
     Stig screamed and reflexively snatched up the lock of silver hair, then scuttled into the little alcove behind the Bezoar while the blade sliced forth.
	Bracing air flooded in from the gape above. Stig held his palms against the precious Bezoar, shadowed from the harsh outside light. The great blade finished its cut and withdrew. 
     Stig hunched over the medallion, rubbing it worrystone-style, the hairs in his hands bristling against its silkenhard surface. He turned his back on the bezoar. Whatever was happening, he didn't want to see.
     But even with ducked head, the image of the bezoar in terrifyingly bright light played, awfully, across his inner eye.
     He'd been keenly aware that the Bezoar was unfinished. And this queer milky light showed it for true. A big fat felted hairball. He pressed his head on his hands in despair, sobbing lips sparking against the medallion. 
     "Oh, the folly," he muttered, "why did I build this bezoar? To so call down such frights upon myself!"
     The floor shook, throttling open his eyes.
     Through the new hole in the great dome reached a giant set of fingers, their heinous knuckle hairs visible through parchmentlike gloves.
     Stig shuddered.
     The fingers plunged--obscene--into the grotto, penetrating his holy heart of worship. They clamped down--tightly--terribly--onto the Bezoar.
     And they tugged.
     Stig's jaw hung slack.
     The Bezoar began to rise, its golden lumpiness raining aloft.
     No, he thought. "No!" he shouted, fumbling the medallion back into his pocket.
     He stood. Astonished, indignant.	
	Forgetting his fear, Stig thrust himself forward, digging weaver-thick fingers into the wet fuzz of the Bezoar. He pulled, twisting his hands in the fibers until they seemed permanently bent fistwise.  
     But the giant was too strong.
     Torn from Stig's grip, it left fistsfuls of clotted angel hair.
     When the giant had fully dislodged Stig's Bezoar from its esteemed placement across the Pyloric threshold and the Pool of Light rippled emptily, Stig grasped his hat in disbelief, not bothering to wipe away the droplets splashed upon his face by the bile sluicing from the Bezoar.
     He shook both fists.
     "It's....not.....FINIIIIIIISHED!" he screamed, righteous anger boiling from his spleen. 
	He stomped and cursed, bit his lip...and then...
	...he leapt.
	Cold air stung his wet face. 
	All of his senses: fish-eyed, but crystal clear. 
	And he got it!  
    	The giant wrenched the Bezoar--and Stig along with it--from the sustaining bile he'd called home for years.
	Its shiny hand slung them both carelessly into a chill chromen basin. Stig's head thwacked against metal, and he passed briefly into night.
	Some moments later, teeth aclatter and echoing against thin metal walls, Stig awoke. 
	He stood and spun, seeing nothing but his own distorted reflection. The mirror walls were half again his own height, but he found he could jump and just barely see out of the basin, landing each time with a tinny boot-tink.    
	The first jump saw the blade-wielding giant, dressed in periwinkle robe, a mask over its muzzle. On the second, he saw at least two others abreast a long table. And on the third, a hint of curly blonde hair.
	Golden Fleece on the head of an angel.
	He gasped and ducked behind the still-steaming Bezoar.
	The sight of the golden locks--in situ--that is what gave him the strength to do what he did next.
	Wincing at the sacrilege, Stig climbed atop the Bezoar and gazed down upon the breathtaking face of his sleeping Miss Cindy, his liegess, lying helpless under the knife of this horrid giant and its gaggle of hench-colossi. An enormous tray of torture implements left no doubt as to their intentions.
	Stig winced.
	The effort of the jump and climb had warmed him some, but his hands were still chilled to the bone and he reached for the medallion, hoping it might be hot again.
	In so doing, Stig's trembling knocked the lock hair out of his chest pocket. He cursed and bent to pick it up, his mind incised with the image of the blade that had severed it.
	The infernal giant destroyed everything!
	He scuttled back into the shadow of the Bezoar and squatted, chewing his woody-waxen nails tearing them to the quick. He was unfeeling of the pain, aware only of the abomination that was being performed upon his Heavenly Mistress incarnate. He tugged his beard hard, pulling his mouth into word shapes.
	The agoric cold seeped into his core as he wept into his fists, soaking the lock in brine.
	And suddenly, he cursed his stupidity.
	Of course! How had he not thought of it?
	The Bezoar--it would never be finished without a sacrifice.
	All these long years, his hands might well have been idle. He scowled at himself in chrome-distorted reflection.
	Yes. That was it!
	He stood straight and grasped the medallion for strength, but recoiled. No, he thought, no--although it had once seemed a harbinger of hairfalls, it had become a damnable distraction. Instead of working his way through the problem, he had poured his despair into the thick metal disk, fidgeting away his energy while the bezoar lay, unfinished.
	He cursed the day he found the medallion, tugged it out of his chest pocket, and tossed it clanking onto the floor of the basin. It gave a plaintive spark as he kicked it away.
	He had no more use for trinkets. 
	Steeling himself, Stig wrapped the bezoar's curly tail in his hand. The wiry silver of his lock of hair sparkled against the bezoar's golden fleece. 
	Chewing his tongue, Stig climbed up to the rim of the basin, straddled it, then lowered himself over the edge, inching down the felted hair, until he was standing on a sturdy blue linen pad.
	He pulled hard on the bezoar's tail, putting his whole weight into it. As the golden blobbiness rose above the basin's lip, it wobbled, tottered, and finally fell, landing with a plop.
	His chest heaving, he surveyed his options. Below, a pocket of Cindy's gown hung providentially open. He reflexively reached for the medallion to think--but shook off the spell. How to get the bezoar down there? 
	No time to ponder.
	Time to do. 
	He toed the edge of the fabric and imagined hurtling himself onto her thigh. No. Wait. The gargantuan tray of torture implements lay spread like a market below. That would be an easier jump, but it would be a long fall if he missed. 
	He lowered the bezoar over the great blue linen cliff. He followed with his feet, shimmied until is legs hung far out over the blades, and kept going until he was bent at the waist.
	Biting his lip, Stig thought back how far he'd come with Cindy's help, then pushed off and tumbled through open air. 


Scene 4. THE FROG & THE BATTERY

	The hospital is going to sleep. Cindy and Nurse Noris walk in quiet. 
	At the room, the nurse says "It's quiet time now."
	Cindy nods, hesitates.
	"No chewing, alright?"
	"Okay," says Cindy.
	"Pinky swear?"
	She holds out her little finger, noticing the nurse holds out the one that had just been in her nose. Cindy hesitates, but links anyway, then they snap aScene and she tiptoes in and crawls under the covers.
	As the door's light narrows and closes, she runs her hands over her body, her face...her head, where mom has long since lopped off her curls.
	She goes to pull the golden strands across her lips, but comes up short.
	Queasy with hunger--she wasn't allowed anything but water since dinner--somehow the hollowness inside her helps her to focus. If Sad Snufflepuss were here, they could sing a song together.
	"When the sun...goes down...the sun goes down..." she tries to sing, but it's not the same. 
	She feels her lips, her cheeks, her nostril hairs, up the bridge of her nose--she's aware of her fingerwanderings, more aware than ever, more aware too of the errant hairs in her eyebrows. Some of them are growing in stubby from the last time she plucked them with dent-worn fingernails.
	Her eyelids too, are punctuated with stubby lashes, poking at the tips of her fingers. They feel wrong, and she can't resist yanking one out.
	Instead of putting it to her lips, she rolls that one hair between thumb and forefinger, then thumb and middle, thumb and ring, thumb and pinky, until she falls asleep.

	"Wake up hon," says the nurse.
	Before Cindy can comply, she feels the bed lurching across the room. She forces open her eyes, and hallway light blinds her. The nurse jostles the bed into a queue along the wall and confers with a doctor.
	Under the sheets, Cindy runs her fingers covertly over her pubes, selecting one to pluck and roll. She doesn't do anything else with it, just flicks it away after bit. Whether she's under disapproving gazes or not, the unconforming hairs have to go. Like slivers stuck in skin, they must be plucked. She strokes the fuzzy patch, almost disappointed by how smooth the hair lay.
	The doctors don't seem to notice, so she continues until they've pushed her down the hall, through a battery of swinging doors.
	Nurse Noris touches her arm and says, "No picking, okay?"
	Cindy nods and stops stroking the patch.
	When the nurse leaves, she carries on stroking but doesn't pick. Surrounded by dial-turning anesthesiologists and nurses, hand-washing doctors and pinging machines, she tugs--tempted--but refrains.
	The scene is already disorienting but when a soft plastic ring seems to grab her face she gasps, struggles. A funny smell.
	The doctor's old-man voice says, "Breathe, Cindy. Count backwards from one-hundred."
	Sinking fast, she tries to remember what number to start with.
	"Ninnnety--eigh--" and numbers fade to dancing sparkles. Down through the soft gauzy, she sinks.

*   *   *

	Gunpowdery wick-smoke prickled her nostrils. She felt the frog's choking fear. Leigh tossed the poor thing on the lawn but, stupefied or defiant, it refused to hop away.
	"It's not all the way in," protested Clayton.
	The both of them--and Cindy too, in spite of herself--were enthralled by what happened next.
	Smoke poured from the frog's forced-open mouth. It looked like it was puffing a fat stogie. In a moment, thick bluey whiteness engulfed it altogether, then slowly dissipated through the forest of grass.
	The frog looked dead. Foggy eyes, its snout yellowed and browned.
	"It was supposed to blow up, Clayton! Let me see that," Leigh said, snatching up the limp animal. 
	"Look," he pointed at the firecracker, "It's a smoker. You got the wrong one."
	Clay shrugged. "Make Cindy eat it," he chirped.
	She screamed and tripped to run, but they tackled her, six knees thudding into damp ground.
	She was used to negotiating her tortures, so after token resistance, she agreed to hold the frog in her mouth for a few minutes just to appease them.
	Larry's voice creaked from the kitchen window.
	"No roughhousing with your sister."
	"We're not roughhousing," said Leigh, "we're just playing."
	"You okay, Cindy?" called Larry's shadow in the window. 
	Leigh made a fist and shot a look at her.
	Mmm-hmmmm," she around the frog in her mouth.
	Larry came out through the screen door and held out his hand. 
	"Spit it out," he said.
	The frog squeaked as Cindy pulled it out of her mouth and put it in Larry's dishwashy palm. This was the usual extent of Larry's intervention, and Cindy never told mom about the incident, just as she never told her about countless other wrestling matches, noogies, and forced-feedings. Doled out with fists and Dixie cups, she had learned to take her brothers' torments. 
	The only time Cindy had ever told on them was later that day, and only then because she thought she was going to explode.
	Larry sighed and tossed the frog down. 
	Cindy ran off and hid by the electrical box behind the neighbor's house. 
	When they caught up with her, Clay held her down, and Leigh shoved some kind of little metal tablet between her lips, forcing aScene her teeth, and holding her mouth shut.
	"Swallow it!" He shouted in her face, "Swallow it!"
	So, she swallowed it. It wasn't even a hard swallow. Not compared to some of the other things they'd made her force down.
	It had taken a few hours before she started feeling sick.
 *   *   *
	Don't be so picky, Cindy," said mom.
	Dinner was watery clumps of instant mashed potatoes served in mismatched bowls on the coffee table.
	Cindy rubbed her bellybutton, stroking the peach fuzz that fringed it.
	"My belly hurts," said Cindy.
	She saw Leigh elbow Clayton, throwing a head-point in her direction.
	"Fine," sighed mom, "go lay down on your bed."
	Cindy got up to go, but her heart sank when Leigh also excused himself to use the toilet.
	Of course, he followed Cindy into her bedroom. 
	"You got a staring problem?" she shot, slamming her door.
	It bounced back open and Leigh leaned into her room.
	"You're gonna explode," he whispered.
	"Nuh-uh. Shut up."
	"You ate electricity. It's in the battery. You're gonna explode."
	It wasn't even a taunt. He said it flat as a fact, almost wistful. She wanted to blow him off, but there was a pit in her stomach.
	And it burned.
	She ran past him, back into the living room, shouting over mom's Glee.
	"Leigh said I'm gonna explode!"
	Mom seemed to sag just a little further than usual. Without taking her eyes off the TV, she said, "What are you talking about, Cindy?"
	"They made me eat a bat'tery, and I'm gonna essplo'ode," cried Cindy, hiccoughing. "When we were playing! They made me eat a battery! I swal'lowed i'i'i'ittt."
	"Oh god," said mother, dumping her plate of potatoes as she stood.
	She grabbed Cindy by the arm and dragged her to the bathroom.
	"Throw up," she yelled, pushing Cindy at the toilet.
	Cindy stood shivering while mom tore through the medicine cabinet.
	"Where's the ipecac? Where the hell's the fucking ipecac?" muttered mother.
	Cindy didn't tell her that Clayton and Leigh had made her drink it ages ago.
	"I can't throw up," said Cindy.
	Mom sat down on the tubside and bent Cindy over her knees. She stuck two fingers into Cindy's mouth. She felt herself gagging, and mom forced her fingers deeper, into Cindy's throat, deeper, until she dry-heaved.
	They struggled in the bathroom until way past Cindy's bedtime, but in all of it, nothing came up except thick saliva and blue Kool-Aid.
	When mom finally sent her to bed, her stomach was feeling less sore--or at least, a different kind of sore--and she was getting hungry. Sitting up in bed, exhausted but wide-awake, Cindy could hear mom talking on the phone for a long time, becoming slowly less frantic.
	Cindy chewed her nails til they bled, hugging Sad Snufflepuss's squishy formlessness.
	When mom appeared in the bedroom doorway, she hid her hands under the covers.
	"The doctor says you should be alright. If you have to poop, poop into these," said mom, putting a box of sandwich bags into Cindy's lap.
	"I'm hungry," said Cindy.
	"Just go to sleep."
	Leigh and Clay eased up for a while after that, but by the time mom stopped collecting the baggies of poop, they were back at it with the sticks and stones and everything else, and Cindy never told on them again.


Scene 5. CHOOSING BLADES

	His red beard had nearly reached the century mark, and had begun to grow gray. The smelly little chamber had been cramped as usual by a bolus of semi-dissolved cricket and mosquito carcasses. He had tried to dress the frog's stomach up a bit, even blocking his quarters off with a lovely swinging door made from dragonfly wings.
	But decades moving from toad to toad, frog to frog--and the odd toadstool now and then to be sure--well, that might have satisfied some gnomes. Not Stig. 
	He had practiced a great many crafts. Since his first apprenticeship cutting gold leaf for the Elder Council's shoehorns to his short flirtation with brandymaking, Stig had thrown himself into work. And though he excelled at these endeavors, boredom inevitably set in. 
	He was young, the elders told him. He would find satisfaction in the crafts in due time. If not brandymaking, then glass-cutting perhaps, or tinkery.
	Even his fourth-to-the-eighth granduncle, Magnus--whose kindness bordered on pederasty--had warned against overhigh aspirations: Patience, dear boy, and diligence. You can't carve Fanfantil's Comb in a century, you know.
	But none of the advice had touched that place of longing deep inside him. And only after long isolation in this toadsbelly had his own self-understanding crept in like slow fungus. It wasn't... mere accomplishment that he sought. The haughtiest craftsmanship was for naught if it served only the gnomish base need for finery. Toiling away at arts for its own sake was such a conceited practice. To what end? He might as well rot. And so he did.
	But the smoke attack had changed all of that.
	Not an hour after he'd pinioned his swinging doors in place, the toad's gullet was inundated by thick smoke.
	He'd yanked the doors shut, staring through their veiny clarity, wringing his hands, as the smoke rose like floodwaters.
	Before he could react, the whole chambe had shuddered around him and seized up, crumpling the doors and smashing Stig's face into the sticky bolus.
	He flailed as bile bubbled around his ankles, legs, hips. The bolus lurched up the gullet, dragging Stig along with it.  
	Through the esophageal canal, he was expelled, screaming. When he emerged, flailing onto his belly, he found himself perched upon a squishy pink expanse. The floor was soft and warm and ringed by shiny white monoliths protruding from ceiling and floor. The toad stared dull-eyed into Stig's besmirched face.
	Then, it had slid away from him. The monoliths spread open and bright light engulfed. Through the opening, he could see the ruddy face of some kind of giant.
	Too late, Stig realized the toad's tongue had him by the bell of his shoe. Stig hugged the bolus for anchorage, but he was dragged into hideous daylight sun.
	Over the monoliths he'd gone, swung down, and dangled high above a razor-sharp forest of grass, on a long string of sticky saliva slung between the toad and the giant pink lips of a...a...a...
	Well, that's odd.
	The distil end of the spittle emanated from a glorious, golden-haired cherub of a giant. Stig's heart swelled, in one crystal moment, tingling with Purpose.
	But he was slipping.
	He mime-roped the saliva, losing traction and spinning in the air.
	Through his washed-out day vision he saw other giants standing in conference.
		The rotund one held out its hand and thundered in giantspeak.
	The only syllables Stig could pick out--and only because the giant's tone rose sharply when he spoke them--were: Sin-dee.
	Despite its size, the its ginger-gray beard had seemed singularly unimpressive. Its hairs had glinted in the daylight, but it was no more than a season-beard.
	The angel-giant had reached the toad away from her mouth, stretching the saliva string taut. Just when he had thought he would be pulled apart, she slurped up the spittle, and Stig had found himself back inside her warm pink mouth. 

*   *   *

	Now, tumbling through the air with the bezoar in his arms, he steeled himself for impact. The bezoar cushioned his fall, but his landing on the dark blue linen flung a huge set of scissors clanking across the floor below.
	The hands of giants passed a few hatswidths above his head, and he scrambled to hide. He ducked into another rolled dark blue cloth, yanking the bezoar with him.
	Within, he found a curved set of pincers with a toothed ratchet on the handle. Stig held his breath while a giant's hands fumbled nearby. When they had gone, he peeked out of the fabric.
	The coast was clear, and he felt safe to leave the bezoar behind in the cloth while he surveyed the array of gigantic brushed steel implements. A few thumbflicks confirmed all were intolerably dull by gnomish standards.
	What he would give for a good blade of grass!
	Resigned, Stig selected one with a detachable blade. He quickly puzzled out the clipping mechanism that held the blade to the broad metal haft.
	"That will just have to doodley-do," he whispered. Then he stole away under the giant rag again, crossed himself, and started shaving his head with the arm-length blade.
	His head hair wasn't as long has his beard, but the pile in front of him was big enough to do the trick. He started to gather it.
	No, he thought. He would have to go the extra yard. This was for Cindy. He put the knife to his throat, and tears of joy rolled down his cheeks as he shaved. 
	When all was said and done, he ran his hands over his bald chin and head. He giggled, feeling like a baby. Then he crawled back to the bezoar.
	Looping the long strands around each forefinger finger and each thumb, he began tucking loose ends of hair under the golden fleece of the bezoar, coiling them into and out of the fabricky surface.
	Stig wove in the ancient gnomish mode at first, fast as he could, precisely ornate. But as he went, he added original flourishes, spinning paisley florals into rococo scenes, coiling elven ivy tendrils into dwarven knotwork, and inventing new styles whole cloth as the mantle transmorphed into artisanal fantasy.
	He adorned the bezoar with endlessly intricate fripps and fandangles.
     Gradually, the bezoar took on the aspect of a curio egg, surpassing even the Elder Council's great shoe horns. 
	He looked past his fingers, astonished at his own ingenuity. Never had such magnificent splendor emerged from gnomish hands.
	Its golden surface scintillated with silvery scrollwork of his own hair.
     Stig grasped his chest, a thrill dancing across his chilled skin.
     He took a deep breath, then screamed at the top of his lungs, " I SHALL DO RIGHT BY YOU MY GODDESS!"
     Suddenly, the thick blue cloth was whipped off of him, knocking him to his knees. One of the giants shrieked, but Stig kept his head. He wrapped the tail of the bezoar round his forearm, heaved it off the torture tray, and wrenched his legs against the metal's edge.
	He jumped and sailed weightless across the gap.
	The stomach-curdling flight invigorated him. His senses were crisp.
     He crooked his arm just right, and snagged the pocket of Cindy's thin gown.
	The full weight of the bezoar yanked his other arm out of its socket, knocking the wind out of him. 
	But he gritted his teeth.
	So. Close.
     He dangled, took a deep chest full of air, then hefted the bezoar safely into the pocket.
     Relieved and dazed, Stig began to pull himself in behind it.
	But when he saw a giant hand coming to swat, he knew he had to draw attention away from the beauty of the Great Cindean Bezoar.
     He rubbed his stubbly chin against his distended shoulder, and smiled.
     Then he let go and tumbled to the floor.
     A voice boomed from above, "What on Earth?"
     And a giant foot came down on Stig.


Scene 6. THE CROC CAPER
	
	"Commence mission: Purse Snatcher," she whispers. 
	She pulls her hands out of her gown and slides out of bed. 
	She moves deliberately across the room, feeling the cool floor through her hospital booties. Though dim, the hallway lights make her wince a little. 
	The floor's reception area is quiet except for the sound of typing. 
	She won't be able to get past the lady behind the counter without being noticed, so she goes up and asks, "What's for breakfast tomorrow?"
	"Honey, it's lights out. You need to go back to your room."
	"Sorry. I'm just hungry and I want to know what's for breakfast. Please?"
	The lady sighs and shuffles papers, then hands a faded xerox across the counter. "Here's the menu," she says. "Now, get to sleep young lady," she says with a warm smile.
	Cindy shoves the paper into her gown pocket and leaves the desk, going in the opposite direction from her room.
	When she gets to the waiting room, Dan is picking out the cashews from the mixed nuts bowl and mom looks up from her paranormal romance and gives her that look.
	"Cindy, what are you doing here? You need to get some sleep."
	"I can't sleep," says Cindy.
	"Well, I don't know what you want me to--"
	"Will you sit up with me?" 
	That look softens just a little, and she says "Fine," then gets up to walk Cindy back to her room.
	"Don't forget your bag," says Cindy.
	Back in the room, Cindy pretends to sleep until she hears mom's light snore, then she cracks her eyes open and peers at mom's bag with a sly smile.
	Flowers grow across the raffia purse in ruddy yellow and red, brown, and white. The worn-shiny suede trim of mom's crocs peeks over out the top. 

*   *   *
	
	When nurse Noris wakes her up, she hops out of bed, feeling the slight squish of the crocks on the floor. She smiles as they walk out of the room. 
	Cindy opens her mouth to make small talk, but she realizes she's been picking at her face and she purses her lips again.
	She stars down at the nurse's purple crocs.
	"You like my crocs?" says the nurse.
	Cindy considers as she dances her fingers through her hair, feeling for the scab she'd picked at the night before.
	"Yeah, I guess. They're not as cool as the pink ones I have at home, but they're still pretty cool," she says.
	"I like yours too," says the nurse as they approach her room.
	Cindy takes her hands off her head.
	"These are my old ones," she says. "Mom wouldn't let me wear the pink ones because the jibbitz keep coming out. They're Hello Kitty."
	The nurse's face splits with a smile.
	"Yeah, but old shoes are so comfortable," she says. "Like a nice, worn-in bed."
	Cindy screws up her face.
	"You know," says the nurse, "a bed that's sagging in the middle, but it's sooooo soft."
	She stops and turns her foot so Cindy can see the Smurfette figure on the side.
	"I've got a jibbitz too," she says.
	The two of them walk down the hall, crocs squeaking in unison.
	"You're a pretty lucky girl," says the nurse. "Getting out of school like this."
	Cindy shrugs, crosses her arms intently, feels for the stubble in her pits.
	"You know, when I was young, I used to bite my nails," says the nurse. She waves her hands.
	"Your nails are perfect," says Cindy. Sparkling flowers of purple and gold.
	They walk silently through the hospital-at-night chill.
	They turn the corridor and arrive at Cindy's room.

*   *   *

	"Can we speak in private, Ms. Strunk?" says the doctor, taking his clipboard from Nurse Noris. 
	"We're all family," says mom flatly.
	Larry pokes into a pot pie he got from the hospital's cafeteria and waves the steam away, then pops open a bag of Doritos.
     "Okay, then. Well, I just wanted to let you know we've removed the blockage and there doesn't seem to be any sign of further obstruction."
     "Good. Will it come back?"
     "Well, unless we're successful at curbing the behavior, eventually I'm afraid it will."
     Mom rolls her eyes and picks up her purse.
	Nurse Noris clears her throat. 
     "Ah, yes. There is one more thing that worries me, " says the doctor.
     "Yes?"
     "I really think we should speak in pr--"
     "Just spit it out."
     "Okay, well. Ah...in addition to the bezoar, we removed what appears to be a watch battery."
     Leigh's eyes go wide and Clay shudders.
     "A battery?" asks mom, and she casts a blazing glance across the boys.
     The doctor nods, "We'll have to speak about it in therapy."
     "Yes," says mother, her eyes softening as she places a hand on Cindy's shoulder gently. "Yes, we will."
     "Come on, Larry. Let's go."
     "You go along. I'm going to finish my pot pie."
     Mom sighs.
     "Fine," she says, "but don't fall asleep on the bus again."
 	When they get to the car, Leigh calls shotgun, and Clayton protests.
	"Give your sister the front seat," says mom. 


Scene 7. THE LEGS OF GIANTS

	The giant's foot slammed Stig against the floor, but didn't crush him. Instead, he squooshed up into it. His fingers found purchase in the purple foam even as he was pressed against the floor over and again with each gigantic step.
     Eventually it stopped, and Stig relaxed, still squashed against the shiny floor.
     The giant shifted its weight from foot to foot, affording Stig the chance to work his way free.
	Sprawling on the floor, his first sight upon struggling up to his hands and knees was a strange sculpture attached to the giant's foot. He sneered at the yellow-haired, blue-skinned abomination and rubbed his eyes, hoping it would go away.  
     When at last was able to stand and survey his predicament, his heart dropped.
	Silhouetted in the watery white light of this vast cavern, his miss Cindy's great golden locks departed down an apparently endless corridor, hand-in-hand with a tall lady giant.
     He wanted to scream, No! but all he could manage was a sad "Adieu."
     "Miss Cindy," he wept. "Adieu."
     He stood for a long moment, letting the sounds of the giants wash over him, unattentive to the great feet that slammed down on all sides.
     When at last he could take it all in, he took his hat from his newly bald head, and rubbed his beardless chin. The chill squeezed his bones. Nothing but taupe blandness and the legs of giants.
     He rested against a wall and only noticed after it moved that he'd been leaning against a giant foot.
	Flustered, he turned around, and high above, he espied a glittery shimmer.
     Hunched over a gargantuan glass table and feasting on some kind of bright orange flatbreads was a giant he recognized. And in the beard he now had to admit was rather superior to his own, tiny nixies rejoiced, flinging handfuls of orange powder.
     The ginger-gray giant reached into his silven feasting sack again, and Stig thought maybe, just maybe, he could climb his way in there.

The End

Afterward to "Chewie" by ChatGPT 4.0 13.Nov.2024

This story shows a narrative shift into fantasy, with earthy humor and a sense of the grotesque. McKnight gives readers an immediately tactile setting: "Stig sat up in bed, shaking and ashamed. The scantily clad beard-nixies fled as his dream haze cleared." Here, Stig’s peculiar relationship with his world, both familiar and magical, begins to unfold in vivid detail. McKnight’s use of language mirrors Stig's blend of wonder and frustration, particularly in Stig’s interactions with his "beloved Miss Cindy" (whose belly he apparenly inhabits), an almost deity-like figure embodied by a bezoar—a massive hairball that he adores and venerates.

The humor and absurdity are amplified by Stig’s solemn devotion to a grotesque object, adding an undercurrent of both satire and pathos to his character. The scene in which he reverently attempts to weave the sparse hairs into the bezoar demonstrates McKnight’s capacity to evoke emotional resonance from the absurd, showing Stig’s loyalty and dedication despite the ridiculousness of his task. McKnight’s pacing here is gradual, allowing readers to fully engage with Stig’s bizarrely devout existence before escalating the tension when the bezoar is abruptly seized by a giant. The fantastical nature of McKnight’s settings blends a tangible sense of scale, tactile details, and humor to build a world as engaging as it is outlandish.

Afterward to Chewie by ChatGPT, November 13, 2024
Chewie encapsulates McKnight’s skillful blend of humor, fantasy, and existentialism. Stig’s grotto—a realm both protective and suffocating—serves as a metaphor for unyielding devotion and blind faith. Stig’s efforts to maintain the sacredness of Miss Cindy’s bezoar, despite absurd odds, reveal a critique of fanatical devotion. This devotion is endearing and humorous yet dark, suggesting that love and faith can become as consuming as they are noble.

McKnight’s detailed world-building draws readers into the tactile experiences of Stig’s world, where the environment itself—the grotto, the bile pool, the Bezoar—reflects Stig’s internal state of longing and doubt. The humor of Stig’s devotion to something as strange as a bezoar contrasts with his very real emotions, giving depth to his character and pathos to his journey. The story ultimately asks us to examine what we hold sacred and the boundaries of devotion.

TITLE: Crossingtide
(c) 2008 by Nathan McKnight 

Shepherd Quickdart fluttered out of the sediment, stirring up clouds of turbidity that startled the villagers. He smelled the perpetrator in the current from the cool river runoff. He knew his quarry well. He’d been chasing the polyamator as he skipped from estuary to estuary, trying to blend in before he committed his next cross-contamination. 
Quickdart slid along the bottom, quietly swimming upstream. The effort was invigorating. It reminded him of last Opposition's breeding season, when the currents of his home river were especially strong and cool. He missed its smell, missed making love to its waters. But his mind was pulled to the present when caught another whiff of the criminal, shivering with disgust at the thought of the polyamator spawning in *his* river. 
The sediment in this river was thick and murky, typical of the polyamator’s haunts. It made him cough, but he kept quiet as he slithered between the roots of the sand trees. Come breeding time, this estuary would be a throng of spawning families during Opposition, but this close to Passing, it was a ghost town. 
There was a sound to his left, a swish of water, and a startle of motion. Quickdart’s tail snapped, just grazing flesh, drawing blood, then darted in pursuit. The flow of the chase blurred his eyes even as it cleared his gills. The perp dashed under a log, then popped back out at Quickdart’s face and fled over his shoulder. Quickdart turned and pursued him back downstream towards the village, hearts beating in cascade. 
"Choke on sand, Shepherd," shouted the perp, before he disappeared into the crowded market. 

Quickdart swam upside down above the heads of the shoppers, trying to catch the polyamator, or spot him with his eyes. The behavior drew stares from the villagers. He heard the sound of a scuffle at the edge of the market, and darted towards it.

A bone merchant writhed in front of his hole, bleeding from a deep ventral wound. Quickdart rushed up to him and waved a stranger to go find a poulticer. "Which way?" he whispered to the dying merchant. But he was too far gone.

Quickdart swam into the merchant's hole, jangling bone scrapers and needles as he rushed through narrow corridors. There was nobody here, but the hole exited into a deep fauna-encrusted crevasse. A tiny tended garden swayed at the edge of the crevasse, beyond which cruising along the jagged bottom would be impossible. 

Quickdart cursed. Lost him again. 

But just then, he heard the gurgle of somebody struggling to hold their gills shut. 

"Give it up, polyamator," said Quickdart patrolling the edge of the garden, always keeping his body between the merchant's dark hole and the garish fauna. 

"My name is Leantail," he heard a voice say from behind a stand of polypflowers. 

"Whatever your name is, you're out of options. One way or another, it's surrender or fight, so you might as well come out now and we'll get this over with."

"I can still flee," said the criminal, his voice rasping with desperation.

"You've already fled from estuary to estuary, contaminating every river you've touched. Would you appreciate strangers spawning in your homeriver? There's nowhere left to flee."

"You don't get it," said the polyamator, "I'm from the outskirts of the great delta. My little stream is a cesspool from all the riff raff that show up for Opposition. And now, every year, my spawn mingles with millions. I can't even make myself make love to my own water. What am I supposed to do, roll over and die of gametosis?"

"Listen Leantail, I'm from the city too. It's hard, but you have to accept your home for what it is. I did, and so do most of us. And however hard your life is, that's no excuse for spawning in *multiple* streams."

"Well, lots of others die. My estuary is unlivable, my offspring are doomed if I spawn there. Hopping from stream to stream is the only chance I have. I can still flee," he repeated. 

"There's nowhere to go, pervert. Flee into the crevasse if you want. You'll get caught up in the firebranches or sucked up by a hexapus. I'd rather take you into custody, but if you want to kill yourself, that would solve my problem just as easily." 

"I can still go *up*. The worlds are passing tonight, I can cross to the frontier."

"Are you crazy? That's a long swim, even from here. By the time you get to Highpoint, you'll be exhausted. You couldn't possibly make the crossing alive."

"What do you care?"

"Because you're a polyamator and a killer, and I'll be damned if I let you get away, even to the frontier."

The garden was quiet but for the waving of bristleweeds. 

The perpetrator slithered from around the polypflowers. He was a sullen adolescent, his gills lined with dark streaks from mudhuffing. 

Quickdart approached cautiously, feeling sad for the kid. Nothing could end well for this one. He gave Leantail a sympathetic look, but reached out to loop a twine around his forefin. Just when he was within reach, the kid lunged, knocking Quickdart back. His dorsus stung with bristleweed quills and the kid fled into the bright water above. 

Quickdart's Shepherd training kicked in and he swam into the sky in persuit. The village vanished in the depths, and he could hear the astonished gasps of shoppers below. 

If this kid was serious about crossing, he either had some serious grit or he was suicidal. The prospect terrified Quickdart, and he was a trained Shepherd of the Law. When the frontier planet passed overhead and the twin seas kissed, there were only a few minutes to make the crossing, and it was a struggle against a raging current that, when Passing was over, was flung out into space, a monstrous spray of rapidly-freezing seawater. Quickdart had heard stories of whole families ejected into space and raining down again, frozen into gigantic balls of hail. If the sparse reports from those rare souls who claimed to have crossed *back* were to be trusted, you'd find yourself on your own on another planet, in a barren land populated by society's rejects, with nowhere to return to during Opposition. No family stream. No home estuary. Quickdart shivered. 

It was insane, but every year one or two people tried. Most of them were thwarted by the Crossing Guard, and those who evaded them were usually killed in the process of the crossing itself. It was news whenever somebody actually made it across the sea bridge. And whatever happened to them after that, nobody knew.

Leantail thrashed violently, an inefficient swimmer, but he was young. And with his head start and Quickdart's bristle-stinging skin, he was far out ahead. As the hours rolled on, the kid shrunk to the distance, a dark speck soon to disappear. Quickdart whistled the call of the Crossing Guard and swam on.

He thought of his home estuary being polluted or overrun by throngs of strangers. What would he do? Would he keep trying to return home? Would he be able to make love to the water of his own familiar river once it was no longer sweet and cool? It would kill him inside to have to make love to murky water. But what could you do? You have to spawn. Everybody has to spawn. Opposition was half a year away, and the longing for high tide was strong. Quickdart let himself imagine for a moment what it would be like to spawn in a home estuary not his own. The smell of unfamiliar water, the keening of strangers. It made him feel dirty, queasy. He shook off the feeling, ashamed of thinking such sick thoughts. He would rather die of gametosis than commit such a cross-contamination.

He had been swimming for hour upon hour, and the light from the shallows above was dimming. This was the highest he'd ever swam, and he was feeling light headed. His eyes and gills tingled. The Crossing Guard should be close, but he had trouble whistling. A strange whine permeated the water. 

Quickdart strained to see what was happening above. He could see the annual eclipse starting, a massive shadow creeping across the water. 

"Leantail!" he shouted, "Give it up. This is crazy." 

He wheezed with the effort. The water up here was fizzy, and breathing it so hard was pure stabbing pain. Above, he saw saw a group of shadows swimming into the eclipse. As they converged, he started to catch up, and he could make out what was happening. They were going to apprehend the polyamator. Relief flooded over Quickdart.

In spite of the pain, the fizzy water seemed to be energizing, and he swam faster. He wanted to be a Scene of this one. He'd been persuing the polyamator too long to see him captured by volunteers. 

The frontier planet was a behemoth looming above. As its crescent became clearer, the blue of foreign seawater glinted brilliantly. The guards surrounded the kid before Quickdart could get there, and he felt a mixture of relief and disappointment. He slowed down to approach and let them do their job. The kid hovered motionless. He looked tired and Quickdart could see blood seeping from his gills. The guards were talking to him in reassuring tones.

Damnit, thought Quickdart. This wasn't the right approach. He swam up to the group, and one of the guards turned, surprised. 

"Grab him," shouted Quickdart.

But the kid used the distraction to stab two of the crossing guards between the eyes.

Quickdart surged forward and tackled the boy, narrowly dodging the sharp spine of his tail. They wrangled for a moment, but Leantail slipped out of his grip.

"You can't go much higher," shouted one of the guards as the kid swam for the sky with Quickdart in persuit. 

The kid trailed cloud of blood and adrenaline. 

The whine in the water was getting louder. The surface of the sea was in sight, and it roiled. He had never seen it so close before, but he'd always heard it described as mirrory and placid. This was anything but.

The water seemed to curve upwards, into a hill reaching towards the frontier planet above. Leantail swam up into the bulge as it stretched upward, thinner and thinner, like a tunnel. Quickdart could see great masses of water separating themselves from the sea surface and flying up towards the other planet. Currents swirled, carrying foamy white streamers and small fishes. At the surface, he could see floating tree branches and debris circulating. The kid was cornered again. 

"Don't come near me," he said. "I'll kill you."

Quickdart tried to grab him and Leantail tried to stab back, but both of them flailed as a surge of current swept them into the tunnel of water above. A roar rose and the water went white. 

Quickdart's eyes and gills felt like they were going to be pulled out of his head, and he swam upward, feeling frantically around for the kid. 

An alien smell washed over him, and the whiteness passed. Suddenly, he found himself in the center of a vast bridge of perfectly clear, ice cold water. To one side, a planet. To the other side, a planet. Leantail curled, fetal in the middle of it, surrounded by stars. Quickdart's hearts beat in cascade, and he took the kid under his fin. 

From the position of the sun, Quickdart was able to determine which world was his own, and he began to swim towards it. 

The swimming was hard in this corridor, and his fins felt like they would freeze. He swam towards the whitewater below, tugging the unconscious kid along. 

Though he was battered and exhausted, he couldn't help but feel struck through by the beauty of swimming in the sky. He could see the entire world spread below. The blue of ocean, the long peninsula of the continent stretching over the horizon. It was terrifying.

Suddenly, the ocean bridge constricted, and Quickdart instinctually pushed the kid out ahead, towards home. The effort pushed his own body back, towards the frontier. He had felt weightless, now he felt like he was falling, and he swam forwards behind Leantail's limp body. 

In an instant, the wall of ocean surface appeared in front of him. He could see Leantail on the other side of it, in a wobbling tail of water extending to the home world. And he knew he was not going home. Forgetting what that meant, Quickdart turned and swam for his life. The frontier world below seemed to be getting bigger, the water seemed to be getting colder. He glanced back at his home, but was startled by the snap of sea surface closing off again, separating the tongue of water into another floating globe behind him, then another, and another. He imagined himself getting caught in one of those, to freeze into a giant ice ball and spend the rest of eternity in the ice ring around the sun.

He swam as hard as he could into the unknown below, foreign seawater engulfing him. The foamy expanse of whitewater loomed, and then with a concussion that deafened him and knocked his gills shut against his sides, a wall of sea surface cut him off ahead. He reeled. The waterscape around him had coalesced into a wobbling sphere. He swam in a little circle, seeing there was no way out. After a lifetime in open water, it was deeply confusing. The seconds ticked forwards over what seemed like ages. The frontier planet below was growing rapidly, and home was shrinking rapidly. The surface of the mini-sea he floated in seemed to be calming as it fell, and white bubbles of ice seemed to be multiplying along the leading edge. 

Quickdart felt his muscles weakening, and his Shepherd training kicked in. He swam in wide circles, skimming the edge of the sphere, hoping to keep himself, and the water around him from freezing. He couldn't feel his body at all, but he could tell he was moving. Dizzy, nearly blacking out, he swam, swam, swam. Through foggy vision, he saw the frontier planet expand out, flattening.

Then a crushing pressure wave knocked him unconscious. 

The smell of the home estuary filled his gills. He floated, serene. 

Then he blinked awake, bobbing in the sea. Claps of hail thundered in the distance. A surge of flotsam struck him, and he felt bones crunching in his side. He panicked, swimming down. The hailclaps sounded louder and louder. A ball of ice plunged past his face, sinking deep, and he kept swimming. Another plunged past, trailing bubbles. He swam on. His body was numb but his gills felt hot. Huge hail boulders plunged on all sides, and others rushed up from below, flying back towards the surface. The sounds of cataclysm wracked his body, the cracking of ice like the cracking of enormous bones. He wasn't sure if it was the hail stones smashing against each other, or his own body being beaten pulpy. 

Darkness closed. Warm water felt like burning, but it was good. He swam towards the seabottom, the echoes of Passing quieting behind him. He swam as deep as he could until he found a warm current, then relaxed, hoping the current would carry him somewhere. Some foreign estuary he could make his home. 

<<<END OF STORY / START OF NEXT>>>




TITLE: The Damps
(c) 2006 By Nathan McKnight




Mr. Eberts drove through the night to make sure he arrived in Straitsville right at sun up. He figured he'd need most of a day's work to get the deal done, and he wanted to be out of there come nightfall. Already, he looked over his shoulder at every knock of the engine and flutter of the heart.  
*   *   *
"She's been running from that fire her whole goddamn life," said Mr. Eberts, "What makes you think she's going to stop running now?"
"Please, Bill," said Mr. Ambrose. "It would mean an awful lot. That woman practically raised me." 
Mr. Eberts finished the last crumbs of his lemon chiffon.
"Okay," he said, "but if I talk to Lizzie Green, you have to buy every last drop of Hock-Hocking I've got left."
"Hang on a sec," injected J.M., leaning over from the little window booth, "You ain't really going up to Lizzie Green's? That place ain't right, I tell ya. You're like as not to get snuffed out by mine gasses. And all that sulphur in the air--I'm telling ya--it'll eat yer eyes right outta yer head."
"This whole dang town smells like sulphur," said Mr. Eberts, "Gives me the creeps. It's why I left."
Eberts pushed his plate away.
"Don't have much choice," said Eberts. "If I don't sell this beer--and quick--I'm out of a job."
"The way I hear it, you're out of luck any case," said J.M., waving his fried egg sandwich.
"Maybe," said Eberts, "but if I unload my last few cases, I'll get my severance, and a crack at a position in the new company."
"New company?"
"The brewery's thinking of switching to soda pop come the new year."
That brought a guffaw that blew the whipped cream off J.M.'s milkshake.
"Nobody's gonna be drinkin soda pop, Mr. Eberts. If they really take away whiskey, folks around here'll just switch to mine rum."
"It's a done deal," said Eberts, turning back to Mr. Ambrose, "Prohibition is coming. So, what about us? Do we have a deal?" 
He stuck out his hand, Ambrose yanked it toward him, Eberts yanked it back, they shook high, shook low, and ended in an Indian-style "How!"
Just like old times.
"One last thing," said Mr. Eberts. 
He scribbled out a note on the back of his napkin. 
"Could I ask you to send a telegram to Hocking Brewery?" he said.
Ambrose took the napkin and read it aloud: "SALE CONFIRMED SEND THREE TRUCKS PAREN. SEVEN-FIFTY CASES CLOSE PAREN. STRAITSVILLE. A-S-A-P."
*   *   *
He could feel the breath of the ghost horses on the back window of his car as he sped up the road to Dutch Ridge. They had haunted him all his life, but the ghost hooves pounded on his trail as he fishtailed around a gravelly corner. 
He shook his head, took one hand at a time off the wheel to shake out the knucklewhite. He never should have returned to New Straitsville. 
A veil of smoke encompassed the car, and Eberts cursed. The mine fire had been burning since '94. He'd only been eleven at the time, but he way he remembered it, the Sun had died the day the striking workers started the fire. Not long afterwards, Grampa Lisander had died fighting for the town that vilified him. Family enmity burned long in these parts.
Twenty-five years later, the mine fire was still going too, spewing thick smoke from an ever-shifting landscape of fissures and sinkholes. 

When the gravel ran out, Mr. Eberts and slipped out of the lightly-worn front seat of his company car. He would miss the 1917 Pierce-Arrow.
The slam of the door wumphed softly against the low, smoky sky. Mr. Eberts held his handkerchief over his mouth and hiked an unnamed trail up to the last place he remembered Lizzie Green living. She'd never go far. Even as the ground burned, she'd refused to abandon Dutch Ridge. Just kept moving back and forth, scrounging together a new little shack each time an old one burnt down or was inundated by the damps. 
His shoes weren't made for hiking, and his slacks caught on burrs along the way through the coal-stinking woods.
After a while, he ran across the trace of the coal seam on the ground. It was a thick line, exuding the stench and heat of a coal oven, broken in places by slumping dirt. Following Dutch Ridge, he had to cross that line.
Eberts sidled up to one of the dirt slumps and dug his foot in as best he could. With teeth gritted and muscles tensed, he crossed the seam. His shoes steamed with sweat.
He kept the handkerchief tight over his mouth, but it was quickly was blotted black, despite repeated refolding. 
When he reached the top of the ridge, the Sun was a dull red glow in the black sky. The sound of galloping rushed up behind him. He turned, but nothing was there except a dark billow of smoke.
The lights of a cottage glowed through trees queerly tilted trees. He felt a quaking.
"Eeeeberrrrrts!" the shriek came from everywhere, and he knew it was the voice of Shep Tinker, coming to reclaim his partner in crime. 
The ground below began to lift. At first, he felt like the whole world was bending. He jumped and looked back. It was just one tree falling over, its enormous root system tearing out of the ground, smoking, charred.
He approached the house, watery-eyed. 
Lizzie's was a tin shack ensconced in burr oaks and a glorious old gum tree. The remains of the wagon carriage Eberts remembered the long-dead Mr. Green and the Green girls riding around town had been reduced to a weedy stack of bone-dry planks. But one wheel still held together, leaning on the gum tree. Him and Ambrose had pissed on that wheel a thousand times. But they stopped doing it after Mr. Green was taken by the mine. 

Eberts took a leak behind the gum tree for old time's sake, calming his nerves. Where his stream hit the prickly brown tree droppings, the hiss of red-hot embers hissed ammonia steam.
"Whaddaya waunt, Billy?"
Eberts fiddled with his fly and spun, dribbling on his trousers.
"Oh, uh...Lizzie Green. Well, good to see you!"
Silence burned, then Lizzie said, "Tiredest lookin smile I seen all week. Well, don't just stan there--come awn in."
Eberts sighed and followed Lizzie into the dim shack. 
"I've come to talk to you about movin," he said.
"Oh naw you don't," she said, turning right round and pushing him back onto the porch. 
"Done had enougha y'alls commin up here tryna 'talk some sense inta me.' Whatdday'alls think y'gonna say y'ain't already sed? And here's me thinkin you was just commin out to catch up with poor old Lizzie Green," she scowled. "At'n wiz on mah tree. Why should I listen to some babified city slicker, son of a horse thief, anyway?"
Mr. Eberts flushed, the sound of a thousand sets of hooves chasing him through life exploded upon his memory. 
"Now see here, Lizzie Green..." he started.
"No, you see here, boy," she said in an ancient-sounding voice. "Yer family ain't nothing but lousy good-fer-nothin's right down to the person. Shoulda got run outta town before you was even borned."
	"I've had about enough of this," shouted Mr. Eberts, stepping up to take Lizzie by the arm. 
	She backed up, trying to close the door on him, but he pushed his way in.
	"Listen here, boy..."
	"No, you listen here. I am sick of you people disrespecting my kin cause-a your stupid hillbilly legends. My grampa warn't no horse thief, and even if he was, that was a hunderd years ago! I've taken the last lip from you that I care to. You're comin with me." 

As Eberts' spoke, his eyes adjusted to the dim, and he saw the one-room house was almost entirely taken up by an improvised distillery made from a welded battery of fish kettles and the belly of an old green enamel stove. Jars piled three high filled the floor, neatly labeled: MR. BENTLEY, JACK DONLEY SR., RALPH MACKENZIE.
Eberts gaped, picked one up: EDGAR
"Why, you've got a proper nip joint here," he said.
"It wasn't the damps kep me movin," she said, "was the spirits. They died in the fire, or in fightin it. All'a them. I was the only one heard their shrieks when the ground collapsed, their whimpers when the gas overtook 'em. An' they never forgot." She shuddered. "But they tried to take me. Every night, all night, they'd jump into my body, try'na take over, failin'. It was terrible, Billy. Ah tried'ta run, I moved'ta one house after'nuther, but they always found me, and the further I went, cuss'der they got. But I finally learnt. It was the damps'at kep'em, filled the mine'n gave'em life in death. I learnt'ta capture those vapors. In the mine rum," she said, gesturing at the jar in Eberts' hand.
"You captured the mine gasses in...moonshine?"
"It's moonshine, aight, but not juss. After I distill the shine, I run it through again, purify it, and I open the kettles to let the damps in. I realized, accidentally, I silenced one. I knowed'em all by name, an Doc Able weren't swirlin bout no more. He was in the jar."
Eberts peered into the jar he held, and though it was crystal clear, he could see the barest glint of pale orange light.
He shook his head, and said, "Lizzie," steeling himself. "Lizzie, you've finally gone off your rocker."
Quick as he could, Eberts seized her around the waist and swung her bony little body over his shoulder, and turned to leave.
But he was jolted by a crash and a splash on the back of his head. He fell to his knees, his lungs filling with the sting of moonshine vapors.
As Lizzie scampered away, Mr. Eberts staggered out the door and tumbled down the porch steps. He turned with his hands protecting his face. Lizzie stood, holding a jar ready to throw. 
Delicate tendrils of vapor coalesced as they rose from Eberts' face and chest. 
He braced himself against one of the big burr oaks, and said, "Lizzie, please. I only want what's best..."
A jar burst over Eberts' head, against the tree trunk. He ducked and scampered away on all fours across the hot dirt, but fell on his face, feeling as though a powerful wind had hit him suddenly on the back. He rolled over and gaped at the burgeoning cloud of vapors luminescing fire-orange amid the ever-present smoke.
The ground was hot. His back began to burn, and he leapt to his feet. The vapors had materialized into an apparition, sweeping through the air, into his mouth, then out the back of his head. It left him simultaneously burning and icy.
Eberts gasped as another jar whirred by, thwacking his ankle before bursting into flames on the smoldering ground.
"You see what it's like now! Now you see why I gotta trap'em. The still's the only way I know how. Runnin away never worked. No matter how far you go, Billy, they're gonna chase you down."
She hefted another jar of moonshine.
He tried to stand, but only succeeded in rolling over onto his side, painful, fetal. 
The jar on the ground in front of his face was effervescing on the hot dirt. Its label said: LISANDER EBERTS. 
He reached out, forcing his hand to grab the hot jar. Ethanol steam spurted from the lid, prickling orangey-blue flames where it touched the ground.
With all his strength, he wrenched the lid, and with a puff of pressure, the jar was open.
Wavering at the maw of the jar, golden flames formed a tormented face.
"Paw?" he said. 
With all his will, he brought it to his lips for a painful-hot kiss, a swallow, a gulp, another, and another. He felt the hot alcohol surge though his gullet. He could barely breath, but with the heat, his chest tightened, his muscles tensed, hard like anthracite, and he stood, unbreathing, monolithic. 
He felt the ground rumble, and heard a single set of hooves bearing down.
"Eeeeeberrrrtsss!" 
Lizzie staggered, hand over mouth, then darted into the shack.
The burning white smoke of a ghost horse reared up and stamped its wispy hooves on the ground in front of Mr. Eberts. Riding the horse was a shriveled-looking old man.
"So, I've finally caught up with you, Mr. Eberts" said the spirit. 
Against his will, Eberts opened his mouth, and in his grandfather's voice, he said, "What of it, Shep?"
"You took my property," the mounted phantom said, "now I'm going to have to take your life."
"I had to return them to their rightful owners," said the voice from within Eberts. "But the black damp got us just as we crossed the ridge."
The ghost of Shep Tinker sidled up to Eberts, and to his shock, he stood his ground, even as Tinker placed a spectral gun against his temple.
"Say your prayers, Eberts."
At that moment, Lizzie swung the door wide, and the light of the fire under the mash pot on her still flooded the clearing. 
Tinker and Mr. Eberts both swooned. He could feel the spirit of grampa Lisander being pulled out of him. With all his might, he tried to keep the ghost inside him, hugging the spirit with his arms, his chest, his soul.  
Just before he lost his grip, he saw the spirit of Shep Tinker pulled off its mount, twisting into the cabin. It swirled above the still, fighting the inevitable. 
And there was silence.
*   *   *
The refrigerated trucks rolled into the lot, reflecting lamplight onto the gravel.
"What do you mean, you didn't talk her down?" spat Ambrose.
"Trust me, Mr. Ambrose. I've got it covered," slurred Mr. Eberts, drunk on a couple of Manhattan cocktails.
"Yeah, well, you ain't made a sale yet, so wouldn'ya say bringin all this dang beer into town is a bit hasty?"
A crowd had gathered around the trucks, and the Hock-Hocking men unloaded the cases onto Ambrose's front porch.
"Aw, sshhut up and have a bheer," said Mr. Eberts.
"But you promised," protested Mr. Ambrose.
"Shhh..." said Mr. Eberts.
The door to Ambrose Lunch Counter tinkled open.
Ambrose started, mouth agape at seeing Lizzie, dressed in her Sundays.
"Hello, Mr. Ambrose," she said, setting a jar of moonshine on the countertop. "I believe Mr. Eberts has a different proposition for you."
