TITLE: The Hellfire Pigeon
(c) 2011 by Nathan McKnight
A story in 8 scenes.

Scene 1. HELLFIRE

"Hellfire!"
      That's what Pa had called it. I dropped the jar, shaking my finger in the air. It didn't hurt really; just a little sting. But it startled me enough to make me jump. I looked down. It wasn't broken, but I was dumbfounded for a second. 
      Then I picked up the glass-and-metal contraption from the ground and started rubbing my wool sweater on the rod that stuck through the thick cork. I?d nearly broken it, but this time when the snap! and the light pricked my finger, I didn't drop it. I was giddy. If Pa hadn't told me the story about finding the hellfire jar, I wouldn't know what it was, let alone what to do with it. 
      But this...well, this had potential. 
* * *
      "Hellfire!" Pa coughed, "That's what Gramps called it," Pa had said when I was a kid. "Him and Uncle Topher went out beagling like usual, and they took me and Pauli along with. That was before we started horsing. Every week, they?d send out the terriers to sniff out fox, with the beagles following them up, and me and Pauli on the little horses and Gramps and Topher on a double." 
      "Why didn't you all ride on a quad or an ostrichpillar?" I asked.
      "Naw Junior?that's what Pa called me when I was little?quads were too expensive back then, and there weren't no such things as ostrichpillars til I musta been, ohhh, twenty-fiveabouts. This was a good coupla years before Kennedy ended slavery, so even if we could afford that kind of thing, the Martinses wouldn'ta let us use them." Father took a wheezy breath and went on: "Anyway, there we were when the forward terriers caught a wiff of fox. They froze and started yippin, and the back terriers took off down a hole. You ain't never seen a foxhunt, so you won't know what that's like--I should send you over to the Johnses to learn--"
      I remember having rolled my eyes. Father was always trying to get me into the things he loved as a child. Foxing, birding... But beagling was for farmers. 
      "No way, Pops," I had said, "I?m a city boy. I was made for horsing." 
      Father had laughed and I sat forward on the edge of my seat.
      "True that, true that," said father. "Well, anyway, them terriers, they all went down a hole. All down the same hole. That's how we knew something strange was going on. A foxhole's a little thing, son. I only got to go a couple times before we stopped the hunts, but even so, I knew enough foxholes to recognize you couldn't fit twelve of any kind of hound down one burrow. Turns out, it wasn't no burrow."  
      "What was it?" I asked.
      "If I tell you," he said, "you have to keep it secret."
* * *
Scene 2. THE ROCK
      After school, the day after I found the jar, Brian caught up to me at the postal coop when I was rolling my transcripts. I had applied for every damn college in riding distance, but hadn't heard back from a single one of them. I was starting to get desperate. 
	"No pigeons for you again, huh?" Brian brushed his fingers through wild red hair. He knew me too well.
      I slid my transcripts into a tube and turned to the closest roadrunner. "Thirty-five cent," it squawked. Must?ve been a slow learner.
	I handed over thirty-seven cents and it snatched the tube from my hands before disappearing into the coop. 
	"Dude, I?m scared," I said to Brian. "Fifteen schools, no response."
	"That's because you applied for a bunch of out-of-state schools, didn't you?" said Brian.
	"Uh--yeah," I lied.
	"So, don't worry about it. Pigeons only fly so fast." 
	"Easy for you to say, you got into MID." 
      The Massachusetts Institute of Domestication was the top school in the country for breed/train majors. 
      "Get your mind off it," he said, "Me and Courtney are going to head out to the Rock tonight. Want to come?"
      "Naw, I?d be the fifth leg."
      "Courtney's friends with Ella, you know."
      "Ella?" I could feel my face warm. "Can you get her to come out with us?"
      "I can ask." 
      "Isn't she going out with...Lester McCoy?" I practically growled his name. I had an instant crush on Ella the first day of freshman year, but I learned right away that she was already going out with another boy, and I had spent every day since developing a deep loathing for Lester McCoy, who had beaten me to beautiful Ella. 
      "That was freshman year. I don't think she's going out with no one anymore."
	 
	I had to go back home to tell Pa I was spending the night at Brian's--and, of course, to stash the hellfire jar in my bag. No way I wasn't going to show this baby off tonight.
	I?d found the jar in Pa's study desk drawer, behind a stack of Cuban cigar boxes. Blackish grime formed a crust between the metal foil inside and the bubbly old glass outside. I had to look at it for a couple of minutes before I recognized it as the hellfire jar Pa had described, but I took it outside and, after having struck myself a couple times with the tiny lightning bolts, I took it to Brian's house. 
       
      "You decided to come, huh?" asked Brian. "We were about ready to leave without you," he said, indicating himself, Courtney, and a gawky girl in a denim skirt with puppy dog eyes. Ella. She made the hair on my arms stand up. 
      The Rock was a big gray slab of lichen-and-graffiti-covered stone jutting out of the ground in the woods up on Brink's Hill. It was about big enough to sit three people on top, and about high enough that you could break your ankle if you jumped off the top of it. There wouldn't have been anything special about it, except that who-knows-how-many generations of hot and bothered teenagers had projected their collective lust for life upon it. The overhanging side was carved with a couple hundred years? worth of ?Jason & Traylene? and ?McCreary 1842? and even one ?Don Noll + Raquel Quinn Francine Quinn?. On the overhanging side, there was a jutting natural bench, worn into a shallow saddle by generations of teenagers' behinds.  
      
      "I heard you and Lester wasn't going out anymore," I said to Ella on the way up the hill.
      "What's it to you?" she said. 
      We didn't say nothing else the entire rest of the walk, but we walked next to each other since Brian and Courtney spent the whole hike whispering, hand-in-hand. 
      When we finally got to the Rock, we were bathed in moonlight. Everything looked ashen.
      Brian started building a fire, and I couldn't wait to show off for the girls. 
      "Watch this," I said to Courtney.
      I rubbed the jar's metal rod on my sweater and snapped Brian's ass with it. He jumped right over the woodpile he?d been building, kicking it all over the place. 
      "What the hell was that?" he shouted.
      "Promise you won't tell."
      "I ain't promising nothing," he said.
      "Promise," I said, "or I'm not telling you."
      He snarled, but curiosity got the better of him, and I told the story of finding the jar in Pa's study, and repeated back Pa's story of finding the hellfire lab as best I could remember it. 
      
      By the time Brian had finished making his fire, it was pitch dark, and I had managed to squeeze my way between Courtney and Ella on the rock bench. He kept his eye on me, and I kept the jar on my lap, anticipating a well-deserved revenge. 
      Him and Ella disappeared behind the rock after a while, Brian making some excuse about "having to show her something" that sounded dirtier than telling the truth.
      Me and Ella talked quietly and stared at the fire, and so slow that I didn't realize it was happening, she nudged her body against mine. 
      I lifted the hellfire jar from my lap and sat it on the ground, and I felt naked, jittery, alive. I put my arm around Ella and gave her a kiss on the cheek.
      The smell of Ella's skin was fresh and soapy with a hint of sweat, and her knobby knees quivered every time I put a hand on one. Eventually, she nuzzled my neck and after a long, comfortable cuddle, we were making out. 
      
      When Brian finally got the better of me, I was lip-locked with Ella. He must have rubbed that jar good, because I felt the jolt in my back, and it jumped right through my thoroughly-wetted lips to Ella's. She screamed and stumbled off the rock bench. 
      When I got to my feet, I knocked Brian down and got the jar from him, and I felt a sense of accomplishment only long enough to turn back and see Ella scramble to her feet in the leaf litter, and look back in terror one time before darting off into the woods. I started after her, but she was gone and I standing alone in the dark, the cold of the rock bench still stinging my behind.
* * *
       "Way back when," Pa?d said, "back in the colonial days, there was this , this...cult. A cult of?" he struggled for a word "?a cult of witches, you know? You know...natur'lists?" he stumbled over the word. 
      I laughed out loud. I only ever heard that word in campfire stories. It went way back in the local lore, but to hear it said seriously like that, well, for a moment I thought all this talk was gonna build up into a regular ghost story. That would be like Pa. 
      But this time, he was dead serious. 
      "Nobody believes in naturalists," I had said after some consideration. 
      "That's what I used to think," said Pa, "before I went down that foxhole. But me and Topher, well, you wouldn't believe what we saw down there. Strange things. Things with spinning wheels and gilt scrollwork and if you touched a thing, it sent lightning right into your body. I never seen anything like it before or since."
      "What did you do?" I?d asked.
      "Well, me, I just gawked. But Topher picked up some jar. A queer thing, like a coffee pot lined with silver foil. He played like it was a genie's lamp, and you wouldn't believe it! Confounded thing stuck me with lightning! Well, the foxhounds sniffed all around, and one of them got burnt real bad right on his ear, and mixed with the burning hair smell, there was something else strange. Like the lightning had a queer smell of its own. About then, Gramps came cussin into the foxhole, shouting ?Hellfire! Get you boys out of here. This here's a hellfire lab?ratory!?"
      "A hellf?" I started, but Pa had shushed me with a knock of his cane?and I?ve still never said that word aloud either.
      "Don't even say it," he?d said. "Forget I even told you the word. Gramps woulda beat the dickens out of me if he?d heard me say it." 
      "But I don't get it," I said.
      "Best that way; don't you even worry about it. Point is, those stories?they?re true. So. So, you go to church and steer clear of anything queer."
      "But what did you do about the foxhole?"
      "Pa sold the land, put an end to the foxhunts. That's when we went into horsing. Best thing that ever happened to us."
      "Yeah, but what about all the hel?um, the strange things? Did you keep them?"
      "Of course not!" spat Pa. "What would we do a thing like that for? No, Gramps told the authorities but who?d believe a bunch of superstitious negroes?" 
      "So the...laboratory...is still out there?"
      "Probably all washed in by now," he said.
      "What about the?" I wasn't sure if I should say the word, "?the naturalists?"
      "Never saw them. For all I know, they?re still out there somewhere. Though, if I know Gramps, he kept a close eye on that place for a long time after that queer foxhunt."
* * *
      That day, I kept an eye out for Ella, but she didn't stop at her locker. In first period, a hall monitor dropped off a note to Mrs. Emmelheinz. Telling me to report to the office. Brian threw a wad of paper at me on the way out the door, and I played it off, but to tell the truth, I was pretty nervous. 
	I sat down beside the principal's office?Mr. Thresher was on the pipe, shouting into the funnel at the front desk, but he waved me in, so I got up off the bench and sat down again at the chair in front of his desk. It felt strange to get to sit in a grown-up chair only to be disciplined?I assumed'like a kid. I figured Ella's ma had complained and I was going to get a talking-to. They wouldn't suspend me for scaring Ella on accident, but even though it wasn't my fault at all, I was going to get an earful. Although, I worried, it was possible the hellfire jar might be something else entirely. Naw, I thought, Ella probably wouldn't have known what had happened. If I had never come across the notion of a hellfire jar, in all the books I?ve read, she wouldn't have known what hit her. I wished I could?ve explained it to her though.
	I snapped out of it when Mr. Thresher stopped his shouting into the tube. 
	"Kelly Barnett," said Mr. Thresher. He was a black man, almost as dark as me, but scrawny with a thin veneer of oiled hair and a pencil-lined moustache. 
	"Yes sir?" 
	"I have good news for you," he said.
	I was genuinely shocked. Was that sarcasm? All I could do by way of response was to cough politely.
	He pulled out a pigeon tube from his desk drawer, and handed it over. I noticed that the silver seal had already been broken, but I took it and unscrewed the metal endcap. 

"Dear Kelly G. Barnett," started the letter, "We are happy to inform you that your application to Ohio Agricultual University has been accepted..." it said.
	
	I was shocked, hopeful, overjoyed, terrified, and disappointed all at the same time. Ag U didn't have the same ring to it as Institute of Domestication. 
      But I was accepted! I was going to be moving off the farm! I didn't even know how to envision living somewhere else. 
	"Let me know if you need a letter of recommendation to get into any of the classes you want," said Mr. Thresher, "You?ve been a great student. You?re bound to do great things," he said. 
	I nodded, and he added with a glimmering smile, "You should go back to class and tell your friends now."
	 
	In fifth period, almost last period, another roadrunner-bourn scroll came into Mr. Ooten's class. As it happened, Brian was in my same class again, and he raised is eyebrows. 
      "Another one?" he mouthed.
	 
	Only, this time, it wasn't a college acceptance. 
	"Mr. Barnett," said Willa, the secretary, "your father is here to pick you up."
	I was at least as startled as I had been when I opened my acceptance letter. Pa had never even been to the school as far as I knew. 
	 
	Pa wasn't happy with me. As soon as we got out front of the school, he stopped and turned on me. 
	"What in hell were you doing up at the Rock?" he was shaking. I could see his nose hairs bristling with the force of his breath.
	 Fifth period had just ended, and clutches of kids were crossing the courtyard, heading hopefully and exhausted, to last period, and when, without waiting for an explanation, Pa knocked me down with a heavy fist thudding hard on my neck, all heads turned. 
	I writhed, feet on the sidewalk, head in the grass. Pa took me by the wrist and lifted me into the air like a five-year-old, my legs flopping limp on the ground. He started wailing on my back, my butt, my legs. Over and over?thud, slap, thwack?until I was limp as a ragweed tassel. 
	Then Pa was gone, and the students were gone, and I lay alone with the breeze. As the last bell rang, Mr. Thresher came out with a wet cloth and toweled me off, helped me to my feet. We walked slowly together to my horse, the school's-out crowd dissipating over the course of our arduous walk to the hitching lot. Thresher helped me on my steed, and I rode him slowly home, my bones aching with each cantoring step.
	When I got home, Pa was waiting on the veranda, rocking in his old chair. 
	"Sorry I had to do that," he said resolutely. I nodded and he went on. "Tomorrow, we?re going to church." 
	I nodded again and went to my room, curling up in bed and crying myself to sleep. 
	 
Scene 3. FRANKLIN?S MONSTER
	From then on out, we went to church on Saturday afternoons and on Sunday mornings, and sometimes on Wednesday nights. Pa had made me apologize to Ella's parents in church the next day. Ella wasn't there, and I don't think her Ma and Pa knew what I was apologizing for.
      The kids at school didn't know what had precipitated the beating either. But after a period of whispers and furtive glances, I actually started making friends. Kids would ask me things about myself, comment on my steed, invite me to hang out, even go to parties. Eventually, almost effortlessly, I found myself with a real circle of friends. 
	I still hung out with Brian now and then, but he and Courtney were spending more and more time together?in part, I think, because of an unspoken acknowledgement that they?d have to get a lot of nookie in before college forced them apart.  
	Ella had made herself scarce, both around school and in church, and I didn't know what to do about it except to shove my face into my pillow every night until I faded out in a blur of linen and drool and slow-leaked tears.
      Through the last months of high school, I worked the stables quietly and tenaciously, and my OAU enrollment forms piled up by the farmhouse's mail slot. I let them pile up after graduation, and they kept sending me mail almost weekly until well into the next year. After that, the flow of encouraging words in perfect office-workers? penmanship slowed to a trickle. 
      I hadn't realized it at the time, but the letters Brian wrote me from MID were lost in that pile too. I shoveled the mail into the magazine rack by Pa's recliner in the parlor, until one day, two years later. 
	Pa?d been in and out of the hospital all my life, with asthma, diabetes, gout. It wasn't anything special this time, just a vague discomfort in his side. I rode him in on my old steed, as usual, and walked him up to where they listened to his heart and palpated his abdomen and felt the bumps on his head, as usual. 
	The doctor said he didn't think it was serious, but he wanted to monitor Pa overnight to be safe. 
	 
	I used the opportunity to get some cleaning done around the house. Pa never let me throw anything away, but I figured I could at least thin out the piles a little without him noticing. 
	In the magazine rack, among the neatly written addresses from OAU, one envelope stood out. It was casually, and accurately addressed to Kelly B. instead of the usual Kelly G. Barnett, and the handwriting wasn't quite as neat as the others. Probably written by a newbie. When I saw that the corner was post-pecked Cambridge, Mass, I felt a flood of fear. For some reason, I thought something bad must have happened to Brian. 
	But no, it was a friendly check-in. Brian talked about the dorms and how he missed Courtney, and about the freshman classes he took?he hadn't started his breed/train classes yet, but he was excited about being high on the waitlist for some famous professor I?d never heard of. The letter was dated over a year and a half ago.
	I smiled through all three pages, and I even sat down to write a response, but I found myself with nothing to talk about, and poor penmanship to boot. I put the letter aside and told myself I?d work on it later. 
	 
	I went to church that night to pray for Pa, and Ella was there, feeding the candlemunks in the abbey. I hurried past her and squeezed into the front pew as services were starting. 
	I didn't think she noticed me, and I started to thank God for that, but then I saw her in her skorts and sandals standing right in front of me.
	"Evening Mrs. Baum," she said.
	I looked up, startled. And she smiled back.
	"Evening," said the elderly lady sitting next to me.
	"I hate to ask you to give up your seat," said Ella in the sweetest tone, "but--"
	"No need to make excuses," said the old lady with a twinkle in her eye as she surrendered her seat to Ella.
	Ella sat, and turned as if to say something, but the organ sung out, the voicedove cooed and sung a long lonely tune, and the congregation went quiet. 
	It was a typical sermon, the kind you can let your mind wander through, occasionally coming back for a nugget of inspiration, but all I could do was try to keep my eyes forward the whole time, and my thighs tight together to avoid brushing Ella's leg. 
	We sang together, prayed together, stood and sat, stood and sat, stood and sat again, and when the organ piped up again, and I tried to get up and run, but as soon as everybody started talking and leaving, Ella put her hand on my knee and turned to me. I about jumped out of my skin. 
	"I wanted to apologize to you," she said.
	I just went "Oh, well, uh..." like a fool. 
      "I told my Pa you tried to touch me wrong, and I know it was wrong of me to do that, but I couldn't say I?d been touched by witchcraft. Anyway, I? glad to see you in church."
      "Oh God," I said.
      "Don't talk that way in church Kelley!"
      She took my arm like I was an invalid who needed to be walked down he aisle, and walked me down the aisle. After we shook the Reverend's hand, Ella led me over to where the candlemunks were folding finery. She put a quarter in the machine, and we split the seed and corn mix between us, half a handful each. 
      "Have you heard about Gerald Fairweather?" She said, as I was dropping a corn kernel into a candlemunk's hopeful little hands. 
      "He's that preacher guy, right?" 
      "He does revivals. I don't want to say anything bad about Reverend Slack, but..." she made a snoring sound, and I chuckled. "You should come."
      "I will," I said. "I will." 
       
	When I rode to pick up Pa at the hospital the next day, I had a sense of dread. It turned out to be well-founded. The doctor said he had stage three liver cancer. 
	Pa's diagnosis was more optimistic. 
      "It's nothing," he said.
	Next day, I rode to Ella's house, to pick her up for Revival. 
	* * *
      "That was funny--kinda for kids--but tell me more about the laboratory," I?d asked Pa.
      Pa had frowned.
      "The hellfire lab? Naw, you don't need to hear any more of those stories."
      "Then tell me more about the foxhunt."
      "Alright," Pa had said. "That I can do."
* * *
Scene 4. WEDDING PRESENTS

	We came back from the wedding reception with a cart full of gifts. Our honeymoon was a long weekend in Gatlinburg and Pigeon Forge. 
	When we got home, Ella took all the gifts back, and brought home a pair of home entertainment monkeys. She knew me too well. 
	She smiled and hugged me while I flipped through the scripts. Every schoolkid knows forced mutation breeding is what allows the wonders of breeding, but call me old, because the pace of progress seems to be picking up more and more. It's unbelievable. The two monkeys took the specially-designed scripts and pantomimed everything from Shakespeare to stand-up comedy. Their voices were squeaky and tinny, but they were funny. 
       
	"I saw Courtney in the mall this morning," said Ella.
	"Oh? How's she doing?" 
	"Good, good. We didn't really talk. But she says Brian's coming home for a visit next month." 
	Pa coughed and sat up in his recliner. 
	"You should pigeon him and invite him over for dinner," said Ella.
	 
	"Why don't we start going back to revival?" asked Ella. "We could go tomorrow night?Brian, you could come too."
	Brian made a noncommittal head wag, and I said, "That's a good idea. I don't know about tomorrow, but I think we'll get around to that again soon." 

	After dinner, we retired to the parlor.
	"My comparative religions professor, he has a theory of universal religion," said Brian. "He had us read this book, have you heard of it?"

	Brian pulled a printed book out of his pack. It had gilt lettering on the cover.

	Franklin's Monster; or: the failed Prometheus.

	"What's it about?" I asked.
	"You gotta read it."
      I flipped through the yellowed pages. Although there were a few archaic words like Genavese, plenty of shalls, except for one word I didn't recognize--electricity--the language wasn't too obscure to understand. I was intrigued, and I almost sat down to read it right there. 
      "Oh, and while I?m thinking about it," said Brian, "Here's another book you should read."
	He handed over a fat book called A Secular History of the United States. 
	"Okay, I'll read them," I said, setting both books aside. 
	"You'll be blown away," said Brian.
	"How's that?" I asked.
	"Everything you know about history, about the government, the church. It's all wrong. Oh!" he said, "I?ve got another one for you too!"
	He started reaching into his pack, but I stopped him.
	"I?m a slow reader," I said. 
	"Haha. Okay," said Brian. "Another time. I wish I could stick around longer. I didn't even get to talk to Ella."
	"That's cause you were busy talking my ears off," I said with a chuckle.
	"True that," he said. 
	 
	It was a few months later, Ella mentioned seeing Courtney in the farmers? market. 
	"She was knocked up, I could tell. And she ain't been seeing nobody, not as far as anyone knows." 
	I snorted and 
	Ella asked about going to revival again, and I said "Naw, I?m tired. Maybe next week."
	"That's okay I suppose," she said, "but you wouldn't mind if I go on my own, would you?"
	"Sure, baby, sure," I said. 
	 
Scene 5. PATRIOTS 
	Me and Brian kept in touch by pigeon for a while, and he kept asking if I?d gotten around to reading those books. I hadn't, and I felt bad lying but I wrote that I?d read the Franklin one and that it was pretty good. I was planning to send out the letter that day, but with the mite blight that summer, service was grounded for some time, and I never got around to sending it.
	Never got around to going back to revival either. Turns out, her and Lester McCoy was having a Revival of their own. 
	 
	"You have to go," I said.
	"Nothing happened," she insisted.
	"I saw you and Lester sitting side by side at the revival. I know I said I wasn't going to come, but I decided to do it anyway...for you." 
	"He was the only one I knew there. We used to be friends, I don't think we should have to stay away from each other just because we used to date in high school. That was years ago!"
	She put her hands on mine, but I shook them off. 
	"Every Thursday night," I said, shaking my head. I felt so stupid.
	"Please, I didn't know how much it would upset you. I'll never go again."
      "You have to go," I said. 
      "What?"
      "I mean, you have to leave here. Now. Get out."
      She was sobbing, and I was too, but she packed a small bag, and made for the door.
      "Where are you going to go?"
      "My Ma's, I guess," she said, propping the door with her body. She looked at me with those puppy dog eyes, and said "Can I take the mule?"
      "Take a crackerhorse," I said. 
      It would've taken two days to get to her mother's house on the old mule. At least on a cracker, she wouldn't have to overnight.
      I helped her tack up, and gestured at a wave when she gave me a last look from the end of the drive.
	 
	Ella was gone, and everything I did made me feel incrementally more hollow, a crumpled Christmas pumpkin. 
	I rode my old steed around town, staring at the pigeon traffic, wondering where they were all going. It had been a hot summer, and the gutter dogs were lazy. The town was penetrated through with the smell of trash. It all felt wrong.
	I spent a couple of days on the farm, trying not to wonder where Ella was staying. 
	Three nights on the farm. Three nights alone. 
	After a while, I gave the horses to pasture, littered plenty of extra straw, left pay enough for the hands, and a note to check up on the place twice a week, packed my steed, and headed north. 
	 
	The steed was out of shape, so I only made about fifty miles a day, but that's exactly what I needed. Along the way, I passed a family on an ostrichpillar and an old couple riding an enormous Hofstadter Quad tricked out as a double, and a smaller crackerhorse gang. The herds of pineywoods dotted the rolling hills. 
	 
	"There was a young man," Pa said. "He was riding alone from Cleveland to Cincinnati--this was back before big endurance horses, so everything was staged out. In those days, crackers were all the rage. Fastest things on the road. You could easily make a hundred and fifty miles a day, staged. The only trouble is, this was when forced mutation breeding was new, and sometimes undesirable traits would show up in the animals. 
	"Well, this young man was making good time, and he decided to push a few more hours to finish the last leg of his journey. It was well beyond sunset when the cracker cast a shoe. They were powerful devils, reliable as hell, but they broke shoes left and right. So, he limped his horse into a deserted tack station outside Springboro.
      Nobody was around, but she saw somebody had carelessly left the stables unlocked, so she hitched her cracker, slid more than enough cash under the window plank to cover trade and tack, and quietly stole away into the stable. By the light of the full moon, she selected a fine-looking new mount. She quietly led him out of the stable, but when she went to lock the stable back up, she noticed it hadn't been left unlocked by accident--it had been forced.
      "Fearing horse thieves, he saddled as fast as possible, but before he was finished, he was startled by a hand on her shoulder. He turned and saw a huge, hulking man, with a strange, frightening face. He turned and struggled to get on the horse quickly. The strange man grabbed his ankle, but just when he thought he was caught by the horse thief, the cracker took off.  
      "Relieved to have gotten away safely, he continued on his way to Cincinnati.
      "Almost a week later, he still hadn't arrived, and his family was getting worried, so they sent word to all the tack stations asking if anybody had seen the young man. Within a few days, they got a letter from the Springboro station manager describing how his deaf night watchman tried to warn a young man not to take their newest cracker, on account of its breeding. The horse had a history of throwing its riders and attacking them.
      "Worried for their boy's life, the family sent out as big a search party as they could muster. It took them weeks, but they finally found the body...almost a hundred miles away, drowned in a watering hole deep in the woods. His face was covered with wounds that looked like suspiciously like horse bites."
      Suddenly, Pa grabbed my shoulders, and I shrieked and about jumped out of my skin. 
      When I calmed down, Pa whispered, "Some say, the wild cracker of Springboro is still wandering the hills of southern Ohio to this day."  
	 
	I hitched my steed and, pigeon tube in hand, wandered through the college town. 
	I pulled the string for Brian's room in Bexley Hall, and a young voice came from the tube. 
	Turned out Brian was TAing a class, but the roommate let me in to wait. The little dorm living room was covered in stacks of papers and pizza boxes. They had a named monkey?Berkowitz?that was trained to fetch beer from the ice house across the street. 
	 
	I must?ve passed out on the sofa, and when I woke up, the windows were dusk-dim. Muffled sounds from students romping in adjoining rooms made the quiet of Brian's room all the more stark. 
	"Hello?" I said timidly.
	I knocked on one of the doors, and when nobody answered, I peeked in. It was dark, but I could make out on a bookshelf, the familiar cylindrical hellfire jar I remembered. I threw the door wide, and crossed the room, tripping on the mattress on the floor. But when I put my hands on the jar, tears welled in my eyes. I don't know why.
	Almost reverently, I rubbed the metal rod on the wool of my boots? lining. For some reason, it surprised me that it still worked. I shook the pain out of my struck finger. 
	"Hello?" I heard a voice from behind me. "Dude, what are you doing in my room?"
	"Sorry," I said.
	"Kelly?" 
	"Brian?"
	He lit a candle and gave me a hug and I didn't know what to say. 
	 
	It turned out, that day when I walked out into the woods, past the fields where the packs of feral dysplasics slithered, Brian had followed me into the woods and when I?d thrown the hellfire jar into a weedpatch, Brian had fetched it, kept it, cherished it.
	"You wouldn't believe it, Kelly," he said, "but this thing's been my hobby since I enrolled. I?ve tried to learn everything I could about it." 
	I shook my head in disbelief, but I listened, with growing apprehension. 
	"The library here is amazing; I'll have to show it to you sometime, but I?ve been rooting through it, and Franklin's Monster...that's the tip of the iceberg." 
	"The book," I said.
	"Yeah, I figured you hadn't read it. But have you heard the story?" He asked, and shrugged when I shook my head. "Well, you know the old campfire story about the guy who sewed together dead bodies and rose the dead with a prayer? Franklin's monster was basically the same thing, only in the book, he used lightning to envigorate the monster."
	I laughed in spite of myself. This talk of hellfire made me feel like I?d crossed some kind of line, somehow made my post-Ella life concrete. Beyond salvation. 
	"But, but, here's the thing..." sputtered Brian, "it was real!"
	Suddenly, all the apprehension that had been building erupted inside me. I almost shouted out at him, No, no, Brian, who are you? I felt then that my last attachment to my old life was gone. Beyond Ella, beyond my childhood friend. No connections left. Only the thought of Pa sitting in his recliner, watching the sunset felt right anymore. 
	I checked myself, and in what I gauged to be a neutral tone, I said, "How do you mean?"
	"Franklin was real, basically just like the novel. He lived in the colonies, and he really did experiments on the dead."
	"Experiments?" I asked.
	"Yeah, like tests, trying things out, trying to learn new things. That's what the Naturalists were about."
	I couldn't help myself, I blurted, "Bullshit. Naturalists!? You?ve got to be fucking kidding." 
	In the split second before I said it, I thought it sounded humorous, but when the words came out, I could tell I was talking down to him. I think that's when we ceased to be friends.
	"Look," he said, "I?m not going to convince you by telling you, but if you look into it for yourself, you'll be surprised. Benjamin Franklin was real. They don't teach you about it in the history books, but find a drawing of the Declaration of Independence...or go and see the real thing in Washington. His signature is right there." 
	He might have been talking total nonsense, but he seemed sincere, and for a moment I decided to humor him.
	"Alright, so what's this have to do with the jar there?" 
	"Franklin was Scene of a Hellfire Club. Basically, I guess they were sort of private clubs that threw these wild parties and stuff. They were kind of underground, but weren't really much of a secret among the elites back then, and all they really did was party. Some of the members were into natural science too, making devices that used steam, and trying to control electrical fluid?the stuff that lightning's made of."
	He pointed at the h-fire jar, excited. I didn't say anything, so he went on.
      "Well, eventually they started drawing too much attention, and people spoke out against them, and when Franklin revived a dead body using electrical fluid...well, they went underground. He was the only Founding Father executed by the state."
	I?d had enough. I felt lost, disconnected from everyone I ever knew, except Pa. And I decided right then that I had to go home and take care of Pa. I should never have left. I told Brian so, and made for the door, but he spoke up...
	"Wait. Kelly, enough about the jar. I?m sorry. I just get so excited. It's too late to leave tonight. Let me take you out for a drink and forget about everything. I?m so glad you?re here. You'll have to tell me all about your horsing. How's your pa? How's Ella?" 
	I laughed and shook my head, "Alright," I said, "you?ve got a deal. Mind if I crash here for the night?"
	"But of course."
	 
	Eventually, after many drinks, my curiosity about the h-fire got the better of me, and over a mug of dark ale, I leaned over to Brian.
      "So, what do you mean you study electrical fluids?"
      "Shhhh," he said, but he leaned in even closer, and said quietly as could be managed above the bluegrass band, "I'm trying to re-invent electrical hydraulics. I had to design my own major--we're calling it 'American folkloric archaeology'--but really, I study naturalist writings, and sometimes the workings of hellfire equipment. I've even gotten in touch with some modern naturalists. They're still around--and they've made progress! Can you believe it?"
	 
	We stumbled into the old farmhouse, laughing with drink and old stories of high school. We?d both judiciously avoided the subject of Ella. Moonlight barely lit the room, and by it, I saw that a man was awakening from a sofa slumber, and when he stood he towered above both of us. He was only a few years older than me and Brian, late 30s I?d guess, but his eyes were late-night sunken. 
	"Kelley, this is Leo."
	"Sorry we woke you up," I said. 
	He looked at me with raised eyebrows, then said in a craggy voice, "Just taking a nap."
	"It's three in the morning," I said.
	I put out my hand, but he didn't offer his. He just hmphed. 
	"I was wondering if you could show Kelley your work."
	Leo eyed me sidewise, but Brian reassured him that I was cool, and he waved us into the back. Behind a ragged old tapestry of Washington crossing the Delaware, we entered the naturalist's lair. 
	In the pitch dark of the hellfire laboratory, Leo struck a lighter repeatedly, lighting candle after candle. Within minutes, the whole room was aglow in honey-colored light. 
	Along one flower-papered wall, there was a huge butchers? block longer than I am tall, covered over with reproductions of old documents, strange drawings, stacks of books with bindings so cracked you couldn't tell what pages had been read the most. It looked like something you?d expect to be covered in an inch thick layer of mildew and cobwebs, but there wasn't so much as a speck of dust, except high on the walls, where a huge thirteen-starred American flag hung.
	The rest of the room looked like a bizarre smithy, but where there should be horse shoes and tongs, the walls were hung with coils of wire, metal rods and foils, glass bulbs, and dozens of tiny objects that defied description. On stools and tables, on the floor, and on the ledges of painted-over windows, were more objects that I had no names for, things like wheels with handles but no pulleys, metal bricks and coils of wire with springs and copper spider-web tracings all over. 
	"So, you?re a naturalist," I said.
	"We prefer the term patriot," said Leo
	I chuckled at the nonsensical statement. 
      I reached towards one of the contraptions?a wheel with metal tabs all around it and an axle-mounted handle. 
      "Don't touch that! Jesus, buddy. You don't know what you?re messing with." shouted Leo.
      "Sorry!" I yelped.
      "Show him how it works," said Brian.
      Leo sighed, but complied. 
      "See here," he said, "turn the handle quickly but steadily?and for chrissake, don't touch anything else."
      I did as Leo said, watching the spinning copper tabs while he pointed Brian to grab some kind of wand and a rabbit pelt. 
      My arm was already starting to get tired when Brian started rubbing the fur vigorously on the shiny black wand. After thirty seconds or so, he reached out, gingerly waving the rod near the spinning wheel. It made a miniscule crackling sound.
      "Keep spinning," said Leo. "Steady, steady!"
      Suddenly the little crackling was replaced by a loud snapping that popped over and over with every turn of the wheel. I hadn't noticed them before, but two little metal rods stuck up from the base of the wheel, and between them, I saw the source of the snapping. 
      I was making lightning! A tiny bolt jumped across the gap, and the popping snap, I realized was miniature thunder. It was like a tiny little thunderstorm with no clouds or rain...and I was controlling it! When I tired, it slowed, when I sped up, it crackled faster and faster. 
      I didn't know what to think. I was thrilled, I felt...powerful. Somewhere inside, there was a blackening terror, but I think I buried it down, somewhere near the thoughts of Ella that had plagued the whole trip. 
      "You can stop cranking if you want," said Leo.
      "Oh, okay." With a little disappointment, I slowed...pop...pop....pop....pop.....pop......pop......pop.
      When it had stopped, and the wheel circled lazily, I turned to Leo. A huge toothy smile parted his beard. 
	"Want to see something really cool?" he said, with a lopsided grin.
	I nodded slowly, wide-eyed. 
	"A colleague of mine perfected this a few years back?a fellow by the name of Gates?you know him, Brian."
	Brian rolled his eyes. "Oh, him."
	"Yeah, he's a piece of work to be sure, but if you can overlook the attitude and lack of personal hygiene, the guy is a top-notch tinker. Worked the better Scene of a decade on this little baby."
	Leo reverently placed wooden plaque with something attached to it, covered in a spotless natural linen sack. It looked like a little trophy in a ghost costume. When he pulled off the linen, I saw it was one of those glass bulbs. Inside, there was a tiny filament of metal stretched around a set of vertical posts. It looked like a tiny stand of trees with a rope wrapped around it.
	I peered in, trying to make out the details, wondering what its purpose was while Leo and Brian messed around with a bunch of wires. Brian pulled out a mounted bicycle from a large closet, and Leo attached spring-loaded copper clips to the base of the glass bulb.
	Brian got on the bike, and Leo blew out each candle one by one, and soon, we were in darkness once more.
	"Go!" came Leo's call from the shadows. 
	A rhythmic spinning of bicycle chain filled the room, and I saw, astonished, that the wire in the glass was glowing, dim orange at first, but as Brian's now-illuminated face strained with the pedaling, it got brighter and brighter until it was a brilliant white that I couldn't look directly at.
	My heart rattled at its cage. This was magical, mystical, and I felt the presence of God right there in that room, in the glowing light of that little crystal bulb. 
	Right then, the fear came gushing to the surface and I almost cried out as tears came into my eyes. With a loud POP! the light went out, and I stumbled backwards, crouching in a corner, awestruck, trembling with reverent fear. 
	We went back into the parlor, and Leo said, "I?ve got two bottles of Scotch. Should we crack open the peaty Islay or the smooth blended one." 
	Me and Brian both pointed to the blended.
      Leo shrugged, and said, "Won by 3/5ths."
      It didn't sink in what he meant, we all had a shot and I don't remember passing out, but I woke up in the morning feeling profoundly alone in a hostile environment.
      Nobody was up yet, so I got my brogans on, quietly let myself out and walked over to the hitching station. I paid the attendant and saddled up. With my throbbing head, it was going to be a painful ride home. 
	 
	When I got back home, Pa was sitting on the veranda with a cigar and a mint julep. 
	"Ella's in the back," he said, casually.
       
Scene 6. TOPHER?S SHED
      I cooked dinner that night for the first time since Ella and me'd gotten married. It'd slaughtered and roasted a chicken, and I must not have gotten the time right, because it ended up pretty dry. 
      "This is awesome, honey," said Ella. "I can't believe you cooked."
      "Nah, it's a little dry." 
      "Not bad for your first time," she said.
      "I used to cook all the time for Pa. You didn't know that?"
      "You did? I'm surprised."
      "I've had to take care of him in his illnesses all my life. Least I could do. He always took care of me."
      "And now you're doing the same thing, what with me in my condition?" She laughed.
      "Something like that," I said. 
      "Okay, well. I'm going to take a plate in to your Pa. Want to pour us a little wine?"
      "Will do," I said, "but just a little. What with--your condition." 
      I put my arm around her and whispered, "Sorry about all that nonsense. I missed you."
      "Missed you too," she said. 
       
      It was a few months later, and the summers had been ludicrously hot lately...the newspapers said it was most likely the livestock flatus from breeders, but the breeders? lobbies protested loudly?that we got pigeoned by the coroner. Uncle Topher had died in his house in Crooksville. The examiner determined he?d had a heat stroke. He?d probably lain there for two weeks before anybody had noticed he wasn't checking his pigeon box. 
      A post script to the coroner's letter mentioned there was some cleanup the family would want to undertake at the residence.
      Turned out, the "cleanup" was a whole garage full hellfire devices. Topher had passed away on the bench in a locked outbuilding that had to be pried open by the constable to retrieve the body. He?d been working on some sort of electrical fluid engine. 
      There were reams of correspondence with other?I guess they were "naturalists". 
      Me and Pa burned them all, but on account of Pa's health and Ella being about ready to burst with my baby any day now, neither one of us had the time to clean out the shed. So, we locked it up tight and put it out of our minds as best we could.
       
      For the baby shower, Ella invited all the nearest neighbors over for dinner, but only Courtney showed up, along with her son, Oscar.  
      Oscar was only six weeks old, but his hair was wild and his blue eyes shone bright. Neither me nor Ella encouraged Courtney to talk about Brian, and she didn't volunteer, so that night, I penned a letter to Brian and pigeoned it the next day. 
	I never received a response, but I hoped I might have encouraged Brian to get in touch with Courtney again. 
	
	"Well," said Ella when she was gone, "at least we know who the father is."
	 
	Kelley Granger Barnett III was born a week later. He had my nose, Ella's eyes, and Pa's mouth.
	 
Scene 7. FILIGREE
	Pa had been getting on me to clean out Topher's shed for years now, and it was the way he put it one day that made me finally take some action.
	"Why don't you get Junior to help you," he said. "You'll make short work of it. Nasty business, but he won't know what it is. You all can get rid of that junk once and for all."
	"That's a good idea," chimed Ella. "Why don't you take Oscar along with you too?"
	 
	The next day, me and Kelley Junior and Oscar went to clean out the shed. 
	We?d gotten rid of about half the crap in that shed when Oscar turned to me and said, "Why did you invite me over to clean out your uncle's shed?"
	"Needed some help," I said, tossing a glass sphere to Junior, who delighted in smashing it on the top of the trash bin. 
	"Topher wasn't my dad, was he?" 
	I choked a laugh into a cough. 
	"No, no, Jesus, no," I said.
	 
	That day, I wrote Brian again. I don't know if he was even at MID anymore, but I still had the address on an old pigeon tube I?d been using to block open the parlor window. 
	At first, I started writing a letter about Oscar, but I crumpled it up and started another. This time, I wrote Brian about all the hellfire devices in uncle Topher's shed. 
	I didn't know what anything in there was, but I had 
put my hands on every damn piece, so I described them in detail...the gilded wheels and metal boxes, fine laceworks of wire stretched between glass bulbs filled with tiny metal filigree. 

Scene 8. THE HELLFIRE PIGEON

      I caught them in the act when I went over looking for Junior the next day. The metal box chirped and whistled, and amid the strange song of the hellfire pigeon, I heard voices. A chill ran through my body, my blood surged through my chest and my head, and I slowly approached behind them, half-fascinated in spite of myself. My jaw hung slack. I listened to the obscure whispers in the song.
      The spark gaps have to be spaced right...practice soldering because the better the connections, them more you can clean up the noise...you might have to add more coils if you get attenuation at the lower frequencies...stop me if you aren't following, Kelley.
      It was all a jumble of nonsense, but when I heard the hellfire pigeon say my son's name, I grabbed Junior by the short hairs and sent him home without his horse. 
      "Kelley!" Brian spun around, flushed.
      "I don't want to hear it, Brian." 
      "I'm just trying to help them keep in touch. Courtney and Oscar are moving to Massachusetts with me, and I want the boys to stay friends."
      "They can pigeon each other all they want."
      "You see how that worked with us, Kelley."
      "It's not right. It's not right, this hellfire. You want my kid to grow up to be some kind of neo-naturalist? I will not stand for it."
      "Kelley, please. Hear me out. You accept gene splicing without question. Nobody bats an eye at forced mutation and controlled breeding. Why should a new technology bother you?"
      "Why do we need something new when breeding has given us so much? We've got horses that don't shit, house monkeys, whale feasts, and apple steaks. Thank Father Charles for that."
      "Well, Darwin laid the groundwork to some extent, but mainly Mendel and the Curies give us forced mutation breeding, and gene sequencing is more recent. That's what's really driving the genetic revolution." I gave him the don't talk down to me look, and he moved on, "But really, what good is it? House monkeys are practically like sla..." he stopped before he said something stupid. "Look. Maybe horses don't shit, but have you smelled their piss lately? Not like when we were kids. So, horses are faster, pigeons are smarter. Still, this is centuries-old technology. Electrial hydraulics though--it's a totally different thing; we have no idea what's even possible. Electrical lighting and radio communications are just the begining."
      "But it's wrong. It's not like breeding at all. Breeding is natural, gene splicing is just farming...I'm not trying to hit you over the head with scripture, but there's something right and true about man's dominion over the Earth. Breeding is a Scene of that. Not this hellfire stuff. That's like fiddling with the very laws of the universe. Why can't you see that?" 
      "But..."
      "I've heard enough. I'm going home." 
      
      When I got home, I rushed into Junior's room. He was playing with a hellfire jar, and I flew into a rage. 
      "You will not become a warlock, not in my house," I shouted. "Do you want to leave, cause there's the door!"
      He hung his head and whimpered, "No." 
      "Alright then. Now, hand over that damned contraption." 
      He did as I asked, and as he did, I saw his eyes look around the room. 
      I roared right in his face, "Get out!"
      When he was gone, I lifted up the cover on his bed. Underneath, there were jars and gizmos, rusty stuff from Topher's shed and elsewhere. I ripped the sheets of the bed, turned over the mattress, flung his toys onto the floor and tipped over his chest of drawers. He'd been hoarding this stuff for who knows how long.
      As I was tearing up his room, finding more and more hellfire, I gradually became aware of a voice.
      "Kelley," it said. "Kelley, calm down."
      I spun around and saw Ella with tears streaming down her cheeks. 
      "This nasty crap has to go!" I shouted.
      "That's fine. You're right," she said, "but calm down. Junior--"
      "Junior is getting a whipping."
      "I agree," she said. "If that's what it takes. But for the love of God, you don't have to tear down the whole house. Junior will learn the lesson better if you're calm, deliberate."
      She reached out and touched my arm, sending a tingle through my whole body, and I broke down. I hugged her close to me and we both sobbed together for long minutes. 
      Afterwards, we stacked up all the infernal devices we could find in the middle of Junior's blue blanket--and it was a goodly pile. We tied it up and Ella helped me carry it out the back door. We put it on the old hay wagon and came back inside. 
      I had been close to beating Junior senseless, but now that I was thinking, I shuddered at the thought. 
      Long after the dust settled, Pa yelled from his recliner, "What's going on in there?" 
      "Nothing," me and Ella yelled back at the same time. 
* * *
       "I?m gonna take you on over to the Johnses, Kelly. You gotta see a fox hunt. Once in your life at least."
      "But foxes are extinct," I protested.
      "Yeah, I know. And foxcats are a new breed, so they ain't quite the same. Still, you gotta watch the beagles in action."
      "Beagles?"
      "We useta call all the hunting animals beagles. The sniffers, the terriers, the beagles themselves. Even the horses and the falcon, if we were talking about them all together, they was all just beagles."
      "That's weird," I said.
      "Imagine this," said Pa, "...the barking hounds, itching to get out of the barnyard. From horseback, you throw open the latch...usually the youngest got that job...that was usually me. I never had to open the gate, cause the dogs pushed through on their own. Once the hounds reached the edge of the yard and vanished into the brush, Pa?my Pa?released the sighting hawks, and finally, the falconer?Gramps, until he got put in a home?then Uncle Topher after that?was the one that let fly the falcon.
      "You?d be waiting on your steed," Pa had said in a hushed voice, "and you?d keep your eye on the birds. They?d flock left and right, swaying over the forest, then, at last, they?d dive, and the sky would be empty except for the falcon, circling the spot fifteen times to mark it, then diving to retrieve the fox."
      "The falcon ate the fox?" I?d sputtered.
      "No, no" said Pa, "he took it from the terriers and returned it to the barn once we all got to the spot on horseback. We?d burn out the foxholes and fill it in while the beagles trekked home." 
* * *
      Next morning, me Junior, and Pa rode a quad over to Topher's. Along the way, I did my best to apologize to Junior for the beating, but my words were all a jumble. Still, he seemed to cheer up as we approached the drive to the shed. I don't know what he was expecting?I?d told him we were going to clean up. 
      I had planned to make Junior do the honors, but the look of excitement in his eye was too much. 
      "Why don't you gather some sticks for torches," I said. 
      "Okay," he said, and went into the woods. The boy was chipper, and I hated to take that away, but the hellfire lab had to go.
      I pulled my horse up next to the shed and pitchforked a pile of hay in front of the padlocked door. The kerosene can was still inside the door, and I splashed it over the glass and metal contraptions, judiciously at first, but then I poured with wild abandon, kicking some of the gear over as I went around the shed, making sure every damned thing would burn. When I was done inside, I went out and splashed arcs of kerosene over the door and shingling. I kicked the haystack right up against the door. Then I lit a torch, and tossed it into the hay.
      I watched impatiently while the fire followed kerosene trails up the shed walls. The fire climbed the outside of the shed, and walked its way into the door. Through the dingy window, I watched the flames dance. Soon enough, the heat was too intense, and I had to back away towards the trailhead. 
      I heard a shout from behind me.
      "Daddy! What are you doing? You're killing the electric pigeon!"
      Kelley Junior darted across the weed lot and into the shed, sending the glowing embers of the haystack whirling behind him. 
      "Junior. Junior!" I screamed.
      "Get him!" shouted Pa, "What are you waiting for?"
      I stood, stunned. My son, so enthralled with naturalism that he would risk his life to save the demonic device. I couldn't move. I was appalled at myself for failing to take action, but even more so for failing to raise him right. It was my weakness he had inherited. 
      Pa pushed me aside and moved faster than I had seen him move in years. He hop-limped into the burning shed, awkwardly covering his mouth and nose with his hand. Moments later, Junior appeared in the door, lugging the hellfire pigeon in both arms. 
      "Where's Pa?" I shouted.
      "Pa?" he said.
      I pushed him aside and ran into the burning shed. Pa was pinned under an enormous collapsed workbench. Sparks were jumping, the strange smell of lightning was in the air, burning my eyes. [Need more closure.]
