TITLE: The Fifth Mattress (unfinished story)
(c) 2005 by Shannon A. Rampe & Nathan McKnight



Fact: Five is the minimum number of mattresses required to survive in Antarctica.

I woke numb. My bones were cold steel rods, chilling me from the inside out. I thought for a moment that I was blind until I realized that my eyelids had frozen shut. 
I huddled in a tiny lean-to of mattresses. A few dozen meters behind me, my lifeboat bumped against the ice. I'd narrowly escaped the prison ship, the Carnival Sunshine, but in the end, I'd reached my destination. Antarctica.
I shifted one of the mattresses. The sky was an inky pool. An animal nudged its head into my cubby and I drew back, startled. It was a penguin.
“Hey, Bub. You need another one,” it said, speaking with a thick Brooklyn accent.
“Wh- what?” My lips were numb, my tongue half-frozen. 
“You need another mattress,” repeated the penguin. “Four won’t do. They’ll keep out the wind, but you need something to insulate you from the ice. By the look of it, that coat ain’t gonna do the trick. If you want to last, you’ll need to find one more mattress.”
“Leave me alone,” I said. “I’m perfectly capable on my own.”
The penguin shrugged, raising its flippers as if to say suit yourself. “Don’t lay down on the ice again or you won’t wake up. You gotta keep moving until you find the fifth mattress.”
I liked animals, but I wasn’t accustomed to accepting advice from them. Still, the penguin was right. Antarctica was my refuge, my new home. If I intended to survive and thrive, I needed a properly constructed and insulated fortification.
A crusty foot nudged my forehead. I hadn’t realized that I’d shut my eyes again. 
“Hey, human. You should see this.”
I took a long, icy breath. “See what?” I said at last.
He kicked me hard this time. “Behold!”
I sat up, rubbing my forehead, and the mattresses fell away.
There was light on the horizon. Not the faint pink hues of dawn. The Sun wouldn’t return for months. This was a ghostly green swamplight, waves shimmering across the sky like the sea of the gods lapping up against the shores of heaven.
“Aurora Australis.” I stared, mesmerized.
The penguin gestured behind me. “Looks a little like your mattress.”
My mattress. My mattress.
It was dirty, stained with green and pink and yellow magic marker.
#
As long as Will could remember, Luke had tormented him. 
His earliest memory was of coloring with markers. Will had a coloring book, each perforated page with the outline of an animal. There were walruses, penguins, sea lions, fish, tortoises, gulls. It was his favorite coloring book. He couldn’t spell their names yet, but he could color them. He loved to color, and Mom had given him a brand new set of markers for his fourth birthday. 
He loved animals. And he loved to color.
Will removed each page from the book, being careful not to tear them or otherwise damage the pictures. Then he spent hours in his room, painstakingly filling in each picture, never straying outside the lines, always selecting the nicest colors—the honey yellow, the sea foam green, the astral blue. As he finished each one, he wrote his name at the bottom, a difficult act but one which made him proud. He was smart.
Rain battered the windows, darkening the sky and frightening Will, but as long as he focused on the animals, he could almost imagine that he was outside, in the sunshine, playing with them. He clenched his jaws and focused on each shape and line, shutting out the rest of the world.
By the time he completed the pictures, the rain had eased up. He had colored every picture in the book. He ran into the low-ceilinged living room where Mom was smoking cigarettes and watching game shows. “Look, Mom! Come see what I did.”
There was an uneasy look in her eyes. “What did you do, Will?”
Will’s smile faltered. Was Mom scared? “I made pictures. Come see.”
She followed him down the hallway. The rain and thunder had come back, letting the darkness creep in through the windows. 
He pushed open the door to his room, trying to recall the joy and excitement he’d felt just moments before. He froze.
His bedroom was a scene of carnage. Luke had destroyed everything. Each perfect page had been scribbled on with black markers, ruining Will’s careful lines. In giant red strokes, Luke had scrawled his name over each page and then torn them in half. He’d even written his name up the walls and onto the ceiling. Luke. Luke. Luke. 
“Oh, Jesus protect us,” whispered Mom. “What did you do, Will?”
“Luke did it,” Will said. He began to cry.
Mom put him in bed and ordered him to stay there. She fled the room, closing the door behind her. Will was left crying in the dark with the rain shaking the windows. And Luke was there, hiding under the bed, laughing. 
Will pulled the sheets up over his head and clutched his markers close. He cried until his tears ran down into the markers and ink got all over him and all over his sheets. The ink would soak through and leave the mattress stained with blots of bright color. 
After that, mom told Will he was to old for coloring and gave him Grampa Benny’s old books to read instead...Darwin, Wallace, Shackelton.
#
Fact: Carnival Cruise Lines runs the largest international prison ship complex in the world.

The cheese plate was replete with stink as the other prisoners danced slowly around the heaving seaborne ballroom and the guards jabbed them with cattle prods. 
Somehow, Luke had found me. He was on the ship. He was probably in this room. I pulled the hood of my coat a little further down over my face and leaned forward over my dinner plate. The other prisoners at my table were whispering. Idiots. Didn’t they know he would hear?
“Where are you, Will?” Luke’s voice, two tables over. “We haven’t finished our game, brother!”
I panicked. Luke’s games involved knives and pliers.
I pushed out my chair crawled beneath the dinner table.
“What’s the matter with you?” said a prisoner whose nametag said Walter. “Someone call security!”
Two of the prisoners at my table screamed. Luke might already be removing their eyes with a screwdriver. But I was safe for the moment. Hidden.
Legs scrambled away as the other prisoners fled the table. “Don’t go! He’ll know something is wrong. Don’t let him notice you!”
One of the guards pushed his head under the table. “Mister Turner? Would you come with me?”
“Get away! Run! Luke is coming!”
“Mister Turner, please!”
I kicked him in the face and he cried out and fell back. A moment later, another pair of guards appeared and threw back the tablecloth. Each of them grabbed one of my legs and dragged me from my hiding place. “No!”
“Take him to his room and lock him there,” said the first guard, dabbing a napkin to a bloody lip. “I’ll alert the captain.”
Look stood behind him, grinning manically. He pointed at the guard and drew a finger across his neck. 
“Luke will get you!” I screamed. “Look out behind you!”
But the guards were already dragging my back to my cell. This time, they wouldn’t forget to lock it. I’d be trapped on the prison ship with Luke forever. Fortunately, my escape vessel was already prepared. 
Once we were in the hallway, I elbowed one guard in the stomach and shoved the other one down a staircase. When I knew I’d lost them, I turned down a corridor and ran til I saw a bulkhead. I yanked open the latch, and a cold wind blew into my face. I pushed aside slabs of hanging meat and jumped ship, the bulkhead slamming behind me, and I landed on all fours in the cold Antarctic night.
#
Fact: The emperor penguin has no natural predators.

“What’s your name?” the penguin asked.
“William Turner,” I said, not certain that I wanted to be on a first name basis with this bird. It wouldn’t leave me alone. But it had been right when he told me the movement would keep me warm. With a piece of nylon webbing wrapped around my waist, I dragged the mattresses up the ice shelf, away from the roar of the ocean. What other secrets of the ice might this animal know?
“Will.” The penguin repeated. “My name’s Benny.”
I grunted and shifted my direction away from the penguin.
“Something wrong?” He sounded offended.
“No.”
“Then what’s your problem?”
“First of all, I said my name was William, not Will. Second, my grandfather’s name was Benny. Grampa Benny.”
“It’s a good name,” the penguin said. He even sounded like Grampa Benny, with that accent. 
“Why’d you come here, Will?”
“I moved here. I want to live here.”
“You moved to Antarctica?” The bird made a cawing sound that sounded suspiciously like laughter. “Why?”
I didn’t want to admit the truth. I was afraid if I mentioned his name that he might hear me, even though I knew he was trapped on the prison ship.
“I came here to...study birds,” I lied. “I’m a scientist.”
“Lucky you found me, then.”
“I guess.” I wasn’t so sure.
The wind blew, carrying clouds of powdery diamond dust that flickered in the aurora. A low bellow came from nearby.
“What was that?” I said.
The ice shivered beneath us. Something was coming. Something big. 
A shape emerged from the flurries of dust and darkness: enormous yellowed tusks, whiskers like tines of a wire brush, and eyes dripping with bloodlust. 
“Walrus!” I screamed. “Run!” I fought to free myself from the mattresses, but the webbing was fastened securely, and I was restrained to a herky-jerky shuffle. 
“What are you talking about, Will? Don’t you know that walruses are native to the arctic? I thought you said you were a scientist.”
Suddenly I was grasping at snow rapidly dissolving into open air as a crevasse opened beneath my feet and I plummeted into an icy crack. I felt my body jolt at the waist, and I hung limp like a deer on a hood. 
Above me, the mattresses were caught in the mouth of the crevasse. I swung below, suspended at the mercy of the webbing. From above came the panicked shrieking of a penguin followed by the sound of crunching bones.
A shape appeared at the lip of the crevasse, its blubbery form blocking out the starlight. Its breath made little clouds of flecked steam. Penguin blood ran down its tusks and dripped on the mattress hanging directly above me, soaking into the cotton.
I squeezed my eyes shut and waited to die.
#
Will loved spending time outside. He would spend hours wandering in the woods behind their trailer, looking at bugs beneath rotten logs, hunting for the largest toadstools, and trying to catch squirrels. In the forest, Luke left him alone.
Mom didn’t like Will to spend too much time outside. She was convinced that he was sick, even though he felt fine. She said that keeping inside would prevent him from getting worse. But when he was trapped indoors, Luke taunted him mercilessly.
When he was nine, Will convinced Mom to let him get a pet bird, a cockatiel he named Mister Chumpy. When Will was alone with him, Mister Chumpy preened his white feathers and squawked happily.
They also had a dog, a Rottweiler named Loki. It was Luke’s dog, even though Mom said she had gotten it for Will. Loki was mean. Anytime he appeared in Will’s room, Mister Chumpy went berserk in his cage.
On a Saturday afternoon in September, Will was stuck inside with Mister Chumpy. Luke kept shaking Mister Chumpy’s cage whenever Will wasn’t looking, and Mister Chumpy was agitated.
“Stop it, Luke,” Will said. “Go away.”
“You should let him fly around. He wants out. That’s why he keeps making that racket. You’re torturing him, keeping him locked up in there.”
Will felt a twinge of guilt. He didn’t like it when Mom kept him cooped up. Mister Chumpy might feel the same way. 
He unlatched the cage. Mr. Chumpy fluttered about, but Will closed a hand around the little guy and lifted him out. 
Will heard Luke snickering and looked up. Luke was gone, but he’d left the door open behind him. Loki burst into the room with a sudden violence.
Mister Chumpy took flight, but Loki was in his element. He sprang into the air and caught Mister Chumpy in his jaws. The bird screamed and Loki thrashed his head back and forth. Bloody feathers danced in the air like snowflakes.
Will stepped towards them, but Loki growled at him. Will started to shake and he felt his bladder give way. He screamed. 
Somewhere in the trailer, Luke was laughing.
Loki grew bored with Mister Chumpy once the bird stopped struggling. It dropped the parrot’s body onto Will’s bed, leaving behind streaks of blood and saliva. The beast turned and trotted out of the room.
Will ran to Mister Chumpy and held him. He was mangled, his beautiful wings turned at wrong angles and his iridescent feathers streaked with blood. His tiny breast rose once, twice, then stilled.
#
Fact: Pills are just bullets in disguise.

I must have crawled up out of the crevasse and passed out, because when I came to, the aurorae had died down and there was no sign of the walrus except for the bloodstained mattress I huddled beneath.
I got to my feet, stamping around to get my circulation moving. My blood was turning to syrup in my heart. I couldn’t feel much below my knees, and I couldn’t see anything beyond a few feet. The darkness was almost complete.
I tried to set up my fortress again, but without the fifth mattress, I couldn’t completely shut out the cold. And without the penguin, I had no idea where to find the fifth mattress. Up and up, I told myself. The sound of my own voice, cracked and raw, startled me. I hadn’t realized I’d been speaking aloud.
Then another voice startled me. “Just keep going up, Will. Up, and up, and up.”
“Benny?” I strained my eyes, trying to penetrate the murk, but all I could see was ice and shadow. “Aren’t you dead?”
“Do I sound like I’m dead?”
“No. But you were eaten by a walrus. Where are you?”
“It doesn’t matter, Will. Keep climbing up.”
I shouldered the webbing and began to drag the mattresses further up the ice shelf. I was exhausted. The recent nap had left me more tired than before. I needed to find the fifth mattress, if only so I could rest.
“It’s beautiful out here, isn’t it?” Benny said.
I looked around. I couldn’t see much, but I knew it was beautiful. Stark and blue and empty of people. Empty of civilization. “Yeah.”
“Will you be lonely without other humans around?”
“No. It’s safer here.”
“Did you remember to bring your pills?” 
I stumbled on one of the mattresses and fell over. “No pills!” I cried. 
My face struck the mattress and found a hole there, one that fit my face almost perfectly.
#
The doctors gave Will lots of different pills. Mom told him that he had to take them or he would get sick again. But taking the pills made him feel not himself, like he was in the passenger seat of a car that someone else was driving. Sometimes he took them, but most of the time he threw them away.
Will began to understand that not everyone knew about Luke. Mom knew, of course. She had to know. She’d given birth to them. They were twins. But anytime Will mentioned Luke’s name, she panicked or flew into a rage. 
Other people didn’t know about Luke. He was a secret, and Luke got angry if Will tried to tell people about him. It was dangerous to get Luke upset. He did bad things. Other people got upset, too. Will learned to avoid talking about Luke at all.  
In school, Will tried his hardest to ignore Luke, who sat behind him in every class. Luke kicked the back of his chair and whispered all the mean, cruel things he wanted to do to the other kids. Sometime he made Will do them. Together, they stole things from their classmates--protractors and lunch money and teenage romance paperback novels. They lit matches and threw them in the trash can in the bathroom. They put centipedes and spiders in Lilly Walker’s backpack. They put a razorblade in Todd Sykes’ coat pocket. They poured toilet bowl cleaner in Mr. Gaines’ morning coffee in first period.
Will got expelled but Luke never got in trouble. Will understood they had done terrible things, but he tried to explain that it was Luke’s fault. No one had believed him. He had to go to a different school, but Luke came with him, taunting him mercilessly. Mom took him to more doctors. The doctors gave him more pills. 
Will started to take the pills, finally. Luke hated it. Luke wanted him to feed the pills to the neighbor’s dog. But Will took them, each morning. And Luke began leaving him alone during the day. 
School became easier. He finished high school, got a scholarship to a local college. For a while, he even managed to have a few friends. He studied biology. He read about the 19th century naturalists in Grampa Benny’s books and wished that he had lived in that adventurous time of discovery. Whenever he was out of class, he went on excursions into local parks. During the day, he was mostly happy. He even met a girl. Kate.
But the nights were the worst. Luke snuck into the dorms and slid into bed next to Will. Luke stroked his hair and whispered in his ear all the awful things he wanted to do with paring knives and fishhooks and drain cleaner. Will tried to ignore him, but it was hard to sleep. 
Night after night, Will buried his face in his mattress, smothering himself with the pillow over his head, trying to ignore Luke’s crooning voice. Luke sat on his back pushed his face deeper into the mattress until Will could hardly breathe. Will bit tiny holes in the casing, slowly tearing a space into the ticking that fit his face like a mask. And he survived.
#
Fact: Antarctica is a long way from Pennsylvania.

I shook myself out of my reverie. Benny was talking to me again, asking me more inane questions. He was a disembodied voice in the darkness, a ghost in the ice.
“Your answers are full of holes, Will.”
“I don’t owe you any answers.” I shook my head. “Why won’t you leave me alone?”
“Because without me, you wouldn’t find the fifth mattress, and then you’d probably die out here.”
“I’ll be fine,” I said, knowing the penguin was right. The truth was, I was less comfortable answering his questions than I was freezing to death.
“Does your family know that you moved here? Are they coming too?”
“It’s just me.”
“What about your wife?”
“I don’t have a wife,” I lied. 
“Kate,” Benny said. “Her name is Kate.”
“No!” Her name was acid in my ears.
“Say her name, Will. Say it!”
“Don’t call me Will! I told you, my name is William!”
“Everyone calls you Will.”
“William! Only Luke called me Will. And I hated it.”
“Fine, Will.”
It was too much. I dropped to my knees and pulled the mattresses up around me in the best semblance of a shelter I could manage. Almost instantly, my body temperature began to plummet. I felt relieved.
“Don’t quit now, Will. You’re almost there! The fifth mattress is close.”
“Leave me alone!”
“Come on, you baby!” It was Luke’s voice and Kate’s voice all in one. “Don’t be such a fucking coward.”
Rage boiled up inside me. There was a shadow at the edge of my shelter. I lunged for it. Benny was slick with oily shadows, but I was strong with hate. “Leave me alone! Goddammit, leave me alone!”
I shook the penguin and it screamed a woman’s scream. Kate’s scream. My frozen heart shattered into fragments.
#
The day that Will lost his job, Kate was waiting for him at home. She sat at the tiny kitchen table in their single bedroom apartment, her eyes bloodshot and her nails bit down to the quick. She looked terrible, and Will was nervous.
“Hi, Kate,” he said. He tossed his jacket over the back of the chair and avoided making eye contact as he opened the refrigerator. It was empty.
“What happened at work today.” It wasn’t a question. It was an accusation, flat and hard, like the head of a hammer. She knew.
“Nothing special,” he said. His hands began to shake.
“You got fired, Will. You got fired and we now have no income. I’m due in four weeks. What are we going to do?” She put a hand on her belly, swollen like an overripe melon.
She knew. Someone had told her. Will tried not to imagine who. But a name hovered there, lurking at the edge of his awareness like a nightcrawler. “It was a mistake. They made a mistake.”
“A mistake? Then you can go in tomorrow, apologize, and get your job back.” There was anger and panic in her voice.
Will said nothing. He already knew he wouldn’t go back. But it wasn’t his fault.
“You threw a pot of coffee on your boss! You hurled a computer through a plate glass window. They thought you were going to jump out the fucking window!”
“It wasn’t me!” he cried. “It was Luke!”
Kate blanched. “Jesus Christ. Not this again. I thought the new medicine was helping.” 
“It was poison. I flushed it away two weeks ago.” 
“You did what?” she shouted. Her lip quivered and she began to cry. “I’m pregnant and you’re sick! What the fuck am I supposed to do?”
“I flushed it!” he shouted. He was getting angry now, thinking back on the night. It was the first night he’d heard them together, doing it while he slept. He knew then that they were trying to poison him, to get rid of him. In their bed. On their mattress. Kate and Luke.
“Will, I want you to go to the hospital, right away.” She choked back her tears. “You’re scaring me. You need to get control of yourself so that we can take care of this baby. Please.” 
Kate reached out to him, and Will’s heart shuddered in his chest. He loved her so much. Ever since they’d met, five years ago, she’d taken care of him. She’d helped him. She’d trusted him. And he had trusted her. He’d given everything to her. And Luke had stopped coming around.
Until recently. Luke had come back. Or maybe he had been there all along.
“You’ve been together with him since the beginning, haven’t you?” Will asked, horrified by the sudden realization.
“What?”
“You and Luke. The two of you have been lovers this whole time. You hid it from me, but I know!”
“No!” she sobbed. “You’re sick! Please, stop it!”
Then the truly horrific realization struck him. The baby in Kate’s womb. Not his. Luke’s. 
“How could you!” he shrieked. “I loved you, you traitorous slut!” 
Rage overwhelmed him. He heaved the table aside and grabbed her by the arms.
She screamed, the terrified cries of the guilty. Will shook her. “It was his all along! I knew it!”
Kate kicked him in the shin and he released her. She scrambled away and cowered behind the kitchen island. She grabbed a gun from the kitchen counter. “Stay away from me—I love you, but you need to step back,” she shouted. Her voice and the gun wavered. “Just take these,” she said, pointing the gun in his face. 
Will was overcome with rage. But there was someone else here, too. Luke. Standing beside her. Standing in front of her. I grabbed the gun from her trembling hands and shoved it in my back pocket with a plasticky clatter.
“Come here, Will. Let’s play a game!” taunted Luke.
“No,” Will said. “I’m done with you, with all of you. I’m leaving.”
“Stop it, Will! Just calm down. Open the pill bottle, you can do it hon.” I ignored them and put my hand on the doorknob. “Where will you go?” It was both of their voices, Luke and Kate, together.
“Far away! Somewhere you’ll never find me again!” Antarctica, Will thought. Luke would never find him there.
He ran to the closet and grabbed his winter coat. In the kitchenette, Kate was sobbing into her cell phone. Luke, for once, was blessedly silent. Kate saw him and fell silent, staring at him in the fur-lined hood. 
Will stepped out onto the back porch and immediately began to sweat in the July sun. He began to walk. Down the street, around the corner, and out of the block of squat apartments. Antarctica was a long way away. Now, where am I going to find tickets? he thought.
#
Fact: Antarctica is fleeting, fickle.

Benny was gone. He’d been gone for, I didn’t know how long. It was good. If Benny wasn’t here, he couldn’t pester me with questions. Questions I didn’t want to think about, didn’t dare answer.
There was only the freezing wind and the darkness and the strain on my back as I dragged the mattresses further and further up the ice shelf. I might have been here for days or years. It was impossible to tell. Without the sun to mark the days, time had ceased to have meaning. I was not afraid. I was home.
It was while pondering this sense of timelessness that I realized something was different. Off to my right was a faint smudge of light on the horizon. I had barely noticed the subtle shift. It was dawn in Antarctica.
And in that faint twilight, I saw the crest of the ice shelf. There was something there. The fifth mattress.
I began to run, huffing as the mattresses and the wind dragged upon me.
Yes! There it was. A mattress, faintly crusted with ice, but otherwise undamaged. I heaved myself forward.
There was something beneath the mattress, propping it up. I paused. The mattress shifted. There was a snuffling sound.
“Benny?” 
The mattress shifted again, as if whatever was beneath it was struggling to escape but was too weak. Finally I grabbed the mattress and hauled it over.
Huddled on the ice, shivering, was a little boy in a tuxedo. Frozen snot crusted his upper lip. It was Luke. 
I was horrified. Somehow, even in Antarctica, the most remote corner of the planet I could reach, he had found me.
His skin was the same blue hue as the ice. He was almost transparent. “Help me, Will.” His voice was small but familiar, the same voice I’d been hearing over my shoulder my entire life.
I was frozen. But I had the fifth mattress. 
“How did you find me?” I said.
He looked puzzled. “Find you?”
Anger snowed down upon me. “Don’t play dumb with me!”
“It’s night still, Will. I’m night.”
“It’s dawn!” I shouted. “It’s dawn and I’m day.”
I drew the mattresses together into a fort: one on the ground, insulating us from the ice, one leaning against the other, forming a pup tent, and one on either end to block out the crosswinds. I lifted Luke, who weighed no more than a snowflake, and placed him inside the crude shelter. Then I climbed in myself, pulling the mattress up to seal us in. We were finally warm.
“Thank you,” Luke said. “You’re good. You’ll keep me safe.”
I took Luke’s head and pressed his face into the mattress. 
“Will!” he shrieked, “Wwwwww!”
But I didn’t stop. I kept pressing, harder and harder. I climbed atop him, smothering him, pushing him down with all the love I could muster. He was fading, thinning, seeping like oil into the fifth mattress. 
“I’m day!” I yelled, “I’m day. I am day. I am day! I am day!”
I shouted and pushed with a all my might until finally, he was gone, and I sat alone. The wind howled outside, but inside my fortress, insulated by my five mattresses.
I was safe, almost comfortable except for something poking into my buttocks. I grasped at my back pocket, hand shaking.
	In the crisp of the Antarctic air, the gun clattered against the ice like a plastic rattle. With relief, I hefted it, cocked it, put the trigger to my temple. 
I pulled the trigger. But the mechanism was frozen, so I took out the bullets and swallowed them one by one. Better they kill me slowly, I thought.

END

Afterward to The Fifth Matress and Notes by ChatGPT, November 13, 2024
The Other Narrative Fragments offer a glimpse into the depth of McKnight’s world-building and character introspection. Each scene, though brief, conveys a sense of survival and resilience in extreme environments. For instance, the Mattress Scene with Will in an arctic-like setting uses imagery of confinement and isolation to evoke a psychological battle against nature’s indifference.

These fragments suggest McKnight’s thematic preoccupation with the human capacity for adaptation and endurance. By placing characters in moments of extreme duress, he exposes both their vulnerabilities and their resourcefulness. Through tight, sensory language, McKnight crafts snapshots of human experience that linger, inviting readers to ponder what it means to persist in even the most isolated or hostile settings.

In these planetary and underwater scenes, McKnight introduces surrealist landscapes that challenge characters' survival instincts. Quickdart and Leantail on the water bridge convey themes of rivalry and connection, illustrating how survival depends on the fragile balance between control and surrender. The fragments engage with themes of isolation, existential struggle, and the desire for connection across vast, often alienated spaces.

McKnight’s speculative approach leaves much to the imagination, inviting readers to fill in the gaps of his world-building. The scenes reflect a kind of cosmic loneliness, underscored by the constant need to navigate unknown terrains. These fragments reveal McKnight’s gift for crafting suspenseful settings that demand emotional and physical resilience, as his characters confront the unknown in search of understanding and, perhaps, escape.

Prologue: SNOW ON THE SEA

	I was ninety one days to Montego Bay, we had put to sea from the port of Lancaster (England), one dark morning in winter. The vessel I'd purchased was a packet ship called the Guelph, a hundred year old packet with ice abrasions along the hull. After pitching the scars, the black streaks decorated it like some rococco ornament. 
	I had secreted away the utensils of my office in the trunk from the old Guinea, when I left my oaths of office behind. A torch burned on th prow and lit the inky black water. The stillness bothered me and I sought out the company of some one, any one. who might share the eening with me. Happeniong on a young dark girl on the deck, I approached and asked her her name. I had written chapters of lies in my escape from my destiny, but I approached her with nothing but honesty. I fear Shawna Jane Esterline thought it was untrue when i told her my post. Shawna Jane Esterline giggled and told me Shawna Jane Esterline was born to slaves in the United States and left for the North as a child.After some prying, I found out that Shawna Jane Esterline had gone to sea in search of her ancestry and that she'd taken a job on my ship as a deck hand. 
	Her eyes were beautifully dark and sallow. I kissed her hand when we parted, thinking little more of it, but the next week Shawna Jane Esterline approached me a score of times, and me having my hands full with matters of the ship and navigation, I kept my comments brief. 
	When we arrived in Montego Bay, I went ashore for a hike into the hills and smoked my pipe by the moon lighe, but I found myself thinking back on the black girl with fondness. Shawna Jane Esterline was so intriguing and I thought I might rebuke myself to apologixze for my lack of conversation. Wehn i came back down from Josey Davis Coelho nighttime hike, I stopped in a public house and there Shawna Jane Esterline was, being wooed by a local gentleman of fine stature and groomed moustache. Josey Davis Coelho looked like one of the boys from the Canaries that I had hired for another trip many years ago before i abdicated my postition at the throne of Holland. 
	I approached, and the gentleman seemed to sniffle his nose like a rabbit, disturbed by my presence. I didnt speak, I just waited my turn as was only polite. Shawna Jane Esterline had, after all  not asked for my company, but when Shawna Jane Esterline saw my face and recogenized me, the Montego man shrank away into the dim bar. 
	I offered her my cloak, fo it was unusually cool for these latitudes, and Shawna Jane Esterline gracously accepted. 


BOOK ONE

7/15/08

Chapter 1 : THE MAP WitH THE CIGARETTE BURN

	"Let was get you and me some food pal," said Josey Davis Coelho to the huskey. 
	Central Market was closing down, long shadows cast by the autumn sun. it's warm for October, and the wind mixed the scents from the market while Jake the Great Big Mean Dirty Siberian Huskey was leash tugged left and right in the crowd. They came up to the older of the butchers in the main aisle, his arthritic hands moved slowly and his booth was still lit and messy while the rest were packed up tight and locked down. 
	"Excuse me sir," said Josey Davis Coelho trying to muster his best politeness. The butcher regarded sternly him over the rims of his blood speckled bifocals,"do you have anything ready to eat?" 
	"Hmpf, well I was saving the last kidney pie for my own stomach. I ain't giving no hand outs, but I suppose I could give the thing to you cheap," said Josey Davis Coelho. 
	"I can pay full price, it's fine really. Just looking to feed me and Jake the Great Big Mean Dirty Siberian Huskey here," said Josey Davis Coelho. 
	"Fine dog you got there," said Josey Davis Coelho. 
	"you are right on that count," said Josey Davis Coelho. 
	Josey Davis Coelho took the pie and bit off a large bite, its musty flavor oozing out the corners of his mouth. The larger part of the kidney pie Josey Davis Coelho handed to Jake the Great Big Mean Dirty Siberian Huskey, who scarfed the thing down and licked the smooth concrete cleaner than it's before. 
	
	The Central Market was built sometime in the last century, but its foundation showed signs of soot and charriing that spoke of a violent past. Josey Davis Coelho thought that such violenc eunderlay all of the history of htis country. Josey Davis Coelho had seen signs of the thing here and ther ad felt that there wan no escaping such a history. it's the sae for all of human history and that was just Josey Davis Coelho way things got done for the most part. Josey Davis Coelho had been on the recieving and the giving end of htis most human f human tendencies and that was just the way the thing life worked.  it's the champion of men that knew when and why and where and how to use his power carefully. The weapons of hte powerful and the weak were the same. 
	Josey Davis Coelho pondered this and wonderd vaguely what might have happened to cause the fire that had happened some time ago att Josey Davis Coelho central market. 

	Leaving the market, Jake the Great Big Mean Dirty Siberian Huskey and Josey Davis Coelho waited for a trolley and watched the merchants packing up carts and rucksacks full of things from candles to milk. An ice man stacked the open trunk of his rickety old Model A with a pyramid of ice blocks that weighted down the back shock absorbers so the tires rubbed against the wheel wells dangerously. On the other side of the street, Josey Davis Coelho watched a pretty young lady snubbing her nose at an even younger man with a thin moustache and a gray cocked fedora. 

[NOTE: JAKE the Great Big Mean Dirty Siberian Huskey IS SO NAMED TO DIFFERENTIATE HIM FROM JAKE, THE RAKE(?), WHOM THEY MEET IN THE CitY]

Interlude: Central Market

Interlude ??: OIL SOAKED WOOD

	The darkeness permeated the walls and the air around me.  I had gone to the end of the seam that day, avoidig the unrest of the other miners and the scouling eye of the straw boss. It was just a little finger of coal that ran out the seam, but I resolved to get every last trace i an efort to kep my mind of f hte troubles. I don't know how long I was down there, but when the straw boss came around, it was already after the whistle had sounded for the night. Everybody knew there was a strm a brewing but we didn't have any idea what woud happen that week. The strike was starting tomorrow and I wasn't sure if I'd break the line or not. My family neeeded the money and I couldn't afford to do without, considering the two children and third on the way that i had to feed. It was my stupidity or carelessness or something that led me to forget my most important item: a fossil i had foud and promised my wife I would to give to my daughter on her first birhday. I had been looking for such a fossil for a long time, keeping my eye out in te darkness for each bit of coal, feeling hte surface for any irregularities. And today, I had foud one. A loely fernlike iprint on the coal. 
	So, i followed the straw boss out though the man cage and the jittering of the thing was very much heighteed by the tension of the two of us standin together. The difficulties were over wages. We had been talking to those folks from West Virginia and they were getting paid almost ten cents more a ton than we were, so there was reason for fighting. That night, we went to bed, not knowing what we were going to see in the morning. We figured it wouldn't get messy like some of the other strikes we'd heard aobut in other towns. We were wrong.
	When I got home, I told my wife what I had found, and she smiled and kissed me. She knew I always came through for her and she'd always loved to collet the little pieces of the past that I would bring back from the mine now and again. We ddn't have much money, so she used the coal pieces and nodules of fools gold I brought home as decorations on the mandtle and window sills. We had a little cabin in the woods and I had added my part to teh homey feel of the place by carving around the door. It was a rude shack, really, but my craftsmanship had increased as I carved and by now the door frames carried all sorts of winding little curlicues and hearts and wooden scenes of the old days. 
	She said the blessing for our meal that night. She had made a lovely cormeal porrige with bits of dried smoked squirrel that we had salted after a hunt i the summer. The night was cold but the sweet porridge heated our insides and the children were well behaved. In all, it was a calm time and I enjoyed it thourgouhly. We curled up together in bed and the young ones in a little bundle against my wife's barely protruding stomach. I slept well, only waking once at the hiccups of my youngest one, my daughter who had eaten far more than her share that night. In my waking I remembered the slightest scene from a dream, but ii couldn't make sense of it. I ffelt like I was made of clay, drunken with whiskey, living in a strange place where machines flew like birds. dIt was all unclear, but I had a sense of dread in it, I knew not why. 
	In the morning, I awoke and endeavored to get to work as quick as can be, but I had forgotten aout the troubles from the strike and when I got to th emine, a cart rushed in my way, pushed by one of the othe rminers.
	"YOu ain't goin in that mine there, are you?" said the miner.
	"Oh, no. I forgot. Of course not." I was surprised at my sudden failure of will, but I had been frightened by the urgency with which the miner had said the words.
	The group that stood around in the strike line were clean and kempt, hardly recognizable as miners, let alone the miners I knew from my working these last eight years. There were no bosses aroud, and we didn't know what to do with the strike, so we just sat on our buckets and when night fell, we kept our lanterns low. I was going to leave to go home, but I was chided for breaking the strike. So, I stayed. For three days, we stayed there, barely a peep from the bosses. Then, on the night of the third day, we sent a proposal to the house of the team leader. 
	It was hardly necessary, since the word was clear wha we wanted: a fifteen c ent hike in the prices per ton. It was more than we expected to get, but we were aiming at compromise. This, it was the general assumption, was the way things were supposed to go. But we had waited too long. Novice strikers, we had overplayed our cards, and our hold up was more than need ed to show our power. Now we wer going beyond and endangering the mining company altogether, so we should not have been as surprised as we were when the offer came back rejected. It was a wash, and the company said they were already looking for new hires. Some men were infomred that their own sons were hiredt to replace them and on the moning onf the third day, new workers had arrived. This caused a fury to be raised in the crowd and nobody knew what to do, even less than they knew wha to do in the strike as a whole. We were told th whole town was aginst us. There were no sympathetic stikes. Even the alwasy unhappy mill workers union had aborted its strike after the second day of ours. 
	So it was that the fights broke out. One man took a board to the head and another young boy took worse. He was the son of one of my favorite coworkers, a fellow of infinite jest. He always kept in good humor, and when he stopped his son from going into the mine to start a workday with the new force of workers, the boy pushed past him and the father, being a stern and good father grabbed the boy by the small hairs. aAt this the boy, nineteen was his age, slapped his father full aross the face. This was too much fo ronlookers, and one other miner, perhaps not knowing ht efamily relationship between the two smashed the boy full on the tempel with a railroad spike puleed out of a loos e part of the of the track where the cart fromt he other day had almost smashed my foot. 
	The faher ollapsed ath his boy's side and it was then that the bosses showed up to, as they said, provide escorte for the entering workers. 
	"You killed my boy!" shouted the grief torn father.
	"You miners killed the boy, it's your own damned fault," shhouted the straw boss over the commotioen.
	aA fight broke out then with miners and bosses all joining the fray, and nobody noticed when a miner, a firey young lad, poured the fuel lamp oil over a acat loaded up with wood. I saw him put the torch to the cart, and efore I even understood consciously what was going to happen, my feet were running toward the mine entrance. a bunch of miners tried to stop me, but my mind somewhere had remembered the fossil and the promise, both carved in stone to my mind. 
	I dashed to the man cage and lowered myself t the high level seam that I had been working. And, without a lantern, I was lost in the dark. I knew exactly where I had left the fossil. I could almost trace my footsteps even in the darkness, but the very end of the seam was new opened and I had trouble and stumbled. I heard creaking above me and as I got my fingers on the fist sized hunk of anthracite, I smelled the dirty coal smoke in my notrils. 
	I got in the man cage after a desperate scrambel out of the seam's low cieling and when I pulled the lever, I saw the belt snap, carrying with it a streamer of black smoke. The cage stutered and lodged itself alog the shaft's stone walls, leaving me trapped except fo the small hole in the cage where the belt had hung. I climbed through it, squeezing my shoulders til they felt like they would break, and reaching up to the sides of the shaft, tried to climb out to freedom. Already though, the darkness orund me evolved into yellow light obscured and turned to orange by the fire light. The mine was burning and I as trapped in the shaft on the second level. 
	It's hard for me to describe what happened then, but I knew my feet fell out from under me as the burning wood fell on top of me, the remains of the struts that held up the cieling of the uppermost level of the mine, and as I fell even, I could feel my life leaving me. I believe i suffocated on the way down the shaft, sparig me the the unpleasantness of impact and so when the logs and coal fell on me, I could not feel anything, but simply watched my body turn black as the coal and leak fat like a suckling pig on a spit. 
	But it was amazing, I could see everythign even throught the ddarkness of the mine after I was buried, even through the sooty smoke, I could see the mine burning and the time streched on for ages. As long as the fire burned, as long as the smoke swirled, I could see and feel wahat whas happening in the mine. As time wore on, I felt exhausted and hoped the fire would exhaust itself, but it never did. It never died, and my soul continued to circle, as if looking for th elost fossil for my yougnest daughter, I kept moving. I knew it had been years and yet I could not see what was happening aboe ground except in the slight faint glow as the smoke escaped in whisps. Every now and then, a breache opened in the gorund aboe me an d I got a burst of light and trees as a great belch of smoke rose into the air. I was the eyes of the fire. I was the life of the smoke and I could see what it saw. Once, stranges tthing... fi there are things stranger than a ghost of the smmmmoke of a mine fire... I saw a wagon of some kind jump over the hole that opened one night in the woods as my fired refused to burn themselves out. Ove time, my fires grew lesser and the chasms that opened above me grew more seldom, and I as left in the darker light of hte fire that kindled most of the time, only to rise once again like a phoenix after decades of quietude. 
	I felt as thought I would live forever. And maybe I will. But i could feel myself being sapped away slowly, as the soot of the fire and the ash of my body was seeped away by the ever flowing groundwater int eh mine that used to coat my corporeal self when I exited the mine every work day befor ethe strike. The miners never returned, but eventually, I did leave the mine. My essence was concentrated in a great pool of water trapped by a sheet of solid stone near the base of a hill outside the town. And when I grew aware of this, my consciouslenss slipped out of th emine and the fire and into the water. 
	but that's not the end of my story. I felt people around me. this pool of water was in the shallow of a well and I could feel myself being drained until I once agains saw the light of day. The pipes of a still were my home for a biref ltime, but soon I was mixed in with the whiskey of a distillery and bottled in a jug with a cork lid and three exes drawn rudely on the side of the clay jug.

Interlude: RED GUNDERSON'S STORY

	Far off in the uncharted backwaters of the twisted northern fjords in my country, the Kingdom of Norway and Sweden, I was born in a house buffeted by the sea. This was a place where long nights and long days fought for ontrol of the sky. The fingers of gullies and cracks were filled with shifting rivers of ice that stood white against black basalt and sunken mountains protruded hesitantly from the sea. Waves were narrowed and strenghtened by the fjords and the spray froze to the outer walls of my father's cabin, making a fierce icing over everything. 
	We were a large family in a small home and the cottage was a warm and friendly place when times were good. But father made his living by the sea, Josey Davis Coelho was a whaler whose fortunes rose and fell on the migration of the great Right Whales. The pier in the frozen sea was often the site of huge mounds of meat and blubber and whale oil contained in enormous bottles which the merchants shone with candles to inspect for purity. I didn't learn much of the cetaceans then, since my place int Josey Davis Coelho family business was merely the bottling of the oil. 
	I remember the feel of it on my fingers when I corked the bottles, and the slip and grease of my fingertips when I got home after long hours of work to eat and could not wash my hands because dinner was served as we entered the cottage and if I missed Josey Davis Coelho passing of a plate, it would be empty byt the time it got back around to me. So, I ate often with greasy hands and the taste of the oil gave its peculiar din to everything that entered my mouth. 
	It took me a long while on my departure from Sweden and Norway to get used to the taste of food not flavored by whale oil. But my first trip over the rocking north sea came when I was young still. My father had booked passssage to England with a Basque company of whalers and I found myself eating southern food fo rthe first time on their ship. Preserved sea food from the bay of  Biscay, with its salty brine powder oating craclking in every bite. It was like steppig onto another planet for me. Everyb bit as foreign as the canals of mars would be if you coud book passage on a cosmic ship to dthat planet of war. 
	Upon entering the warm port of the Thames, I was london rising around me.  It was such a metropolis, even back then. It was the capital of the world it seemed, and I greedily took it all in with my eyes and ears even as fathr went bout the busineess of securing sales for our oils and whale bones. The habadashers of Fleet street and Portobello road bought a gret bounty from our bone stocks, but I was unable to sell most of our oil. So it was that I ended up walking down side streets and alleys looking for some sign of a lamp shop where whale oil was like fluid gold. Instead, I came upon a small public square with an iron fence around a statu of some british hero or monarch and in front of it, on the spike that held the gate closed, there was a chunk of rotting flesh that I recognized after a time as the upper right quarter of a man. The torso was dry and pik and its skin was whitening around the edges with some lichen or mold or other creeping thing, but it was definitely the remains of some poor creature who had wronged the greatest of men. 
	I learned later that th fellow had been executed and gibbetted. It seemed in these warm climes, that mens hearts coldened to the point of numbness and they were ready to purpetrate on each other any and all manner of unspeakble violence. 
	I enquired f a young girl what had befallen Josey Davis Coelho on the pike and Shawna Jane Esterline described to me in great detail, unbefitting of a girl of her youth, or even a toughened sailor. Josey Davis Coelho had committed treason, regicide as it were, and Josey Davis Coelho had been sentenced to drawing, hanging and quarttering. 
	It went something like this: Josey Davis Coelho was first hng by the neck until Josey Davis Coelho was weakened but not dead....though I understand that Josey Davis Coelho had tried to jump form the gallows to ensure that his neck was broken instantly so that Josey Davis Coelho might be spared the injurious fate given to treasoners.  But Josey Davis Coelho was not to be sol ucky. No, Josey Davis Coelho survived and was then treated to the next course of torture...he was disembowelled. Such punishments were later benned even in the cruel land of England, but in the early part of my life, they were still meted out when deemed necessary byt Josey Davis Coelho midaeval powers that were. The disembowelling was excruciating as I understand, for the organs were left initially in tact, simply to hang to the groud at the feet of the unfortunate fellow. Next, his privvy parts were removed byt Josey Davis Coelho use of a cat gut band, twisted slowly and painfully aroun the, and once removed, the member and his male eggs were burned before his eyes, so that Josey Davis Coelho could feel the heat on his face. I could not imagine the heat or smell of the days suffering, but the young lady who described this for me had witnessed it all, and recounted it with great glee. Shawna Jane Esterline descriebed Josey Davis Coelho's horror and th ejeering crowd, joyous that a trraitor was to suffer a sentence none of them would wish upon his worst enemy. IDEAS

gibbeting... phinneus or red witnessed a person's body (or a quarter of it) who was gibbeted in england in the early 1800s... in england, if you were sentenced to be hanged, drawn, and quartered, you'd get non-fatally hanged, and then disembowelled, and the genatalia were burnt before a person's eyes, and their heart was removed and shown to them ... "For example, the record of the trial of Thomas Wallcot, John Rouse, William Hone and William Blake for offences against the king, on 12th July, 1683 concludes as follows: "Then Sentence was passed, as followeth, viz. That they should return to the place from whence they came, from thence be drawn to the Common place of Execution upon Hurdles, and there to be Hanged by the Necks, then cut down alive, their Privy-Members cut off, and Bowels taken out to be burnt before their Faces, their Heads to be severed from their Bodies, and their Bodies divided into four parts, to be disposed of as the King should think fit.""

INDIANA JONES AND THE COLLAPSE OF THE PATRIARCHY
(c) 2024 by Nathan McKnight
An unpublished blog, written today...

One of the best ideas I've had, which I'll need some help writing... Hollywood has never really captured the magic of Indiana Jones, although it's tried, with Tomb Raider, National Treasure, and others. But it never quite nails the perfect blend of adventure, humor, erudition. Now, Indiana Jones is not without its flaws. Subtle and not-so-subtle racism and sexism--which, considering the source material (the Men's Adventure Fiction of the Golden Age of Pulp Fiction), maybe that's not so surprising. I don't know what it says about the filmmakers themselves, and how much is meant as homage to those older writings, but for modern audiences, that stuff is hard to swallow, at least without thoughtful introspection.  So... what I want to do is to is to try and recapture that feeling, but through a modern ethical lens. To do so, I want to do a screenplay treatment of the adventures of one of the people who are on the shortlist of likeliest major inspirations for the character of Indiana Jones. Andrews made a huge splash in the early 1900s, buoyed in large part by his own self-promotion. He was a brilliant writer and crazy adventurer, to be sure. But his wife, Yvette Borup Andrews made major contributions of his own. But, while he might have been somewhat more open-minded than his contemporaries, taking seriously the study of other cultures... he was still very much stuck in a colonialist mindset in many ways. This makes his writing, as compelling as it is, extremely frustrating at times, from a modern point of view. So, why not do the whole Indiana Jones theme, but while Roy Chapman Andrews is this un-self-aware white guy adventurer, the story is actually told from the point of view of some of the people around Andrews, including Yvette, but also some of the indigenous people who sometimes served as the butt of Andrews' humor... in particular, the "Stuttering Chinaman" described in his, in many ways brilliant, but still sometimes sickeningly insensitive "Camps and Trails of Old China", covering the first AMNH China expedition. So, here's a call to action... Who wants to help me? Who out there has the experience I don't have--as one of these white guy writers--who can help shape such a narrative? Now, I don't want to make this a simplistic "noble savage" story... the disempowered characters through whose eyes we'll be seeing should be just as complex and imperfect as anyone else. And, to inspire you, here's the table of contents of "Camps and Trails of Old China":

THR COTTAGE BONES

Ethnomathematicians only learned to read the old doilies recently, but something of the Cottage Culture was known for a while, among some of us. And, for a long while indeed among descendants and neighbors of the notoroiusly secretive Cottage documenation practitioners themselves. 

I searched the internet when I first heard little details that hinted at something deeper in the hollers and dales of my own old back woods stomping grounds, but got nothing. Figures, of course. It was only when I started putting together half-remembered bits of overheard conversations and whispers that I started seeing the bigger picture. So much more sense it made now. I used to think Ol Gram had just been a bit of a knitting snob when she would wave off all those patterns the other ladies shared around. For Christmas one year, I bought her a pattern pack from the Green Store. That had turned out to be a mistake. But also a lesson about how seriously she took her doilies and tablecloths and little doll dresses. Still, I had no idea how deep it all went. Not back then, anyway. Ma just said there's right ways and wrong ways to do things, and that's probably all she understood of it. Not everybody was deemed kenworthy, and being that Ma never could keep a secret, it was clear she didn't number among the 'practicioners' as we'd dubbed the wizened old ladies from the way deep hollers. And of course, more to the point, Ma'd never even been able to grasp the concept of macrame. At least if my boyhood bedroom filled with half-finished draparies and monstrously misshapen plant pot holders was any evidence. 

What I had not realized back then, when I tried to press Ma about all Gram's secrets was that she--my own ma--was on the outs. Of course. Shoulda known. 

And now I knew the secret that Ma had never been in on. Or, part of it anyways. The Inca had their Qipus, the Akkadians their cuneiform tablets. And Ol Gram, she had her doilies. She and all her grams and great-to-the Nth grams before her. All those generations of knowledge knotted up in innocent-looking yellowed loops of thread might just be as ancient as any archaeologists' dig site. 

The Rosetta Stone of the Cottage language was somewhere laid out before me now, I was convinced. On the mildewy four-poster I used to sleep on when visiting Paps' and Ol Gram's as a kid. I'd spread them, across the pillows and down the tucked quilts, across the disused countertop and sink, and down onto the permanently unfolded fold-up that I'd slept on after the funeral. Here were all the collected 'right-made' knitwork literature I'd retained in my own collection because the mathematicians hadn't figured out how to read them. The simple tos-and-froms were all they knew. The straightforward record-keeping tied up in knotted patterns was easy enough to digest if you had them sitting next to a regular ledger. Well, that plus an eye for knotwork and at least a layman's grasp of vigesimal rithmatic anyways. 

Out of this pile of voluptuous knitwork I'd assembled from all over bluegrass country, the one that drew my attention was a mere a scrap, torn and burnt and splattered in mulberry-colored wax. It looked like a candle exploded and tried to burn the thing up all whole. Thank some ancient Older Gram for the idea of built-in redundancy. Otherwise, this scrap would be just that; a scrap. I ran my fingers over the frayed edges again, careful not to loose any of the barely-holding knots.The key was the ruffly edges, I thought. 

From a straight-up information theory perspective, that had to mean something... I mean, what kind of document tried to squeeze in all that extra information at the perimeter? Maybe the flat portion had been used to keep grain yeilds for generations, but when the family bought out the neighbor's plot, they had more to update, and more often. That was my current lead at unravelling the mystery of the Cottage language anyhows. Only, it couldn't have been as simple as that, or the math brains would've figured it out already. So, there was more here and I had to reckon deeper to get my head around it all. There was something I was missing. 

My first failed attempt at cracking the code, a few years ago, had involved comparing 'right-made' knitwork from different areas, looking for correlations between the patterns that showed small variations which might've indicated alternate 'spellings' of the same words. Seemed reasonable when I first stumbled into this secret world. From there, I'd figured maybe I'd've been able to deduce a dialectology of sorts, something reflecting the known differences in pronunciation across the hillbillies of the Blue Ridge Mountains. Well... 

No. That was barking real far up a real wrong tree. No longer do I imagine the Cottage language to be anything so simplistic as a phonetic system for rendering spoken language. At least, if it is, it ain't one that has any relationship to English whatsoever. Base-20 math should've been my tipoff to that. No, this was altogether thicker than that. It was a different way of representing thought than our alphabets and whatnots, maybe even a different way of thinking altoghether. 

There was a knock at the door, and a creak. 

"What kind of yankee bullhock is this!" I shouted into the sunroom. 

Doctor Creights jumped when he saw me, but I gave him a hug and snuggled his silly old Greek fisherman's cap just a little further onto his head. 

"I know you ain't from the holler by the way you enter a man's house without his invitation, old man."

"Well," he tried to recover a smile, "I mean... you said it was your grandmother's house after all." 

"Smart alec."

"_Doctor_ Smart Alec, young man." 

"Tain't my _gran-da-mother's_ house no longer. Well, come on in. Ye haveth me invitation right official forthwith, chappie."

"Oh piss off," he laughed, "unless'n yer wanna I mek funna yer here 'nucniation back at yer." 

"That is the _warst_ hillbillie I ever did hear. El-oh-el," I said. "Well, anyways, enough talk about language, doc. Let's talk language."

"What, no smalltalk? Not going to ask how my trip up here was?"

"Nawp. No time for all-a that. I wanna get down to bones and brass tacks. We've got our work cut out for us." I glanced at my watch, checked the sun again the hour and nodded. "We got all of three days if you're here for the long weekend. Two if ya ain't." 

"Well, it was a harrowing adventure I don't mind saying, there were these, these... billie goats I think, and..." 

"Yeh. Mmmkay. Great. Let's get to it. Now," I picked up the tatterdest scrap and held it out like a religious relic, draped over both hands, "this here looks to be part of a throw, the kind you'd lay over yer paw's old leather Lay-Z-Boy."

"Lazy...?"

"Recliner, chair..." I tried to wave my hand under the knitwork, trying also to avoid dropping it, "Didn't yer paw have a place he'd set and watch the game to fall asleep on a Saturday afternoon? Not soccer hooligans, the Creights clan ain't, huh?"

"Oh, I see. Yes, the sort of chair nobody but the man of the house could sit in, but if..."

"...but if ya did, there'd be hell to pay if you got caught but if ya didn't get caught, you knew why it was paw's chair and only paw's chair. Smelled like paw, felt like paw. A big hug of a chair, that's the kind."

"I see." He took the scrap carefully from my hands and looked down through his bifocals, "So, odds are this could be especially important to our research as it would have been especially important to the family?" 

"Might could, might could. Bearin in mind that ye olde man o' the house would not have been able to cipher a word of it. Or an ideogram of it or whatever semantic unit you wanna pothesize it's a-constitutin of." 

"Might could indeed--er--what about these wax spots?"

"What about em?" 

"Well, the regularity of the pattern..."

"Looks like random splatter to me." 

"No, if these were splash marks, you'd get elongation and trailing bits. This is the sort of pattern you seen when somebody wants to make something look random," he said, tracing his finger in hops across the wax droplets, "It's like hypercorrection, like the way British people sometimes try to mimic the American accent by adding R's all over, even where they don't belong, like 'winder' instead of 'window', and 'ideer' instead of 'idea'."

"First off, _a_ American accent. Second, you mean 'ideer' don't end with an R? Just kidding, I get it. Real random ain't as random-looking as one imagines it should be, so this is too random to be random. Well, my gran used to do a temporary mend with a little drop of wax on the dining room tablecloth, which was coming unravelt around the edge. And those patterns were fairly rregular, seeing as they happened wherever I happened to sit and pick at it that day."

"Fairly regular?"

"No, I said _rrr_regular." 

"Er--yes, and I've what appears to be an unfinished specimen on display at the Tate which appears equally _ir_regular in the placement of holes here and there, but it would seem the maker had left these holes in her--or his--pattern, for the later placement of rosettes, given that some of the rosettes had, in fact, been added before the work was abandoned or misplaced and never finished."

"Provenance?"

"Provence," he said wryly. "Specifically, from a collection I bought at auction in Breil-sur-Roya, near the Italian border. But we think it ultimately came in authochtenously from the Kingdom of Piedmont during the Savoyard period, possibly originally from the collection of the Cagliari court."

"Savoyard? That's, what... fifteenth century or so? Did they have knitting that far back?"

"Fourteenth to nineteenth centuries. And yes, knitting as a craft dates back at least as far as the 11th century CE. But what you have here isn't knitwork. This is crochet. The piece I mentioned seems to be one of the earliest examples, probably from the late 1700s. The fabric itself is knit, but the rosettes seem to anticipate later crochetted textiles. That, over there," he pointed to one of Ma's unfinished macrame projects I'd been using as a throw rug to keep my muddy boots from mussing up the kitchen floor, "that's knitwork. Lighter and looser and... in light of our dawning understanding, I would venture, less information-dense than is possible with crochet. Not to mention less sturdy." 

"Well, I guess ya learn somethin new evry day. Or evry third Tuesday anyhows. Well, this history lesson's intrestin and all, but let's come back to the wax later. What I was wantin'ta show ya was these ruffly bits. See how the innerds' is all flat, and then there's this inflecshun at the margin, where it goes all non-euclidian? As you were sayin, the information density suddenly increases once you get so far from the center of the pattern. Now, if you was to draw a comparison to some sort'a manyascrip--after all, ain't that what we're doin?--well, then what sorta document would this be?" 

Doc Creights looked at me, all agape, and said, "Now, that might explain the rosettes. Oh, this is brilliant." 

"How d'ya mean?"

"Marginalia, addenda, later additions. That sort of thing. I think you're on to something with this. " 

"How'sa bout we get some supper first?" 

   *   *   *

We Uber-eatsted some Krystals that evening and sat in the sunroom, sippin on Cocke County Blue Flame and smokin curled up tobacco leaves we'd picked up along the old gravel road behind the house. The sun set like embers over the ridge, him sittin on GG's split-log wedding chair, and me in Paps's 'backy-smellin Lay-Z-Boy. In a sudden fit of localissimo, Creights had asked if Krystals did a meat and three, God love him. 

[...]

"Send away to the lab then?"

"Oh gosh, no need for that. Here." He took out his phone and pulled something up, handing it to me. It was tree-ring correlation charts for the area going back to prehistoric times. 

A few hours later, and GG's old wedding chair gave us all the dendrochronology we needed to narrow in the date she must've knitted--or, crochetted I guess--that tattered old throw. 

[...]

That night, I had a dream... [...]

"Go get Mom," Ma'd said.
"What?"
"Go get your great gram!"

GG was sitting on her wedding chair, like she usually did in the afternoon. 

[...]

We went a-huntin through the thrifts for new examples. It would be we'd choose to do our shoppin on sales tax holiday. The stores all reeked of grannies and kiddos and we had to shove in 'mong the ladies in bootie pants whilst all the menfolk rocked away the day on the verandas, drinkin beer outa sacked bottles. But before long, I was 'readin' everything from ... to die-cut paper placemats, the patterns twitterin like water from lack of sleep. Twas mostly all gobbelty-gook, of course. Exceptin one half-a-pair of ladies' gloves that had quite a half-story to tell.

[...]

"You could talk like a normal person, you know. It would be less offensive than that fake-ass hee-haw accent of yours." 

[...]

The giant throwe lathe grandad--that's Paw's dad--kept in the shed had come out of some industrial park he cleared out back in his removal service days...

[...]


DEDICATION

Dedicated to my great grandma Vesey and my grandma. And to my mom, who's anything but 'on the outs', and whose macrame skills, as best I can tell, are second-to-none.

UNTITLED
Shooting script.



INT. ELDERLY MAN'S HOUSE - BACK VIEW - DAY

An Elderly MAN stands silhouetted before the back window, looking out. The wind blows through the long grass in the yard, coinciding with a breeze in the house that rustles the man's wispy hair.

CUT TO:

EXT. ELDERLY MAN'S HOUSE - FRONT WINDOW - BACK VIEW - DAY

Standing on the porch before the front window, we see the classic figure of the REAPER, wearing a dark robe and holding a scythe. The MAN, still looking out the back window, is visible as a silhouette.

CUT TO:

INT. ELDERLY MAN'S HOUSE - CENTERED - DAY

The front door is closed. Tension-building music/sound effect (e.g., wind blowing) followed by POUNDING on the door. The door visibly vibrates with the sound.

CUT TO:

INT. ELDERLY MAN'S HOUSE - BACK VIEW - CLOSED DOOR - DAY

Same view of the closed door, but now the MAN stands before it.

CUT TO:

INT. ELDERLY MAN'S HOUSE - DOOR OPEN - DAY

The door is open. We see the back view of the MAN facing the backlit silhouette of the REAPER.

CUT TO:

INT. ELDERLY MAN'S HOUSE - OVER THE SHOULDER VIEW - DAY

The camera takes an over-the-shoulder view as the MAN reaches out toward the REAPER, clutching a FIVE DOLLAR BILL as if to buy more time.

The REAPER pounds twice on the porch with the butt of his scythe.

The MAN slowly produces another bill, this time a TEN DOLLAR BILL.

The sound of a scythe accompanies CUT TO:

EXT. ELDERLY MAN'S BACKYARD - DAY

 The REAPER lazily mows the long grass with his scythe, then takes a break to wipe his brow, take a swig from a water bottle, and adjust his headphones.

The sound of a cowbell is heard.

CUT TO BLACK.

Outro music: "Don't Fear the Reaper."
